Hölder continuous mappings, differential forms and the Heisenberg groups Thanks: P.H. was supported by NSF grants DMS-2055171 and DMS-2452426. Thanks: A.S. was supported by NSF Career DMS-2044898. A.S. is a Humboldt fellow
Piotr Hajłasz, Jacob Mirra, Armin Schikorra

Abstract
. We develop analysis of Hölder continuous mappings with applications to geometry and topology of the Heisenberg groups. We cover the theory of distributional Jacobians of Hölder continuous mappings and pullbacks of differential forms under Hölder continuous mappings. That includes versions of the change of variables formula and the Stokes theorem for Hölder continuous mappings. The main applications are in the setting of the Heisenberg groups, where we provide a simple proof of a generalization of the Gromov non-embedding theorem, and new results about the Hölder homotopy groups of the Heisenberg groups.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 26B35, 53C17, 53C23, 58A10, Secondary: 30L99, 55Q25, 55Q70, 58A14

1.
Introduction

The goal of this paper is to develop differential analysis of Hölder continuous functions and mappings with applications to geometry and topology of the Heisenberg groups.
While the aim of Introduction is to clarify the paper’s objective, it does not provide precise statements or include all proven results. Instead, it provides a general overview of the paper’s content.
Since the paper is long, we thought it would be important to explain the main ideas without using complicated and technical terms.

The paper begins with
Section

2
. Since this section, gathers preliminary material that will be used in later sections, we describe its content at the end of Introduction. The main theme of the paper starts in
Section

3
.

Hölder continuous functions need not be differentiable at any point. It is also possible to construct, for any
0<\gamma<1
, a strictly increasing,
\gamma
-Hölder continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f^{\prime}=0
almost everywhere,
[
62
]
. In that case,
f^{\prime}
does not satisfy the fundamental theorem of calculus, and hence
f^{\prime}
is not the distributional derivative. That also means that even if a Hölder continuous function is differentiable a.e., properties of the derivative do not say much about properties of the function.

On the other hand Young,
[
73
]
, proved that if
f\in C^{0,\alpha}([0,1])
and
g\in C^{0,\beta}([0,1])
,
\alpha+\beta>1
, then the Stielties integral exists and satisfies:

(1.1)

\Bigg|\int_{0}^{1}f(x)\,dg(x)\Bigg|\leq C\|f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}\|g\|_{C^{0,\beta}}.

Here is another approach to this inequality. One can prove that

\Bigg|\int_{0}^{1}f(x)\,g^{\prime}(x)\,dx\Bigg|\leq C\|f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}\|g\|_{C^{0,\beta}}\quad\text{if }f\in C^{0,\alpha}([0,1]),\ g\in C^{\infty}([0,1]),

see
Corollary

3.16
. Now, if
f\in C^{0,\alpha}

g\in C^{0,\beta}
, then we can define

(1.2)

\int_{0}^{1}f(x)g^{\prime}(x)\,dx:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{1}f(x)g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)\,dx,

where
g_{\varepsilon}
is a smooth approximation of
g
by convolution.
We need to understand that the expression on the left hand side of (
1.2
) is a formal notation since
g^{\prime}
is not a function. However, we can interpret
g^{\prime}
as a distribution acting on test functions
f\in C^{0,\alpha}
and the way
g^{\prime}
acts on
f
is defined through the above limit. One can also prove that the distributional integral (
1.2
) coincides with the Stielties integral (
1.1
).

The result of Young found numerous applications in stochastic analysis
[
28
]
, but here we are interested in a different direction in which it evolved.

More recently,
several groups of researchers developed, independently,
a multidimensional version of the Young integral

(1.3)

\int_{\mathcal{M}}fdg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n},\quad f\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathcal{M}),\ g_{i}\in C^{0,\beta}(\mathcal{M}),\ \alpha+n\beta>n,

where
\mathcal{M}
is an
n
-dimensional oriented Riemannian manifold (cf.
Corollary

3.11
).
Using hard harmonic analysis, Sickel and Youssfi
[
66
]
extended the integral (
1.3
) to potential spaces that are much more general than Hölder spaces. This result went largely unnoticed in parts of the Geometric Analysis community until Brezis and Nguyen
[
5
,
6
]
later rediscovered it in the context of Hölder functions. Additionally, Conti, De Lellis, and Székelyhidi
[
16
]
studied a similar integral in a special case related to the rigidity of isometric immersions and convex integration, while Züst
[
74
,
75
]
developed the theory in the setting of currents in metric spaces, with applications to the geometry of the Heisenerg groups in mind.

We should also mention that the Onsager conjecture
[
57
]
led to another, but related direction of analysis of Hölder continuous functions. Onsager conjectured that weak solutions to the incompressible Euler equations satisfy
the law of conservation of energy if their spatial regularity is above
\frac{1}{3}
-Hölder and
may violate it if the regularity is below
\frac{1}{3}
-Hölder. While the affirmative (easier) part of the conjecture was proved by Constantin, E, and Titi
[
15
]
and Eyink
[
25
]
, the difficult part, counterexample when
\gamma<\frac{1}{3}
, was constructed by Isett
[
44
]
using convex integration. Although we will not mention this direction of research in the paper, we want to emphasize that we used ideas from
[
15
]
in the proof of
Theorem

7.4
.

Let us briefly mention the approaches to define the generalized integrals (
1.3
) given by Züst
[
75
,
74
]
and Brezis and Nguyen
[
5
,
6
]
.

Züst used Young’s result (
1.1
) and a clever induction argument (induction over the dimension) to define (
1.3
).
Let
I^{n}=[0,1]^{n}
. Young’s theorem (
1.1
) and a formal integration by parts allows us to define

\int_{I^{2}}dg_{1}\wedge dg_{2}:=\int_{\partial I^{2}}g_{1}\,dg_{2}\qquad\text{for }g_{1},g_{2}\in C^{0,\beta}(I^{2}),\ \textstyle{\beta>\frac{1}{2}}\,.

Then, we define
\int_{I^{2}}f\,dg_{1}\wedge dg_{2}
using a Riemann sum approximation: Divide
I^{2}
into
k^{2}
identical squares
\{I_{ki}\}_{i=1}^{k^{2}}
with centers
p_{ki}
, and we define

(1.4)

\int_{I^{2}}f\,dg_{1}\wedge dg_{2}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{k^{2}}f(p_{ik})\int_{I_{ki}^{2}}dg_{1}\wedge dg_{2}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{k^{2}}f(p_{ik})\int_{\partial I_{ki}^{2}}g_{1}\,dg_{2}.

It turns out that the Riemann sum (
1.4
) converges provided

f\in C^{0,\alpha}(I^{2}),\quad g_{1},g_{2}\in C^{0,\beta}(I^{2}),\quad\alpha+2\beta>2.

Integral (
1.4
) allows us to define

\int_{I^{3}}dg_{1}\wedge dg_{2}\wedge dg_{3}:=\int_{\partial I_{3}}g_{1}\,dg_{2}\wedge dg_{3},\text{ provided }g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\in C^{0,\beta}(I_{3}),\ \textstyle{\beta>\frac{2}{3}}\,.

It is clear now how to use induction to define (
1.3
) when
\mathcal{M}=I^{n}
, and the case of a general manifold is obtained through partition of unity and local coordinate systems.

The approach of Brezis and Nguyen is based on a very different idea. The key observation is that there is abounded extension operator

\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}:C^{0,\alpha}(\mathcal{M})\to C^{0,\alpha}\cap W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]),\text{ provided }p\in(1,\infty),\ \textstyle{1-\frac{1}{p}<\alpha\leq 1}.

Moreover, we can guarantee that
\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}u=0
near
\mathcal{M}\times\{1\}
.
While this result follows from a general theory of traces in the Sobolev
W^{1,p}
spaces, see
Proposition

2.53
for a straightforward argument.

Assume now that
f
and
g_{i}
are as in (
1.3
) and let
f^{\varepsilon}
,
g_{i}^{\varepsilon}
be the approximations by convolution. It is easy to see that we can find
p\in(1,\infty)
and
q\in(1,\infty)
, such that

\textstyle{\alpha>1-\frac{1}{p},\quad\beta>1-\frac{1}{q},\quad\frac{1}{p}+\frac{n}{q}=1}.

Let
F^{\varepsilon}=\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}(f^{\varepsilon})
and
G_{i}^{\varepsilon}=\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}(g_{i}^{\varepsilon})
. Then integration by parts yields

\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{\varepsilon}\,dg_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\int_{\partial(\mathcal{M}\times[0,1])}F^{\varepsilon}\,dG_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge dG_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}dF^{\varepsilon}\wedge dG_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge dG_{n}^{\varepsilon}.

Since
dF^{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}
,
dG_{i}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{q}
, and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{n}{q}=1
, it follows from the Hölder inequality
that the integral on the right hand side converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and we can define

\int_{\mathcal{M}}fdg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{\varepsilon}dg_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}dF\wedge dG_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dG_{n}.

For details, see Theorems
3.1
and
3.8
. Note that this can be regarded as a higher dimensional generalization of (
1.2
).

The technique of Brezis and Nguyen is very powerful and it has recently been used and extended for example in
[
30
,
49
,
63
]
. Similar “compensation by extension” ideas were also present in earlier works, e.g.
[
13
,
14
]
.

Section

3
of our paper is devoted to a detailed study of integrals related to (
1.3
), both in the category of Hölder continuous functions and in a wider category of fractional Sobolev spaces.
Our approach is based on that of Brezis and Nguyen.

Building on the idea of distributional Jacobians (
1.3
), in
Section

4
, we study pullbacks of differential forms by Hölder continuous mappings. Since the pullback of a differential form has a Jacobian-like structure, one can believe that
the generalized integral

(1.5)

\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}(f^{\varepsilon})^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau

is well defined if
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
\kappa
in a
k
-form on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
C^{0,\alpha}
coefficients and
\tau
is an
(n-k)
-form on
\mathcal{M}
with
C^{0,\alpha\gamma}
coefficients, under suitable assumptions about
\alpha
and
\gamma
, see e.g.,
Theorem

4.1
.

Pullbacks of differential forms under fractional Sobolev maps have also been studied in a recent paper by Detaille, Mironescu and Xiao
[
21
]
.

In
Section

5
we apply the theory of Jacobians and pullbacks from Sections
3
and
4
to study change of variables and integration by parts.

If
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded domain and
f:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is continuous, then for each
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\partial\Omega)
one can define the winding number
w(f,y)
, see
Definition

5.6
. The winding number is defined in terms of degree and roughly speaking, it says how many times (counting orientation) the image of
\partial\Omega
under the mapping
f
wraps around
y
.

The winding number appears in several formulas related to the change of variables. For example if

\Omega
is a bounded domain with smooth boundary,

f\in C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then (see
Lemma

5.7
and
Corollary

5.10
)

(1.6)

\int_{\Omega}(u\circ f)\,df_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge df_{n}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u(y)w(f,y)\,dy

and

(1.7)

(-1)^{i-1}\int_{\partial\Omega}(u\circ f)\,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{df_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\partial u}{\partial y_{i}}(y)w(f,y)\,dy.

Note that (
1.6
) is the classical change of variables and (
1.7
) is a version of Green’s theorem.

Using the theory of distributional Jacobians mentioned above we extend these and other similar formulas to the case when
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})

\gamma\in\big(\frac{n-1}{n},1\big]
, see
Section

5
for precise statements. Note that under the given assumption the measure of
f(\partial\Omega)
is zero and hence
w(f,y)
is defined for almost all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Related results have been obtained in
[
16
,
47
,
51
,
52
,
77
]
.

For example, as a special case we obtain the following result (Corollaries
5.27
and
5.28
):

If
\gamma=(\gamma^{x},\gamma^{y})\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{S}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\alpha\in\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big]
and if

f=(f^{x},f^{y})\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
is an extension of
\gamma
, then

(1.8)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\gamma}xdy-ydx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\gamma^{x}d\gamma^{y}-\gamma^{y}d\gamma^{x}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{2}}df^{x}\wedge df^{y}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(f,y)\,dy.

Note that the integral on the right hand side equals the oriented area enclosed by the curve
\gamma
.

The remaining part of the paper is devoted to analysis of Hölder continuous mappings from Euclidean domains and Riemannian manifolds into the Heisenberg groups.

It is fair to say that the study of
local
geometry of the Heisenberg groups has been initiated and popularized by Gromov in his seminal paper
[
32
]
.
The Heisenberg group provides a
local
model for contact manifolds since according to the Darboux theorem, every contact manifold is locally equivalent to the Heisenberg group. While the questions in contact topology are concerned with the
global
structure of contact manifolds, questions in the geometry of the Heisenberg group are concerned with the
local
structure of contact manifolds.
The local geometry of the Heisenberg group is very complicated. In some directions it is Euclidean while in other directions is is purely fractal. For this reason Gromov
[
32
, 0.5.C,0.5.D,3.1]
, initiated an investigation of Hölder continuous maps into the Heisenberg groups and more general sub-Riemannian manifolds: Hölder maps can catch the fractal nature of the space. The remaining sections of the paper are devoted to this program.

Section

6
is a brief overview of the theory of the Heisenberg groups. All results in this section are known and its purpose it to fix notation and list results that are needed later. Let us just mention here that the Heisenberg group is
\mathbb{H}^{n}=\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
equipped with a certain Lie groups structure.
We denote the coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
by
(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},t)
and then

X_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}+2y_{j}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\quad Y_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}-2x_{j}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\quad T=\frac{\partial}{\partial t},

is a basis off the left invariant vector fields. However, the Heisenberg group is equipped with the
2n
-dimensional
horizontal
distribution
H\mathbb{H}^{n}
,

H_{p}\mathbb{H}^{n}=\text{span}\left\{X_{1}(p),Y_{1}(p),\dots,X_{n}(p),Y_{n}(p)\right\}\quad\text{for all }p\in\mathbb{H}^{n}.

The horizontal distribution is nothing else, but a standard
contact
structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, because

(1.9)

H_{p}\mathbb{H}^{n}=\operatorname{ker}\upalpha(p)\subset T_{p}\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\quad\text{where}\quad\upalpha=dt+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(x_{j}\,dy_{j}-y_{j}\,dx_{j})

is a standard contact form.

A Lipschitz curve
\gamma=(\gamma^{x_{1}},\gamma^{y_{1}},\ldots,\gamma^{x_{n}},\gamma^{y_{n}},\gamma^{t}):[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is
horizontal
if it is tangent to the horizontal distribution. It is not difficult to check that
\gamma
is horizontal if and only if for every
s\in[0,1]
,

(1.10)

\gamma^{t}(s)-\gamma^{t}(0)=-4\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\gamma_{j}|_{[0,s]}}x_{j}\,dy_{j}-y_{j}\,dx_{j}\Bigg),\quad\text{where }\gamma_{j}=(\gamma^{x_{j}},\gamma^{y_{j}}).

That is, the components
\gamma_{j}
can be arbitrary Lipschitz curves, but then the component
\gamma^{t}
is uniquely determined from (
1.10
). In particular, if
\gamma
is a closed curve, then
\gamma^{t}(1)-\gamma^{t}(0)=0
and it follows from the standard Lipschitz case of (
1.8
) that the sum of oriented areas bounded by the curves
\gamma_{j}
equals zero.

The Heisenberg group is equipped with the Carnot-Carathéodory metric
d_{cc}(p,q)
which is the infimum o lengths of horizontal curves connecting
p,q\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
. The metric
d_{cc}
is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to the Korányi metric
d_{K}
defined in (
6.5
), and in what follows the Heisenberg group is regarded as a metric space
(\mathbb{H}^{n},d_{K})
.
We prefer to work with the Korányi metric, because unlike the Carnot-Carathéodory metric,
d_{K}(p,q)
can be computed explicitly.

The metric
d_{K}
is not smooth and it has a fractal nature. In fact, for any compact set

K\subset\mathbb{H}^{n}
there is a constant
C\geq 1
such that
C^{-1}d_{K}(p,q)\leq|p-q|\leq Cd_{K}(p,q)^{1/2}

for all
p,q\in K
. Hence the topology induced by the
d_{K}

metric is the same as the Euclidean topology, however, the Hausdorff dimension of the
Heisenberg group equals
2n+2
, while the topological dimension is
2n+1
, so methods from smooth differential geometry cannot be directly applied to the Heisenberg group.

In
Section

7
we study Hölder continuous mappings from a Euclidean domain or from a Riemannian manifold into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. A Lipschitz mapping
f
into
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is Lipschitz as a mapping into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
if and only if
f^{*}\upalpha=0
, where
\upalpha
is the contact form from (
1.9
). Moreover, Lipschitz mappings into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
always satisfy
\operatorname{rank}Df\leq n
, see
Proposition

6.14
. The main objective of
Section

7
is to generalize these results to the case of Hölder continuous mappings into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

Le Donne and Züst
[
47
]
appear to be the first to use the concept of Jacobians of Hölder continuous mappings (
1.3
) in the setting of the Heisenberg groups.

In this section, we develop this idea further, including the analysis of pullbacks of differential forms.
Another important idea that is heavily used in the section and also in Sections
8
,
9
and
10
is described next.

If
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and
\kappa\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
, then locally we have

(1.11)

\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa\|_{\infty}\leq C\varepsilon^{k(\gamma-1)},

see
Lemma

7.13
. Here
f_{\varepsilon}
is the approximation of
f
by convolution. Estimate (
1.11
) easily follows from the definitions of pullback and convolution. Note that estimate (
1.11
) diverges to
\infty
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, if
0<\gamma<1
.

In particular, (
1.11
) is true if
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
, because
\operatorname{id}:\mathbb{H}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is locally Lipschitz continuous. However, in the case of the contact form
\upalpha
we have a much better estimate (
Theorem

7.2
):

If
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then locally we have

(1.12)

\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\|_{\infty}\leq C\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}.

This time, the estimate converges to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, if
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
.

Corollary

2.15
, which follows from the Lefschetz
Lemma

2.9
, implies that any form
\kappa\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
,
n+1\leq k\leq 2n+1
, can be decomposed as

(1.13)

\kappa=\beta\wedge\upalpha+d(\gamma\wedge\upalpha),

and hence

f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\kappa=f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\beta\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha+d(f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\gamma\wedge f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\upalpha).

This allows us to get a much better estimate than that from (
1.11
), because while we estimate
f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\beta
and
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\gamma
using (
1.11
), we can estimate
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha
using (
1.12
).

Note that if
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
, then
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
.
Using the above ideas we prove the following characterization of Hölder continuous mappings into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
(see Theorems
7.1
and
7.4
).

If
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
,
\gamma\in\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big]
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
;

(b)

f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\rightrightarrows 0
uniformly on compact sets;

(c)

f^{*}\upalpha=0
in the distributional sense i.e.,
\int_{\Omega}f^{*}\upalpha\wedge\phi=0

for all
\phi\in\Omega_{c}^{m-1}(\Omega)
.

The integral in (c) is understood in the sense of (
1.5
).
Quite surprisingly, in condition (b) we do not require any estimates for the rate of convergence
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\rightrightarrows 0
, but afterwards, we have (
1.12
).

The condition about the rank of the derivative
\operatorname{rank}Df\leq n
, has the following generalization, see
Theorem

7.18
.

Let
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\gamma\in\big(\frac{n+1}{n+2},1\big]
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
m>n
. Then

(1.14)

\int_{\Omega}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\phi=0\text{ for all }\kappa\in\Omega^{n+1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})\text{ and all }\phi\in\Omega_{c}^{m-n-1}(\Omega).

Note that in the case of smooth mappings, (
1.14
) is equivalent to
\operatorname{rank}Df\leq n
.

Finally, note that (
1.10
) also holds for Hölder continuous curves in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, see
Theorem

7.6
(cf.
[
47
, Lemma 3.1]
). This is used to characterize existence of liftings of
Hölder continuous mappings
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
to Hölder continuous mappings
F:\Omega\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
, see
Theorem

7.12
.

Using methods of
Section

7
, in
Section

8
we prove a generalization of the Gromov non-embedding theorem
[
32
, 3.1.A]
due to
[
37
]
, see Theorems
8.1
and
8.2
. In a special case, the theorem says that there is no mapping
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n+1};\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\gamma\in\big(\frac{n+1}{n+2},1\big]
, such that
f|_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}=f|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}
is a topological embedding (cf.
Corollary

8.4
). A rough idea of the proof (of this special case) is that for such a mapping
f(\mathbb{S}^{n})
would bound a “hole”. The precise meaning of the “hole” can be expressed in terms of the Alexander-Poincaré duality (cf.
Theorem

8.10
and
Remark

8.11
) according to which the de Rham cohomology with compact support of the complement satisfies

(1.15)

H_{c}^{n+1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{n}))=\mathbb{R}.

Now, if
\eta\in\Omega_{c}^{n+1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{n}))
,
d\eta=0
, is a generator of (
1.15
), then the integration by parts yields

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}f^{*}\eta\neq 0

and this contradicts
Theorem

7.18
with
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{B}^{n+1}
,
\kappa=\eta
and
\tau\equiv 1
(cf. (
1.14
)). The actual proof of
Theorem

8.2
is more complicated than that of the special case described here,
because the result is more general; we cannot refer directly to
Theorem

7.18
, but we mimic its proof.

The content of
Section

8
is somewhat related to the work of Balogh, Kozhevnikov, and Pansu
[
3
]
. Adapting a technique for integrating differential forms along Hölder maps, developed by Züst
[
74
,
75
]
and Le Donne and Züst
[
47
]
, they proved that any
C^{0,\gamma}
homeomorphism between
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
and
\mathbb{H}^{n}
must satisfy
\gamma\leq\frac{n+1}{n+2}
. Their results also apply to more general Carnot groups.

Section

9
provides background material about the Hodge decomposition needed for the last
Section

10
. This section is devoted to study of the Hölder homotopy groups of the Heisenberg group. While several results about the Lipschitz homotopy groups have already been proved, see
Theorem

10.3
, very little is known about the Hölder homotopy groups.
First note that it is very easy to prove that
\pi_{k}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{H}^{n})=0
for all
k,n\geq 1
and
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)
(see
Proposition

10.2
), but the case
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
is more complicated.

In
Theorem

10.5
we prove that

\pi_{n}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\neq 0\quad\text{ for all }{\textstyle\gamma\in\big(\frac{n+1}{n+2},1\big]}.

In fact it follows immediately from the special case of the non-embedding theorem mentioned above i.e.,
Corollary

8.4
, and
Theorem

10.5
should be credited to Gromov. If
n=1
, we obtain
\pi_{1}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{H}^{1})\neq 0
for all
\gamma\in\big(\frac{2}{3},1\big]
. This result is sharp, because Wenger and Young
[
72
, Theorem 1.1]
proved that
\pi_{1}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{H}^{1})=0
for all
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{2}{3}\big)
. The case
\gamma=\frac{2}{3}
is, however, open.

The main result of
Section

10
is
Theorem

10.7
:

\pi_{4n-1}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{H}^{2n})\neq 0\quad\text{ for all }{\textstyle\gamma\in\big(\frac{4n+1}{4n+2},1\big].}

The proof is difficult. The Lipschitz case
\pi_{4n-1}^{\rm Lip}(\mathbb{H}^{2n})\neq 0
was proved in
[
38
]
using a certain generalization of the Hopf invariant that required the use of the Hodge decomposition. The proof presented here follows a similar approach, but now all estimates have to be adapted to the Hölder category. This makes the arguments much more difficult.

Finally, let us describe the content of the preliminary
Section

2
.
In Sections
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.4
, we review exterior algebra and differential forms, including some properties of contact and symplectic forms. While the material is known, we couldn’t locate
Proposition

2.14
and
Corollary

2.15
in the literature.

Section

2.5
is devoted to a general theory of Hölder continuous functions. Again, the material is well known, but it is not easy to find all results in one place. Because of an increasing interest in analysis of Hölder continuous functions and mappings, we decided to make this section detailed and self-contained with hope that it will be a useful reference to the readers.

Section

2.6
reviews the Sobolev spaces
W^{1,p}
and the fractional Sobolev spaces
W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}
that are the boundary trace spaces for
W^{1,p}
spaces. In
Lemma

2.45
we state that there are bounded trace and extension operators

\operatorname{Tr}:W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M})\to W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\partial\mathcal{M})\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{ext}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}:W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\partial\mathcal{M})\to W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M}).

Unfortunately, no short proofs of this result are known. While there are elementary proofs, all of them are pretty technical. For that reason, we decided to include a proof of a special case of the boundedness of the extension operator in
Section

2.7
:

\operatorname{ext}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}:C^{0,\gamma}(\partial\mathcal{M})\to W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M})\quad\text{provided }\textstyle{1-\frac{1}{p}<\gamma\leq 1},

see
Proposition

2.53
,
Corollary

2.55
, and
Corollary

2.56
. Since the paper is devoted to analysis of Hölder continuous mappings, this special case is the most important one.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we collect definitions and results related to differential forms, Hölder continuous mappings, Sobolev spaces and fractional Sobolev spaces needed in the rest of the paper. While most of the material presented here is known, some of the results proved here could be difficult to find in the literature.
We assume that the reader is familiar with basic theory of differential forms, Riemannian manifolds, and Sobolev spaces. We recall some of the elementary definitions with the purpose of clarifying notation used in the paper.

2.1.
Notation

By
C
we will denote a generic constant whose value may change in a single string of estimates.
If
A
and
B
are non-negative quantities, we write
A\lesssim B
and
A\approx B
if there is a constant
C\geq 1
such that
A\leq CB
and
C^{-1}B\leq A\leq CB
respectively. This will always be clear from the context on what parameters
C
depends. We will also write
A\lesssim_{U,V}B
and
A\approx_{U,V}B
if the implied constants depend on parameters
U
and
V
.
If
0\neq a\in\mathbb{R}
, then the sign of
a
is defined as
\operatorname{sgn}a=1
if
a>0
and
\operatorname{sgn}a=-1
if
a<0
.

\mathbb{N}
will denote the set of positive integers, and

\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\,x_{n}\geq 0\}
will denote a half-space.

A\Subset B
means that the closure
\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
of
A
is a compact subset of
B
.
We will also use notation
X\Subset Y
to denote compact embedding of Banach spaces.
An open ball of radius
R
will be denoted by
B(x,R)
or
B^{n}(x,R)
and we will also write
B_{R}^{n}=B_{R}=B(0,R)
. The unit ball and the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
will be denoted by
\mathbb{B}^{n}=B^{n}(0,1)
and
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
.
The volume of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
will be denoted by
\omega_{n}
.
Uniform convergence will often be denoted by
f_{n}\rightrightarrows f
.

If
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a domain, then
f\in C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
means that
f
can be extended to a function in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

We say that a bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is Lipschitz if its boundary is locally a graph of a Lipschitz function. A domain is smooth if its boundary is locally a graph of a smooth function. Smooth domains are examples of Riemannian manifolds with boundary.

By a smooth function or a smooth manifold we will mean a
C^{\infty}
smooth function or a manifold.
A closed manifold is a smooth compact manifold without boundary. If
\mathcal{M}
is a Riemannian manifold, then
C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
denotes the class of functions that are smooth up to the boundary. Clearly, if
\partial\mathcal{M}=\varnothing
, then
C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
.

By
C^{0,\gamma}
,
0<\gamma\leq 1
we will denote the class of
\gamma
-Hölder continuous functions.
We will also use notation
\operatorname{Lip}:=C^{0,1}
for Lipschitz functions.
Various classes of compactly supported functions will be denoted by
C_{c}^{\infty},C_{c}^{0,\gamma}
, etc.

De Rham cohomology and de Rham cohomology with compact support on an oriented manifold
\mathcal{M}
will be dented by
H^{k}(\mathcal{M})
and
H^{k}_{c}(\mathcal{M})
respectively. No other cohomoloy theory will be considered.

If
X
is a space of real valued function, then
X^{n}
stands the space of mappings
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
such that
f\in X
. For example,
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m}
is the class of smooth mappings from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. If
X
is a normed space, then
X^{n}
is also a normed space with the norm
\|f\|=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|f_{i}\|
.

The
L^{p}
norm
1\leq p\leq\infty
of a function
f
will be denoted by
\|f\|_{p}
. If a function
f
defined in
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is sufficiently smooth,
D^{0}f=f
and
D^{m}f
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, denotes the vector of all partial derivatives of order
m
, so

|D^{m}f|:=\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|=m}|D^{\alpha}f|^{2}\Big)^{1/2},\quad\text{and}\quad\|f\|_{m,p}:=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\|D^{k}f\|_{p},\quad 1\leq p\leq\infty,\ m\in\mathbb{N},

is the Sobolev norm.
The integral average will be denoted by the barred integral:

(2.1)

\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0ptE}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{E}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{E}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{E}}f\,d\mu=\frac{1}{\mu(E)}\int_{E}f\,d\mu.

The Jacobian of a mapping
f:\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is denoted by

J_{f}(x):=\det Df(x)
.

Symbols
\upomega
and
\upalpha
will be solely used to denote the standard symplectic and the standard contact forms defined by

\upomega=\sum_{i=1}^{n}dx_{i}\wedge dy_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\upalpha=dt+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}dy_{i}-y_{i}dx_{i}).

By writing
w_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{w_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge w_{k}
we mean that the form
w_{i}
is omitted in the wedge product.

2.2.
Exterior algebra and differential forms

The canonical bases of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}
will be denoted by

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad dx_{1},\ldots,dx_{n}

respectively.
The
k
-covectors

dx_{I}:=dx_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{i_{k}},\quad I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}),\ 1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq n

form a basis of
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}
. We will denote the length of
I
by
|I|=k
.

The
k
-covectors are identified with alternating multilinear maps on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\ldots\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
that on basis elements are defined by

dx_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{i_{k}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})=\det\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}dx_{i_{1}}(v_{1})&\ldots&dx_{i_{1}}(v_{k})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
dx_{i_{k}}(v_{1})&\ldots&dx_{i_{k}}(v_{k})\end{array}\right]=\det\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}v_{1}^{{i_{1}}}&\ldots&v_{k}^{{i_{1}}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
v_{1}^{i_{k}}&\ldots&v_{k}^{{i_{k}}}\end{array}\right],

where

v_{j}=v^{1}_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+\ldots+v^{n}_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}.

The wedge product

\wedge:\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}\times\bigwedge\nolimits^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}\mapsto\bigwedge\nolimits^{k+\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}

is a bilinear map defined on bases as

dx_{I}\wedge dx_{J}=dx_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{i_{k}}\wedge dx_{j_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{j_{\ell}},

where

I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}),\ J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell}),\ 1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq n,\ 1\leq j_{1}<\ldots<j_{\ell}\leq n.

Here, we identify
dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}=-dx_{j}\wedge dx_{i}
; in particular
dx_{i}\wedge dx_{i}=0
.

It is worth noting that for
\alpha\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\beta\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{\ell}\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have

\alpha\wedge\beta=(-1)^{k\ell}\beta\wedge\alpha.

\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}
is endowed with a scalar product defined
on the basis by

\langle dx_{I},dx_{J}\rangle=\begin{cases}1\quad&I=J\\
0\quad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The Hodge star operator
\ast:\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}\to\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}
is a unique linear isomorphism that satisfies

\alpha\wedge(\ast\beta)=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\,dx^{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{n}\qquad\text{for all }\alpha,\beta\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}.

The Hodge star operator can be also computed more explicitly: If
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
,
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq n
, then

\ast dx_{I}=\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma(I))dx_{J},

where
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-k})
,
1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<\ldots<j_{n-k}\leq n
,
I\cap J=\varnothing
,
is the set of complementary indices and
\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma(I))=\pm 1
is the sign of the permutation

\sigma(I):(1,2,\ldots,n)\to(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k},j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-k})
.
Moreover we have,

\ast\ast\eta=(-1)^{k(n-k)}\eta\quad\quad\eta\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}.

Smooth differential
k
-forms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
are smooth sections of
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}
:

\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=C^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}\right).

If
X
is a space of functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then we also consider the space of forms

\Omega^{k}X(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with coefficients in
X
(so by definition
\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\Omega^{k}C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
). If
X
is a Banach space, the space
\Omega^{k}X(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is equipped with a Banach norm

\|\kappa\|_{X}=\sum_{|I|=k}\|\kappa_{I}\|_{X},\quad\text{for $\kappa=\sum_{|I|=k}\kappa_{I}\,dx_{I}$.}

That includes, for example, forms with Hölder,
L^{p}
, Sobolev or fractional Sobolev coefficients

\Omega^{k}C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\Omega^{k}L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\Omega^{k}W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\Omega^{k}W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Hölder, Sobolev or fractional Sobolev spaces will be discussed in Sections
2.5
and
2.6
.

The exterior derivative
d:\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\Omega^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a linear operator uniquely determined by

d\left(fdx_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{i_{k}}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{j}}fdx^{j}\wedge dx_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{i_{k}}.

Clearly, this construction also gives exterior derivatives of forms with other regularity e.g.,

d:\Omega^{k}C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\Omega^{k+1}C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\quad\text{or}\quad d:\Omega^{k}W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\Omega^{k+1}L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

It is worth noting that for a
k
-form
\alpha
and an
\ell
-form
\beta
we have

(2.2)

d(\alpha\wedge\beta)=d\alpha\wedge\beta+(-1)^{k}\alpha\wedge d\beta.

The co-differential
\delta:\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\Omega^{k-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as

\delta=(-1)^{n(k-1)+1}\ast d\ast,

with the obvious generalization to
C^{1}
and Sobolev forms. The Laplace-de Rham operator
\Delta:\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as

\Delta=d\delta+\delta d.

One can check that for functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
(i.e. for
k=0
-form) it coincides with the usual Laplace operator.

2.3.
Good coordinate systems

While in
Section

2.2
forms were defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one can use local coordinate systems to define forms (including Sobolv, Hölder etc.) on a smooth oriented manifold
\mathcal{M}
.
A differential
k
-form
\gamma
on a smooth oriented
n
-dimensional manifold
\mathcal{M}
, with or without boundary, can be expressed in local coordinates

x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}):U\subset\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
as

\gamma=\sum_{|I|=k}\gamma_{I}\,dx_{I}\quad\text{in $U$.}

We will only consider
good coordinate systems

(x,U)
that satisfy:

(1)

If
U\cap\partial\mathcal{M}=\varnothing
, then
x(U)=\mathbb{B}^{n}
and
x
extends to a smooth coordinate system in a larger domain
V\Supset U
.

(2)

If
U
is a neighborhood of a boundary point, then
x(U)=\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}:=\mathbb{B}^{n}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
is the unit half-ball and
x
extends to a smooth coordinate system in a larger domain
V\Supset U
.

(3)

If
\mathcal{M}
is oriented, then
(x,U)
is orientation preserving.

Note that our assumptions will guarantee boundedness of derivatives of all orders.

If a function space
X
‘behaves well’ under a change of variables, and
\mathcal{M}
is a smooth oriented closed
n
-dimensional manifold,
we say that
\gamma\in\Omega^{k}X(\mathcal{M})
if for every good coordinate system
(x,U)
,
we have
(x^{-1})^{*}\gamma\in\Omega^{k}X(\mathbb{B}^{n})
.
Then we define the norm of

\gamma\in\Omega^{k}X(\mathcal{M})
by

(2.3)

\|\gamma\|_{X}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|(x^{-1}_{i})^{*}\gamma\|_{X(\mathbb{B}^{n})},

where the sum runs over a finite covering
\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}U_{i}
by
good coordinate systems
\{(x_{i},U_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
.
Due to compactness of
\mathcal{M}
, the norms defined for different coverings are equivalent.
This approach applies to spaces like
C^{0,\gamma}
,
L^{p}
,
W^{1,p}
or
W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}
.

If
\gamma\in\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M})
(or
\gamma\in\Omega^{k}W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M})
etc.), then we can define the differential
d
, via the local representation in a good coordinate systems
U

d\gamma=\sum_{|I|=k}d\left(\gamma_{I}\,dx_{I}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{|I|=k}\partial_{x_{i}}\gamma_{I}\,dx_{i}\wedge dx_{I}\quad\text{in $U$.}

The Hodge-star operator
\ast
was defined with respect to the Euclidean structure (scalar product) in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
\mathcal{M}
is an oriented
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold, then we have the orientation and the scalar product in every tangent space and hence in every cotangent space
T_{x}^{*}\mathcal{M}
, which allows us to define the Hodge-star operator
\ast:\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}T_{x}^{*}\mathcal{M}\to\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-k}T_{x}^{*}\mathcal{M}
. Then we define
\delta:\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M})\to\Omega^{k-1}(\mathcal{M})
and
\Delta:\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M})\to\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M})
as in the Euclidean case

\delta=(-1)^{n(k-1)+1}\ast d\ast,\qquad\Delta=d\delta+\delta d.

One can easily extend the above constructions to smooth oriented compact manifolds with boundary.

If
\mathcal{M}
is an oriented and compact
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary, then we have the itegtation by parts formula

(2.4)

\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle d\alpha,\beta\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle\alpha,\delta\beta\rangle\quad\text{for all $\alpha\in\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M})$ and $\beta\in\Omega^{k+1}(\mathcal{M})$}.

Lemma 2.1

(Stokes theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an oriented smooth manifold,
\dim\mathcal{M}=n
. If
\omega
is a compactly supported Lipschitz
(n-1)
-form, then

\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\omega=\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\omega.

Since the integration by parts holds for Lipschitz functions, the standard proof (cf.
[
48
, Theorem 16.11]
) applies verbatim to the case of Lipschitz forms.

2.4.
Symplectic and contact forms

Definition 2.2
.

With the coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
denoted by
(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n})
, the
standard symplectic form
is

(2.5)

\upomega=\sum_{i=1}^{n}dx_{i}\wedge dy_{i}\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\quad\text{that is,}\quad\upomega(v,w)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(v^{x_{i}}w^{y_{i}}-v^{y_{i}}w^{x_{i}}\right),

where

v=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v^{x_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}+v^{y_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad w=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{x_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}+w^{y_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}.

We also regard
\upomega
as a differential form

\upomega\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
with constant coefficients.

Remark 2.3
.

In what follows
\upomega
will always stand for the standard symplectic form.

Definition 2.4
.

A vector space
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
is
isotropic
if
\upomega(v,w)=0
for all
v,w\in V
.

Lemma 2.5
.

If
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
is isotropic, then
\dim V\leq n
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{J}:\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
be an isomorphism defined by

\mathcal{J}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}},\qquad\mathcal{J}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\right)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\quad\text{for $i=1,\ldots,n$.}

It is easy to check that

\upomega(v,w)=-\langle v,\mathcal{J}w\rangle

for
v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,
where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the standard scalar product in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
If
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
is isotropic,
then for all
v,w\in V
,
0=\upomega(v,w)=-\langle v,\mathcal{J}w\rangle
. Therefore,

\mathcal{J}
maps
V
to the orthogonal complement of
V
so

2\dim V=\dim V+\dim(\mathcal{J}V)\leq 2n,\qquad\dim V\leq n.

∎

Remark 2.6
.

If we identify
T\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with
T\mathbb{C}^{n}
by

T\mathbb{R}^{2n}\ni\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}},\,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}},\,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{n}}\Big)\mapsto\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+i\,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}+i\,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{n}}\Big)\in T\mathbb{C}^{n},

then
\mathcal{J}
corresponds to the multiplication by
i
.

Definition 2.7
.

Given
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
1\leq k\leq n
, we define the
interior product

\iota_{w}:\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}\to\bigwedge\nolimits^{k-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}\quad\text{by}\quad\iota_{w}(\xi)(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k-1})=\xi(w,v_{1},\ldots,v_{k-1}).

Lemma 2.8
.

If
\xi\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}
,
\eta\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then

\iota_{v}(\xi\wedge\eta)=\iota_{v}(\xi)\wedge\eta+(-1)^{k}\xi\wedge\iota_{v}(\eta).

For a proof, see
[
48
, Lemma 14.13]
or
[
69
, Proposition 2.12]
.

If
\gamma
is a differential form, then we will write:

\gamma^{k}=\underbrace{\gamma\wedge\ldots\wedge\gamma}_{k}.

We have

(2.6)

\upomega^{n}=\underbrace{\upomega\wedge\ldots\wedge\upomega}_{n}=n!\,dx_{1}\wedge dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{n}\wedge dy_{n}.

Lemma

2.8
and a simple induction argument imply that

(2.7)

\iota_{v}(\upomega^{k})=k\upomega^{k-1}\wedge\iota_{v}(\upomega),\quad\text{for $v\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}$.}

Lemma 2.9
.

For
1\leq k\leq n
, the Lefschetz operator

L^{k}:\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\to\bigwedge\nolimits^{n+k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*},\quad L^{k}(\xi)=\xi\wedge\upomega^{k}

is an isomorphism.

Remark 2.10
.

The result is known. Standard proofs involve representation theory of
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
, see
[
43
, Proposition 1.2.30]
. The proof presented below, due to Calabi, is very elementary, see
[
8
, Proposition 1.1a]
and
[
7
]
.

Proof.

Since the spaces
\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}
and
\bigwedge\nolimits^{n+k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}
have equal dimensions, it suffices to show that
L^{k}
is injective. We will use a backwards induction starting with
k=n
. If
k=n
, then (
2.6
) implies that

L^{n}:\bigwedge\nolimits^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}=\mathbb{R}\to\bigwedge\nolimits^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}

is an isomorphism and hence it is injective.

Suppose that the result is true for
k
. We need to prove it is true for
k-1
. Thus
we need to prove that if

L^{k-1}(\xi)=\xi\wedge\upomega^{k-1}=0
for some

\xi\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-(k-1)}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}
, then
\xi=0
.

Clearly,
\xi\wedge\upomega^{k}=0
. If
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, then
Lemma

2.8
and (
2.7
) yield

0=\iota_{v}(\xi\wedge\upomega^{k})=\iota_{v}(\xi)\wedge\upomega^{k}\pm\xi\wedge\iota_{v}(\upomega^{k})=\iota_{v}(\xi)\wedge\upomega^{k}\pm k\underbrace{\xi\wedge\upomega^{k-1}}_{0}\wedge\iota_{v}(\upomega)

so
L^{k}(\iota_{v}(\xi))=\iota_{v}(\xi)\wedge\upomega^{k}=0
and hence
\iota_{v}(\xi)=0
by the injectivity of
L^{k}
(induction hypothesis).
Since
\iota_{v}(\xi)=0
for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, it follows that
\xi=0
. The proof is complete.
∎

Corollary 2.11
.

The exterior product with the symplectic form is an isomorphism:

L^{1}(\cdot)=\cdot\wedge\upomega:\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\to\bigwedge\nolimits^{n+1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}.

Definition 2.12
.

The
standard contact form
on
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
with coordinates
(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},t)
is

\upalpha=dt+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(x_{j}dy_{j}-y_{j}dx_{j})\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}).

Remark 2.13
.

In what follows
\upalpha
will always stand for the standard contact form.

Coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
will be denoted by

(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n})\quad\text{and}\quad(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},t)

respectively so

(2.8)

\{dx_{I}\wedge dy_{J}:\,|I|+|J|=k\}

is a basis of
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}
and

(2.9)

\{dx_{I}\wedge dy_{J},\ dx_{I^{\prime}}\wedge dy_{J^{\prime}}\wedge dt:\,|I|+|J|=k,\ |I^{\prime}|+|J^{\prime}|=k-1\}

is a basis of
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})^{*}
.

We have a natural embedding

(2.10)

\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\subset\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})^{*}

which maps the basis (
2.8
) onto a subset of the basis (
2.9
) by the identity map. With this embedding elements of
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}
are regarded as elements of
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})^{*}
that do not contain
dt
.

In particular, at every point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
we can identify

d\upalpha(p)=4\sum_{i=1}^{n}dx_{i}\wedge dy_{i}\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})^{*}\quad\text{with}\quad 4\upomega\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\subset\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})^{*}.

Proposition 2.14
.

If
\kappa\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
,
n+1\leq k\leq 2n
, then there are smooth forms

\beta\in\Omega^{k-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma\in\Omega^{2n-k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})

that do not contain components with
dt
, and satisfy

\kappa=\beta\wedge\upalpha+\gamma\wedge(d\upalpha)^{k-n}.

Moreover, if
\kappa(p)=0
, then
\beta(p)=0
and
\gamma(p)=0
.

Proof.

Since

dx_{1}\wedge dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{n}\wedge dy_{n}\wedge\upalpha(p)=dx_{1}\wedge dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{n}\wedge dy_{n}\wedge dt\neq 0,

we conclude that for every
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
,

\{dx_{1},dy_{1},\ldots,dx_{n},dy_{n},\upalpha(p)\}

is a basis of
T^{*}_{p}\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}=\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}T^{*}_{p}\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
.
This basis induces a basis of
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}T_{p}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
consisting of the elements of the form

dx_{I}\wedge dy_{J}\qquad\text{and}\qquad dx_{I^{\prime}}\wedge dy_{J^{\prime}}\wedge\upalpha(p),

where
|I|+|J|=k
and
|I^{\prime}|+|J^{\prime}|=k-1
so we have a unique decomposition

\kappa(p)=\beta(p)\wedge\upalpha(p)+\delta(p),

where

\beta(p)
belongs to the span of
dx_{I^{\prime}}\wedge dy_{J^{\prime}}
,
|I^{\prime}|+|J^{\prime}|=k-1
, and

\delta(p)
, belongs to the span of elements
dx_{I}\wedge dy_{J}
,
|I|+|J|=k
, that do not contain
dt
.
Since the decomposition is unique, if
\kappa(p)=0
, then
\beta(p)=0
and
\delta(p)=0
. This and Lemma
2.9
will also imply that
\gamma(p)=0
.

Since
dx_{I}\wedge dy_{J}
,
|I|+|J|=k
is a basis of

\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\subset\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})^{*}
(see (
2.10
)) and coefficients of
\delta(p)
smoothly depend on
p
, we have that

\delta\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1},\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\right)\subset C^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1},\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})^{*}\right)=\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}).

According to
Lemma

2.9
,

L^{k-n}:\bigwedge\nolimits^{2n-k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\to\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\quad\text{and hence}\quad(L^{k-n})^{-1}:\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\to\bigwedge\nolimits^{2n-k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}

are isomorphisms. Therefore,

\gamma:=4^{n-k}(L^{k-n})^{-1}\circ\delta\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1},\bigwedge\nolimits^{2n-k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\right)\subset\Omega^{2n-k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})

is a smooth form that belongs to the span of
dx_{I}\wedge dy_{J}
,
|I|+|J|=2n-k
.
Now the definition of the Lefschetz operator yields

\delta=4^{k-n}L^{k-n}\circ\gamma=4^{k-n}\gamma\wedge\upomega^{k-n}=\gamma\wedge(d\upalpha)^{k-n}.

In the last equality we used the identification of

4^{k-n}\upomega^{k-n}\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{2k-2n}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{*}\subset\bigwedge\nolimits^{2k-2n}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})^{*}\quad\text{with}\quad(d\upalpha)^{k-n}\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{2k-2n}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})^{*}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Corollary 2.15
.

If
\kappa\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
,
n+1\leq k\leq 2n+1
, then there are smooth differential forms

\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in\Omega^{k-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Omega^{k-2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})

such that

\kappa=\beta\wedge\upalpha+\gamma\wedge d\upalpha=\beta^{\prime}\wedge\upalpha+d(\gamma^{\prime}\wedge\upalpha).

Moreover, if
\kappa(p)=0
, then
\beta(p)=0
and
\gamma(p)=0
and if
\kappa=0
in an open set
U
, then
\beta^{\prime}=0
and
\gamma^{\prime}=0
in
U
.

Proof.

If
n+1\leq k\leq 2n
, then
Proposition

2.14
yields

\kappa=\beta\wedge\upalpha+\tilde{\gamma}\wedge(d\upalpha)^{k-n}=\beta\wedge\upalpha+\underbrace{(\tilde{\gamma}\wedge(d\upalpha)^{k-n-1})}_{\gamma}\wedge d\upalpha.

If
k=2n+1
, then
\kappa
is proportional to the volume form
(d\upalpha)^{n}\wedge\upalpha
so there is a smooth function
f
such that

\kappa=f(d\upalpha)^{n}\wedge\upalpha=0\wedge\upalpha+\underbrace{f\upalpha\wedge(d\upalpha)^{n-1}}_{\gamma}\wedge d\upalpha.

This proves existence of the decomposition

\kappa=\beta\wedge\upalpha+\gamma\wedge d\upalpha
which also yields

\kappa=\big(\beta-(-1)^{k-2}d\gamma\big)\wedge\upalpha+d\big((-1)^{k-2}\gamma\wedge\upalpha\big):=\beta^{\prime}\wedge\upalpha+d(\gamma^{\prime}\wedge\upalpha).

The proof is complete.
∎

2.5.
The space of Hölder continuous functions

In this section we collect basic properties of spaces of Hölder continuous functions.
While all results presented here are known, we decided to provide details, because
some of the results are difficult to find and, secondly,
good understanding of the material presented here plays an important role throughout the paper.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces and
0<\gamma\leq 1
.
A mapping
f:X\to Y
is
\gamma
-Hölder continuous if

(2.11)

[f]_{\gamma}=[f]_{C^{0,\gamma}}:=\sup\left\{\frac{d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))}{d_{X}(x,y)^{\gamma}}:\,x\neq y\right\}<\infty.

The space of all
\gamma
-Hölder continuous mappings
f:X\to Y
will be denoted by
C^{0,\gamma}(X;Y)
.

C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm b}(X;Y)
is the space of bounded
\gamma
-Hölder continuous mappings i.e., the image of every mapping is contained in a ball in
Y
.

The space
C^{0,\gamma+}(X;Y)
is a space of mappings
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(X;Y)
such that for every compact set
K\subset X
we have

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\ \sup\left\{\frac{d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))}{d_{X}(x,y)^{\gamma}}:\ x,y\in K,\ 0<d_{X}(x,y)\leq t\right\}=0.

Fix
A\subset X
. Then for
\varepsilon>0
, we define

(2.12)

[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon,A}:=\sup\left\{\frac{d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))}{d_{X}(x,y)^{\gamma}}:\,x,y\in A,\ 0<d_{X}(x,y)<\varepsilon\right\},

and we set
[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon}:=[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon,X}
.

Clearly
[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon,A}\leq[f]_{\gamma,\infty,X}=[f]_{\gamma,\infty}=[f]_{\gamma}
.
Observe also that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\,[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon,A}=0

if
f\in C^{0,\gamma+}(X;Y)
, and
A\Subset X
is a relatively compact subset of
X
.

Note that

C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})
is a Banach space with respect to the norm

\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}:=\|f\|_{\infty}+[f]_{\gamma}.

We equip
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma+}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})
with the norm of
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})
.
In the special case when
m=1
i.e., when
Y=\mathbb{R}
, we use notation
C^{0,\gamma}(X)
,
C^{0,\gamma+}(X)
,
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
,
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma+}(X)
.

The space
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
is a real Banach algebra with respect to the pointwise multiplication. Namely we have:

Lemma 2.17
.

C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
is a real Banach space. Moreover if
f,g\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
, then

fg\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
and

\|fg\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\leq\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\|g\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}
.

Proof.

The proof that
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
is a Banach space is easy and left to the reader. Since

|(fg)(x)-(fg)(y)|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}|g(x)-g(y)|+|f(x)-f(y)|\|g\|_{\infty}
, it follows that

[fg]_{\gamma}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}[g]_{\gamma}+[f]_{\gamma}\|g\|_{\infty}.

Therefore

\|fg\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\|g\|_{\infty}+\|f\|_{\infty}[g]_{\gamma}+[f]_{\gamma}\|g\|_{\infty}\leq\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\|g\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}.

∎

Corollary 2.18
.

If
f_{\varepsilon}\to f
and
g_{\varepsilon}\to g
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, then
f_{\varepsilon}g_{\varepsilon}\to fg
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

Lemma

2.17
yields

\|f_{\varepsilon}g_{\varepsilon}-fg\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\leq\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\|g_{\varepsilon}-g\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}+\|f_{\varepsilon}-f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\|g\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}
and the right hand side converges to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
X
be a metric space. If
f,g\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
u\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
\alpha,\gamma\in(0,1]
, then

u\circ f\in C^{0,\alpha\gamma}_{\rm b}(X).

Moreover, for any
0<\beta<\alpha
we have

(2.13)

\|u\circ f-u\circ g\|_{C^{0,\beta\gamma}}\lesssim[u]_{\alpha}\left(\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}+\|g\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\right)^{\beta}\|f-g\|_{\infty}^{\alpha-\beta},

where the implied constant depends on
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
only.

In particular, the composition operator

(2.14)

C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})\ni f\mapsto u\circ f\in C^{0,\beta\gamma}_{\rm b}(X)

is continuous.

Remark 2.20
.

In fact, we prove a sightly stronger estimate than (
2.13
),

\|u\circ f-u\circ g\|_{C^{0,\beta\gamma}}\lesssim[u]_{\alpha}\left([f]_{\gamma}+[g]_{\gamma}+\|f-g\|_{\infty}\right)^{\beta}\|f-g\|_{\infty}^{\alpha-\beta}.

Proof.

The fact that
u\circ f\in C^{0,\alpha\gamma}_{\rm b}(X)
follows from the observation that
u\circ f
is bounded in the supremum norm (because the image of
f
is bounded and
u
is continuous) and the easy estimate

[u\circ f]_{\alpha\gamma}\leq[u]_{\alpha}[f]_{\gamma}^{\alpha}.

Clearly, we have

(2.15)

\|u\circ f-u\circ g\|_{\infty}\leq[u]_{\alpha}\|f-g\|_{\infty}^{\alpha}.

Now, we will estimate
[u\circ f-u\circ g]_{\beta\gamma}
. Let
A>0
. The value of
A
will be determined later.

If
d(x,y)<A
, then

(2.16)

\begin{split}&\big|\big((u\circ f)-(u\circ g)\big)(x)-\big((u\circ f)-(u\circ g)\big)(y)\big|\\
&\leq|(u\circ f)(x)-(u\circ f)(y)|+|(u\circ g)(x)-(u\circ g)(y)|\\
&\leq\left([u\circ f]_{\alpha\gamma}+[u\circ g]_{\alpha\gamma}\right)d(x,y)^{\alpha\gamma}\\
&\lesssim[u]_{\alpha}\left([f]_{\gamma}+[g]_{\gamma}\right)^{\alpha}d(x,y)^{(\alpha-\beta)\gamma}d(x,y)^{\beta\gamma}\\
&\leq[u]_{\alpha}\left([f]_{\gamma}+[g]_{\gamma}\right)^{\alpha}A^{(\alpha-\beta)\gamma}d(x,y)^{\beta\gamma}.\end{split}

If
d(x,y)\geq A
, then

(2.17)

\begin{split}&\big|\big((u\circ f)-(u\circ g)\big)(x)-\big((u\circ f)-(u\circ g)\big)(y)\big|\\
&\leq 2\|u\circ f-u\circ g\|_{\infty}\leq 2[u]_{\alpha}\|f-g\|_{\infty}^{\alpha}d(x,y)^{-\beta\gamma}d(x,y)^{\beta\gamma}\\
&\lesssim[u]_{\alpha}\|f-g\|_{\infty}^{\alpha}A^{-\beta\gamma}d(x,y)^{\beta\gamma}.\end{split}

Now we choose
A>0
so that the right hand sides on (
2.16
) and (
2.17
) are equal i.e.,

A=\left(\frac{\|f-g\|_{\infty}}{[f]_{\gamma}+[g]_{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}.

This yields

(2.18)

[u\circ f-u\circ g]_{\beta\gamma}\leq[u]_{\alpha}\left([f]_{\gamma}+[g]_{\gamma}\right)^{\beta}\|f-g\|_{\infty}^{\alpha-\beta}.

Now, (
2.15
) and (
2.18
) imply (
2.13
).
∎

Taking
m=1
,
u(x)=x
and
\alpha=1
yields the following results.

Corollary 2.21
.

Let
(X,d)
be a metric space and
0<\gamma\leq 1
. If
(f_{n})\subset C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
is a bounded sequence (with respect to the norm of
C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm b}(X)
) and
f_{n}\rightrightarrows f
uniformly, then
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
and for any
0<\beta<\gamma
,
f_{n}\to f
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}(X)
as
n\to\infty
.

Corollary 2.22
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
\gamma\in(0,1]
. If
(f_{n})\subset C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
is a bounded sequence (with respect to the norm of
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
) that is pointwise convergent on a dense subset of
X
, then there is
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
, such that for every compact (or more generally, totally bounded) set
K\subset X
and every
0<\beta<\gamma
,
f_{n}\to f
in
C^{0,\beta}_{\rm b}(K)
.

Proof.

Since the family
(f_{n})
is equicontinuous, convergence on a dense subset of
X
implies convergence at every point of
X
. Denote the limit function by
f
. Clearly,
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
. If
K\subset X
is compact (or totaly bounded), then the pointwise convergence on
K
of the equicontinuous family
(f_{n})
implies the uniform convergence on
K
and the result follows from
Corollary

2.21
.
∎

In the next result we will need the following general version of the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem, see

[
59
, Theorem 1.4.9]
.

Lemma 2.23

(Arzelà-Ascoli)
.

Let
X
be a separable metric space and let
(f_{n})
be a bounded and equicontinuous sequence of real valued
functions defined on
X
. Then, there is a subsequence
(f_{n_{k}})
that is pointwise convergent on
X
to a uniformly continuous function and the convergence if uniform on every compact (or more generally, every totally bounded) subset of
X
.

Proof.

In a more standard version of the theorem it is assumed that
X
is compact, but the proof in this more general case is almost the same and similar to the proof of
Corollary

2.22
.
First, using a diagonal argument we find a subsequence
(f_{n_{k}})
that is convergent on a countable and dense subset of
X
. Since the family is equicontinuous, convergence on a dense subset implies pointwise convergence at every point of
X
. If
K\subset X
is compact (or totally bounded), then pointwise convergence on
K
of the equicontinuous family
(f_{n_{k}})
implies the uniform convergence of
(f_{n_{k}})
on
K
.
∎

Corollary 2.24
.

Let
X
be a separable metric space and
0<\gamma\leq 1
. If

(f_{n})\subset C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
is a bounded sequence (in the norm of
C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm b}
), then there is a function
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
and a subsequence

(f_{n_{k}})
such that for any compact (or more generally, totally bounded) set
K\subset X
and any
0<\beta<\gamma
,

\|f_{n_{k}}-f\|_{C^{0,\beta}(K)}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
.

Proof.

It follows from
Lemma

2.23
that there
is a subsequence
(f_{n_{k}})
that converges uniformly to a function
f
on every compact (totally bounded) set
K\subset X
.
Clearly,
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
. Now,
Corollary

2.21
yields the convergence

\|f_{n_{k}}-f\|_{C^{0,\beta}(K)}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
for any
0<\beta<\gamma
.
∎

If
u\in C^{0,1}
,
Proposition

2.19
yields continuity of the composition operator (
2.14
) for all
\beta<1
. In order to prove continuity with
\beta=1
, i.e., continuity in
C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm b}(X)
, we need stronger assumptions about
u
.

Definition 2.25
.

A continuous, non-decreasing, and concave function
\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
, such that
\omega(0)=0
is a modulus
of continuity of a function
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
defined on a metric space
(X,d)
if

(2.19)

|f(x)-f(y)|\leq\omega(d(x,y))\quad\text{for all $x,y\in X$.}

Lemma 2.26
.

If
\omega_{1}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is non-decreasing, bounded and
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\omega_{1}(t)=0
, then there is a continuous bounded concave function
\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
,
\omega(0)=0
, such that
\omega_{1}(t)\leq\omega(t)
for all
f\in[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

Let

(2.20)

\omega(t):=\inf\{\alpha t+\beta:\,\text{$\omega_{1}(s)\leq\alpha s+\beta$ for all $s\in[0,\infty)$}\}.

It is easy to see that
\omega
is concave and
\omega\geq\omega_{1}
, so
\omega
is nonnegative.
Moreover, concavity implies continuity of
\omega
on
(0,\infty)
, see

[
60
, Theorem A, p.4]
.
Since
\alpha\geq 0
in (
2.20
),
\omega
is non-decreasing. It remains to show that
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\omega(t)=0
.
For
0<\beta<\sup_{s>0}\omega_{1}(s)
define
\alpha=\sup_{s>0}(\omega_{1}(s)-\beta)/s
. Note that
\alpha
is positive and finite, because
\omega_{1}(s)-\beta<0
for small
s
.
Clearly,
\omega_{1}(s)\leq\alpha s+\beta
for all
s>0
.
Hence
\omega(t)\leq\alpha t+\beta
, so
0\leq\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\omega(t)\leq\beta
. Since
\beta>0
can be arbitrarily small, we conclude that
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\omega(t)=0
.
∎

The following result is well known.

Proposition 2.27
.

Every bounded and uniformly continuous function on a metric space has a modulus of continuity.

Proof.

If
f
is constant, then we take
\omega(t)\equiv 0
. Thus assume that
f
is not constant.
Let

\omega_{1}(t)=\sup\{|f(x)-f(y)|:\,d(x,y)\leq t\}
. Since the function
f
is uniformly continuous and bounded,
the function
\omega_{1}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is non-decreasing, bounded, and
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\omega_{1}(t)=0
.
Clearly,
|f(x)-f(y)|\leq\omega_{1}(d(x,y))
for all
x,y\in X
, and
the result follows from
Lemma

2.26
.
∎

Lemma 2.28
.

Assume that
u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
is such that
\|u\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}<\infty
, and
\nabla u
has the modulus of continuity
\omega
i.e.,

|\nabla u(x)-\nabla u(y)|\leq\omega(|x-y|)\quad\text{for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$.}

(a)

If
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
0<\gamma\leq 1
, then
u\circ f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
and

\|u\circ f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\leq\|u\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}[f]_{\gamma}.

(b)

If
f,g\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm b}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
0<\gamma\leq 1
, then

\|(u\circ f)-(u\circ g)\|_{\infty}\leq\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}\|f-g\|_{\infty}

and

(2.21)

[(u\circ g)-(u\circ f)]_{\gamma}\leq\omega(\|f-g\|_{\infty})[f]_{\gamma}+\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}[f-g]_{\gamma}.

Therefore, the mapping

C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm b}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})\ni f\mapsto u\circ f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)

is continuous.

Remark 2.29
.

A particularly important case is when

\nabla u\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}
for some
0<\gamma\leq 1
, i.e. when
\omega(t)=Ct^{\gamma}
.

Proof.

(a) The inequality
\|u\circ f\|_{\infty}\leq\|u\|_{\infty}
is obvious. To prove the estimate

[u\circ f]_{\gamma}\leq\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}[f]_{\gamma}
we argue as follows

\displaystyle|(u\circ f)(x)-(u\circ f)(y)|=\left|\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}u(tf(x)+(1-t)f(y))\,dt\right|

\displaystyle\leq|f(x)-f(y)|\int_{0}^{1}|\nabla u(tf(x)+(1-t)f(y))|\,dt\leq\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}[f]_{\gamma}d(x,y)^{\gamma}.

(b)
The inequality

\|(u\circ f)-(u\circ g)\|_{\infty}\leq\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}\|f-g\|_{\infty}
follows from almost the same argument as the one used in part (a). To prove (
2.21
) we use a similar argument, but the estimates are slightly more involved. For simplicity of notation let

\xi_{t}=tf(x)+(1-t)f(y)\quad\text{and}\quad\eta_{t}=tg(x)+(1-t)g(y).

Then

\displaystyle\big((u\circ f)-(u\circ g)\big)(x)-\big((u\circ f)-(u\circ g)\big)(y)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}(u(\xi_{t})-u(\eta_{t}))\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(\xi_{t})(f(x)-f(y))-\nabla u(\eta_{t})(g(x)-g(y))\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla u(\xi_{t})-\nabla u(\eta_{t}))(f(x)-f(y))+\nabla u(\eta_{t})((f-g)(x)-(f-g)(y))\,dt

so

\displaystyle\big|\big((u\circ f)-(u\circ g)\big)(x)-\big((u\circ f)-(u\circ g)\big)(y)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\nabla u(\xi_{t})-\nabla u(\eta_{t})|\,[f]_{\gamma}d(x,y)^{\gamma}+\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}[f-g]_{\gamma}d(x,y)^{\gamma},

and it remains to observe that

|\xi_{t}-\eta_{t}|\leq\|f-g\|_{\infty}
.
∎

Corollary 2.30
.

Let
u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
0<\gamma\leq 1
. If
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm b}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})
, then
u\circ f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
.
Moreover, the composition operator
C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm b}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})\ni f\mapsto u\circ f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
is continuous.

Proof.

By the definition of the class
C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm b}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})
, the mapping
f
is bounded.
Let
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
be equal
1
on the image of
f
, and let
\tilde{u}=\varphi u
.
Proposition

2.27
shows that
\tilde{u}
satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

2.28
and hence

u\circ f=\tilde{u}\circ f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(X)
. If
f_{i}\to f
in
C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm b}(X;\mathbb{R}^{m})
, then images of all mappings
f,f_{i}
are contained in one bounded set. Taking
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
equal
1
on that set allows us to use
Lemma

2.28
and conclude that
u\circ f_{i}=\tilde{u}\circ f_{i}\to\tilde{u}\circ f=u\circ f
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}
.
∎

Definition 2.31
.

Fix a mollifier kernel
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
,
\eta\geq 0
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta\,dx=1
, and for
\varepsilon>0
define

\eta_{\varepsilon}(x)=\varepsilon^{-n}\eta(x/\varepsilon)
. For
f\in L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we define the
\varepsilon
-mollification
of
f
by

f_{\varepsilon}(x):=f*\eta_{\varepsilon}(x)=\varepsilon^{-n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(z)\eta\left(\frac{x-z}{\varepsilon}\right)\,dz.

Clearly,
f_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}
. If
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
1\leq p<\infty
, then

(2.22)

\|f*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{p}\leq\|f\|_{p}\quad\text{and}\quad\text{ $\|f_{\varepsilon}-f\|_{p}\to 0$ as $\varepsilon\to 0$.}

Since
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial\eta_{\varepsilon}/\partial x_{i}\,dx=0
, we have a useful identity that will be repeatedly used in later sections

(2.23)

\begin{split}\frac{\partial f_{\varepsilon}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)&=f*\frac{\partial\eta_{\varepsilon}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)=\left((f-f(x))*\frac{\partial\eta_{\varepsilon}}{\partial x_{i}}\right)(x)\\
&=\varepsilon^{-n-1}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}}(f(x-z)-f(x))\frac{\partial\eta}{\partial x_{i}}\left(\frac{z}{\varepsilon}\right)\,dz.\end{split}

The next results will discuss approximation of Hölder functions by smooth ones.

Proposition 2.32
.

If
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
0<\gamma\leq 1
, then

\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\leq\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}
,

(2.24)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}=\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\|f_{\varepsilon}-f\|_{C^{0,\beta}}=0

for any
0<\beta<\gamma
.
In particular
f
can be approximated by smooth functions
in the
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}
norm.

Remark 2.33
.

Later we will see that smooth functions are
not
dense in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, see
Proposition

2.37
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}
, and
[f_{\varepsilon}]_{\gamma}\leq[f]_{\gamma}
, so

\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\leq\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}
.
It is also easy to see that
f_{\varepsilon}\rightrightarrows f
uniformly, and hence
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}=\|f\|_{\infty}
.

For
x\neq y
, we have

\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{|x-y|^{\gamma}}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{|f_{\varepsilon}(x)-f_{\varepsilon}(y)|}{|x-y|^{\gamma}}\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}[f_{\varepsilon}]_{\gamma}\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}[f_{\varepsilon}]_{\gamma}\leq[f]_{\gamma}.

Taking the supremum over all
x\neq y
, yields
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}[f_{\varepsilon}]_{\gamma}=[f]_{\gamma}
. This and uniform convergence of
f_{\varepsilon}\rightrightarrows f
proves the first limit in (
2.24
). Convergence in
the
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}
norm follows from
Corollary

2.21
.
∎

Corollary 2.34
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact Riemannian manifold with or without boundary, and let
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
,
0<\gamma\leq 1
. Then there is a sequence
(f_{k})\subset C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
such that
\sup_{k}\|f_{k}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}
and
f_{k}\rightrightarrows f
uniformly. Therefore,
f_{k}\to f
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}(\mathcal{M})
for any
0<\beta<\gamma
.

Proof.

Assume first that
\partial\mathcal{M}=\varnothing
.
Using the Whitney embedding theorem we may assume that
\mathcal{M}
is smoothly embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, where
n=\dim\mathcal{M}
. The new Riemannian metric on
\mathcal{M}
inherited from the embedding into
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, will be comparable to the original one due to compactness of
\mathcal{M}
.

According to the tubular neighborhood theorem, see for instance
[
67
, §2.12.3]
, there is an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
containing
\mathcal{M}
, such that the nearest point projection
\pi:U\to\mathcal{M}
is uniquely defined, smooth and Lipschitz.

Let
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
satisfy
\phi=1
on
\mathcal{M}
. Then we can define bounded extension and trace operators

C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{c}^{0,\gamma}(U)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})

by

Ef(x)=\phi(x)(f\circ\pi)(x)\quad\text{and}\quad Tf=f|_{\mathcal{M}},

so
(T\circ E)f=f
for
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
.
This allows us to conclude the result as a direct consequence of
Proposition

2.32
.
Indeed, we approximate
f
by
T\circ(Ef)_{\varepsilon}
.

If
\partial\mathcal{M}\neq\varnothing
, we can glue two copies of
\mathcal{M}
along the boundary to and obtain a closed manifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
, extend
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
functions to
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
and apply the above argument to
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
.
∎

Remark 2.35
.

We will use a similar argument in the proof of
Corollary

7.15
.

Remark 2.36
.

If in addition the manifold
\mathcal{M}
is oriented,

Corollary

2.34
easily extends to the case of approximation of forms
\omega\in\Omega^{k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
. We just need to replace the extension operator by

E\omega(x):=\phi(x)(\pi^{*}\omega)(x)\in\Omega^{k}C_{c}^{0,\gamma}(U)
and approximate
E\omega
by a standard mollification in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
. We leave details to the reader.

Proposition 2.37
.

Let
0<\gamma<1
. Then a function
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

can be approximated by a sequence smooth functions
f_{k}\in C^{\infty}

so that for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\|f_{k}-f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}(K)}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, if and only if

f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma+}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Remark 2.38
.

If
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,1/2}(\mathbb{R})
equals

|x|^{1/2}
in a neighborhood of
0
, then
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,1/2}\setminus C_{\rm b}^{0,1/2+}
and hence it cannot be approximated by smooth functions on compact sets in the
C_{\rm b}^{0,1/2}
norm.

Remark 2.39
.

The result is false for
\gamma=1
, because
C^{0,1+}
consists of constant functions only (partial derivatives are equal zero). In the proof of Proposition
2.37
we use the assumption
\gamma<1
only in one place when we consider the exponent
1/(1-\gamma)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
can be approximated by a sequence of smooth functions

f_{k}\in C^{\infty}
. We need to show that
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma+}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Let
B_{R}=B(0,R)
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be given. Then for a sufficiently large
k
,

|(f_{k}-f)(y)-(f_{k}-f)(x)|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|x-y|^{\gamma}\quad\text{for all $x,y\in B_{R}$.}

Let
M=\sup_{B_{R}}|\nabla f_{k}|
. The mean value theorem yields

|f(y)-f(x)|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|x-y|^{\gamma}+|f_{k}(y)-f_{k}(x)|\leq\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+M|x-y|^{1-\gamma}\right)|x-y|^{\gamma}

so

|f(y)-f(x)|\leq\varepsilon|x-y|^{\gamma}\quad\text{for all $x,y\in B_{R}$ satisfying $|x-y|<(\varepsilon/2M)^{1/(1-\gamma)}$.}

This proves that
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma+}
.

Suppose now that

f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma+}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We will show that the approximation by mollification
f_{t}
has the desired property i.e.,
for every ball
B_{R}
,
\|f_{t}-f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}(B_{R})}\to 0
as
t\to 0
.
Since
f_{t}\rightrightarrows f
uniformly, it remains to estimate the constant in the Hölder estimate of the difference
f_{t}-f
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
be given. It follows from the definition of
C^{0,\gamma+}

that there is
R>\tau>0
such that if
x,y\in B_{2R}
,
|x-y|<\tau
, then
|f(x)-f(y)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon|x-y|^{\gamma}
. Hence,

|f_{t}(x)-f_{t}(y)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon|x-y|^{\gamma}

if
0<t<R
and

x,y\in B_{R}
satisfy
|x-y|<\tau
. This easily follows from the definition of
f_{t}
, because
f_{t}(x)

is a weighted average of
f
on the ball
B(x,t)\subset B_{2R}
.
Therefore,

(2.25)

|(f_{t}-f)(x)-(f_{t}-f)(y)|\leq\varepsilon|x-y|^{\gamma}\quad\text{for all $x,y\in B_{R}$ satisfying $|x-y|<\tau$.}

Let
0<\delta<R
be such that
\|f_{t}-f\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon\tau^{\gamma}/2
for all
0<t<\delta
.
If
x,y\in B_{R}
,
|x-y|\geq\tau
, then

(2.26)

|(f_{t}-f)(x)-(f_{t}-f)(y)|\leq 2\|f_{t}-f\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon\tau^{\gamma}\leq\varepsilon|x-y|^{\gamma}.

We proved in (
2.25
) and (
2.26
) that if
0<t<\delta
, then

|(f_{t}-f)(x)-(f_{t}-f)(y)|\leq\varepsilon|x-y|^{\gamma}\quad\text{for all $x,y\in B_{R}$.}

This proves that
f_{t}\to f
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(B_{R})
. The proof is complete.
∎

Corollary 2.40
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a connected compact Riemannian manifold with or without boundary and let
0<\beta<\gamma\leq 1
. Then

(1)

The embedding
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})\Subset C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}(\mathcal{M})
is compact.

(2)

Every function
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
can be approximated by
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
functions in the
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}
norm.

(3)

If
0<\gamma<1
, then
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
can be approximated by
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
functions in the
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}
norm
if and only if
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma+}(\mathcal{M})
.

Proof.

(1) follows from
Corollary

2.24
. (2) follows from
Corollary

2.34
and (3) is a consequence of
Proposition

2.37
and the proof of
Corollary

2.34
.
∎

2.6.
Sobolev spaces

There are many excellent textbooks on Sobolev spaces and we assume that the reader is familiar with the material of Chapter 5 in
[
23
]
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open,
1\leq p\leq\infty
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. The Sobolev space
W^{m,p}(\Omega)
is the space of all functions
f\in L^{p}(\Omega)
with distributional derivatives satisfying
D^{\alpha}f\in L^{p}(\Omega)
, for all
|\alpha|\leq m
.
W^{m,p}(\Omega)
equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{m,p}:=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\|D^{k}f\|_{p}

is a Banach space.

We will be mainly interested in the first order Sobolev space
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
,
\|f\|_{1,p}=\|f\|_{p}+\|\nabla f\|_{p}
, as well as in the fractional Sobolev spaces defined below.
If
1\leq p<\infty
,
W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
is the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in the Sobolev norm.

By analogy with the case of Hölder continuous functions we will use notation

[f]_{1,p}=\|Df\|_{p}.

If
\mathcal{M}
is a smooth compact manifold, with or without boundary, we define the Sobolev space
W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M})
as the set of all functions
f
whose representation in a good coordinate system (Section
2.3
) belongs to
W^{1,p}
. The space
W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M})
is equipped with a norm defined by a formula similar to (
2.3
).
It is well known that
W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
coincides with the space on bounded Lipschitz functions and clearly the same is true for
W^{1,\infty}(\mathcal{M})
.

The next result will be useful.

Lemma 2.41
.

If
f,g\in W^{1,n}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
f-g\in W_{0}^{1,n}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is open, then

(2.27)

\int_{\Omega}df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}=\int_{\Omega}dg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}.

Proof.

Assume first that
f,g\in C^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
f-g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Since

(2.28)

df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}-dg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}dg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{i-1}\wedge d(f_{i}-g_{i})\wedge df_{i+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n},

integration and Stokes’ theorem yield

\int_{\Omega}df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}-dg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i-1}\int_{\partial\Omega}(f_{i}-g_{i})dg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{i-1}\wedge df_{i+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}=0,

because
f_{i}-g_{i}=0
in a neighborhood of
\partial\Omega
.

Assume now that
f,g\in W^{1,n}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
f-g\in W_{0}^{1,n}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let

g^{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\phi^{\varepsilon}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
be such that
g^{\varepsilon}\to g
and
\phi^{\varepsilon}\to f-g
in
W^{1,n}
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. Then
f^{\varepsilon}:=g^{\varepsilon}+\phi^{\varepsilon}\to f
.

The result is true for
f^{\varepsilon}
and
g^{\varepsilon}
, and it suffices to prove that the integrals (
2.27
) for
f^{\varepsilon}
and
g^{\varepsilon}
converge to the corresponding integrals for
f
and
g
.

Applying (
2.28
) to
f
and to
f^{\varepsilon}
in place of
g
, and using Hadamard’s inequality

|dh_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dh_{n}|\leq|\nabla h_{1}|\ldots|\nabla h_{n}|

(the volume of a parallelepiped in bounded by the product of lengths of the edges), we obtain

(2.29)

|df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}-df^{\varepsilon}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df^{\varepsilon}_{n}|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\nabla f^{\varepsilon}_{1}|\ldots|\nabla f^{\varepsilon}_{i-1}|\,|\nabla(f_{i}-f^{\varepsilon}_{i})|\,|\nabla f_{i+1}|\ldots|\nabla f_{n}|.

Since the integral of the right hand side converges to zero by Hölder’s inequality, it follows that

(2.30)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}df^{\varepsilon}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df^{\varepsilon}_{n}\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}\quad\text{as $\varepsilon\to 0^{+}$.}

A similar argument applies to
g
and
g^{\varepsilon}
.
∎

In the remaining part of this section we will discuss fractional Sobolev spaces. While there are many excellent textbooks discussing the theory of Sobolev spaces,
it is much harder to find a good introduction to the theory of fractional Sobolev spaces.
We refer the reader to Chapters 3 and 4 of
[
20
]
and Chapters 14 and 15 of
[
50
]
for proofs of results stated below.
In Remark
2.46
we will provide detailed references.

Definition 2.42
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an
n
-dimensional Riemannan manifold without boundary (in particular
\mathcal{M}
can be a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
).
Let
0<s<1
,
1<p<\infty
. The fractional Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(\mathcal{M})
is the space of all functions
f\in L^{p}(\mathcal{M})
such that

\|f\|_{s,p}=\|f\|_{p}+[f]_{s,p}<\infty
, where

[f]_{s,p}=\left(\int_{\mathcal{M}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|^{p}}{d(x,y)^{n+sp}}\,dx\,dy\right)^{1/p}.

The subspace
W^{s,p}_{0}(\mathcal{M})\subset W^{s,p}(\mathcal{M})
is the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
in the
W^{s,p}
norm.

A word of warning. In the literature there are different notions of “fractional Sobolev spaces”. Another popular choice are the so-called Bessel-potential spaces. The space that we are considering here is often referred to as Sobolev-Slobodeckij space, Besov space, or the trace space (cf. Lemma
2.45
). The seminorm
[f]_{s,p}
