is often called the Gagliardo-norm.

Lemma 2.43
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
or let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth closed
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold. Let
0<s<1
,
1<p<\infty
. Then
W^{s,p}(\mathcal{M})
is a Banach space and smooth functions
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})\cap W^{s,p}(\mathcal{M})
form a dense subset of
W^{s,p}(\mathcal{M})
.

Remark 2.44
.

With the same technique one can show that smooth forms
\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M})
are dense in
\Omega^{k}W^{s,p}(\mathcal{M})
,
0<s<1
,
1<p<\infty
,
0\leq k\leq n
, see also Remark
2.36
.

Lemma 2.45
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
or
let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth compact
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifolds with boundary,
n\geq 2
.
Let
1<p<\infty
.
Then, there is a unique bounded linear trace operator

\operatorname{Tr}:W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M})\to W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\partial\mathcal{M})

such that
\operatorname{Tr}f=f|_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
if
f\in C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
.
Moreover there is a bounded linear extension operator

(2.31)

\operatorname{ext}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}:W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\partial\mathcal{M})\to W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M})

such that
\operatorname{Tr}\circ\operatorname{ext}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}=\operatorname{Id}
on
W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\partial\mathcal{M})
.

In particular, if
\Omega
is a bounded domain with smooth boundary, then Sobolev functions in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
,
1<p<\infty
, have a well defined trace on the boundary that belongs to the fractional Sobolev space
W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\partial\Omega)
.

Remark 2.46
.

For a proof of Lemma
2.43
, see Proposition 4.27 and Proposition 4.24 in
[
20
]
, and for a proof of Lemma
2.45
, see Proposition 3.31 and Theorem 3.9 in
[
20
]
.
The reader may also find proofs of both Lemmata
2.43
and
2.45
in
[
50
]
.

While the proofs presented in
[
20
]
are mostly in the Euclidean case, the extension to the case of manifolds is straightforward (use partition of unity and local coordinate systems). In fact
[
20
, Proposition 3.31]
deals with the case of traces on the boundary of a smooth domain and the argument needed for the case of manifolds is similar.

While we will not prove the above results, in the next section we will show the construction of the extension operator.

Corollary 2.47
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth closed
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold and
1<p<\infty
,
1-\frac{1}{p}<\gamma\leq 1
. Then the embedding
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})\Subset W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\mathcal{M})
is compact.

Proof.

Let
1-\frac{1}{p}<\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
. Boundedness of the embedding

C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M})\subset W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\mathcal{M})
easily follows from the definition of the fractional Sobolev space so the result follows from Theorem
2.40
:

C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})\Subset C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M})\subset W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\mathcal{M})
.
∎

2.7.
The Gagliardo extension

In this section we will explain the construction of an extension operator from Lemma
2.45
in detail.
While we will not prove Lemma
2.45
, we will
prove a result weaker than (
2.31
), the existence of a bounded extension

(2.32)

\operatorname{ext}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}:C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\partial\mathcal{M})\to W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M}),\quad 1-\frac{1}{p}<\gamma\leq 1.

see Corollary
2.55
.

Definition 2.48

(Gagliardo extension)
.

Let
\eta
be a mollifier kernel as in Definition
2.31
and let
\psi\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty))
be a cut-off function
satisfying
0\leq\psi\leq 1
,
\psi(t)=1
for
0\leq t\leq 1/2
and
\psi(t)=0
for
t\geq 3/4
.
For a function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we define the extension operator
\operatorname{ext}_{R}f
by

(2.33)

\operatorname{ext}_{R}f(x,t)=\psi(t/R)(f*\eta_{t})(x)\quad\text{for $x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$ and $t>0$.}

Note that
\operatorname{ext}_{R}f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(0,\infty))
. Moreover if
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
equals zero outside the ball
B_{R}
,

\operatorname{ext}_{R}f
has compact support inside
B_{2R}\times[0,R)
.
That is, it does not necessarily vanish at the bottom part of the boundary
B_{R}\times\{0\}
, but it equals zero in a neighborhood of
the remaining part of the boundary
S_{2R}\times[0,R]\cup B_{2R}\times\{R\}
.

Lemma 2.49
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and let
n\geq 2
, then

\|\operatorname{ext}_{R}f\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\quad\text{for any $R>0$ and $f\in W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})$.}

For a proof see
[
20
, Proposition 3.62]
and also
[
50
]
.
Using this result, partition of unity and local coordinates one can construct the extension (
2.31
). Below, we will provide a detailed construction of such an extension, and we will prove boundedness of (
2.32
),
see Corollary
2.55
. A similar proof based on Lemma
2.49
leads to (
2.31
). We leave details to the reader.

We will need the following fact.

Lemma 2.50
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain,
\gamma\in(0,1]
, and
m>0
. If
F\in C^{1}(\Omega)
satisfies

|\nabla F(x)|\leq m\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)^{\gamma-1}\quad\text{for all }x\in\Omega,

then
F\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\Omega)
and
[F]_{\gamma}\leq C(\Omega,\gamma)m
.

Proof.

If
x,y\in\Omega
, then we can find a curve
\gamma:[0,L]\to\Omega
parametrized by arc-length, such that

L\lesssim|x-y|\qquad\text{and}\qquad\operatorname{dist}(\gamma(t),\partial\Omega)\gtrsim\min\{t,L-t\}.

Here the implied constants depend on
\Omega
only.
This is a well known and easy to prove fact. It is also a consequence of the fact that bounded Lipschitz domains are uniform, see e.g.,
[
35
, p. 225]
and references therein. Now we have

\begin{split}|F(x)-F(y)|&\leq\int_{0}^{L}|\nabla F(\gamma(t))|\,dt\leq m\int_{0}^{L}\operatorname{dist}(\gamma(t),\partial\Omega)^{\gamma-1}\,dt\\
&\lesssim m\int_{0}^{L}\min\{t,L-t\}^{\gamma-1}\,dt=2m\int_{0}^{L/2}t^{\gamma-1}\,dt=\frac{2^{1-\gamma}m}{\gamma}L^{\gamma}\lesssim m|x-y|^{\gamma}.\end{split}

The proof is complete.
∎

The next two results provide basic local estimates for the extension operator in the Hölder case.

Lemma 2.51
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1]
. Assume that a function
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
vanishes outside
B_{R}
. Then

\operatorname{ext}_{R}f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+})
satisfies

|D(\operatorname{ext}_{R}f)(x,t)|\lesssim[f]_{\gamma}\,t^{\gamma-1}.

Proof.

Since
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial\eta_{t}/\partial x_{i}\,dx=0
we can subtract the constant
f(x)
from
the function
f
in the following estimate

\begin{split}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}(\operatorname{ext}_{R}f(x,t))\right|&=\left|\psi(t/R)(f-f(x))*\frac{\partial\eta_{t}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\right|\lesssim t^{-n-1}\int_{B_{t}}|f(x-z)-f(x)|\,dz\\
&\lesssim t^{-n-1}t^{n}[f]_{\gamma}\,t^{\gamma}=t^{\gamma-1}[f]_{\gamma}.\end{split}

The estimate for the partial derivative with respect to
t
is similar, but slightly more involved. We will need three simple observations

•

When taking the derivative
\partial/\partial t
, we can subtract the constant number
f(x)
;

•

Since
f
vanishes on the boundary of
B_{R}
,
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq[f]_{\gamma}\,R^{\gamma}
;

•

We have
|\partial\eta_{t}/\partial t|\lesssim t^{-n-1}\chi_{B_{t}}
.

Applying the product rule to
\psi(t/R)\cdot(f*\eta_{t})(x)
and using the above observations we have

(2.34)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(\operatorname{ext}_{R}f(x,t))\right|\lesssim R^{-1}|f*\eta_{t}|(x)+\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(\underbrace{f*\eta_{t}-f(x)}_{(f-f(x))*\eta_{t}})\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim R^{-1}\|f\|_{\infty}+\Big(|f-f(x)|*\left|\frac{\partial\eta_{t}}{\partial t}\right|\Big)(x)\lesssim[f]_{\gamma}\,R^{\gamma-1}+t^{-n-1}\int_{B_{t}}|f(x-z)-f(x)|\,dz

\displaystyle\lesssim[f]_{\gamma}(R^{\gamma-1}+t^{\gamma-1}).

Since
\operatorname{ext}_{R}f(x,t)=0
when
t\geq R
, the above estimate improves to

\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(\operatorname{ext}_{R}f(x,t))\right|\lesssim[f]_{\gamma}\,t^{\gamma-1}.

We proved that the full derivative in
x
and
t
satisfies

|D(\operatorname{ext}_{R}f(x,t))|\lesssim[f]_{\gamma}\,t^{\gamma-1}
.
∎

Lemma 2.52
.

Let
1<p<\infty
,
1-\frac{1}{p}<\gamma\leq 1
and
R>0
. Assume that a function
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
vanishes outside
B_{R}
. Then

(2.35)

\displaystyle[\operatorname{ext}_{R}f]_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+})}

\displaystyle\lesssim[f]_{\gamma},

(2.36)

\displaystyle[\operatorname{ext}_{R}f]_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+})}

\displaystyle\lesssim[f]_{\gamma},

where the implied constants depend in particular on
R
and other given constants.

Proof.

Assume that
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
f=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R}
.
Note that
\operatorname{ext}_{R}f
vanishes outside
B_{2R}\times[0,R]
.

In particular, if
(x,t)\in\Omega
, where
\Omega=B_{5R}\times[0,5R]
and

\operatorname{ext}_{R}f(x,y)\neq 0
, then
\operatorname{dist}\{(x,t),\partial\Omega\}=t
.
Therefore, Lemma
2.51
shows that the function
F=\operatorname{ext}_{R}f
satisfies assumptions of Lemma
2.50
with

m\approx[f]_{\gamma}
, and (
2.35
) follows directly from Lemma
2.50
.

Now we will prove (
2.36
).
Since
\gamma>1-1/p
, we have

(\gamma-1)p>-1
, so the function
t^{(\gamma-1)p}
is integrable near zero and
Lemma
2.51
yields

[\operatorname{ext}_{R}f]^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+})}=[\operatorname{ext}_{R}f]^{p}_{W^{1,p}(B_{2R}\times[0,R])}\lesssim\int_{0}^{R}\int_{B_{2R}}[f]_{\gamma}^{p}\,t^{(\gamma-1)p}\,dx\,dt\lesssim[f]^{p}_{\gamma}\,R^{n+1+(\gamma-1)p}.

The proof is complete.
∎

The next result is a consequence of Lemma
2.52
.

Proposition 2.53
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary and let
1<p<\infty
.
Then, for
1\geq\gamma>1-\frac{1}{p}
there is a bounded linear extension operator

\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}:C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})\to W^{1,p}\cap C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M}\times[0,1])

such that the extension of
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
, is
C^{\infty}
smooth in
\mathcal{M}\times(0,1)
, and equal zero in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{M}\times\{1\}
.

Remark 2.54
.

The proof of Proposition
2.53
is based on Lemma
2.52
. If instead, we use Lemma
2.49
we obtain the existence of a bounded extension
\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}:W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\mathcal{M})\to W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M}\times[0,1])
. The construction of the extension operator remains the same.

The following result is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.53
and the collar neighborhood theorem according to which a neighborhood of
\partial\mathcal{M}
is diffeomorphic to
\partial\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]
.

Corollary 2.55
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with boundary,
1<p<\infty
and

1\geq\gamma>1-\frac{1}{p}
. Then, there is a bounded linear extension operator

\operatorname{ext}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}:C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\partial\mathcal{M})\to W^{1,p}\cap C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M}).

Proof of Proposition
2.53
.

Denote the Riemannian distance on
\mathcal{M}
by
d
.
Let
\varphi_{\alpha}:B_{3}\to\mathcal{M}
,
\alpha=1,2,\ldots,N
be parametrizations of
\mathcal{M}
such that
\bigcup_{\alpha=1}^{N}\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{1})=\mathcal{M}
(here
B_{3}=B(0,3)
and
B_{1}=B(0,1)
are balls in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
). Let
\{\psi_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1}^{N}
be a partition of unity subordinated to the covering
\{\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{1})\}_{\alpha=1}^{N}
of
\mathcal{M}
.

Given
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
, write
f=\sum_{\alpha}{\psi_{\alpha}f}
. Note that each of the functions
(\psi_{\alpha}f)\circ\varphi_{\alpha}
has compact support
in
B_{1}
.
Note also that

\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})\times[0,\infty)\ni(x,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Xi_{\alpha}}}{{\longmapsto}}(\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}(x),t)\in B_{2}\times[0,\infty)

is a diffeomorphism with bounded derivatives.
Recall that the extension operator
\operatorname{ext}_{1}
is defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by (
2.33
) with
R=1
.
Then define the extension

\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f(x,t)=\sum_{\alpha:\,x\in\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})}(\operatorname{ext}_{1}((\psi_{\alpha}f)\circ\varphi_{\alpha}))\circ\Xi_{\alpha}(x,t):=\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{ext}_{\alpha}f,

where

(2.37)

\operatorname{ext}_{\alpha}f(x,t)=\begin{cases}(\operatorname{ext}_{1}((\psi_{\alpha}f)\circ\varphi_{\alpha}))\circ\Xi_{\alpha}(x,t)&\text{if $x\in\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})$,}\\
0&\text{if $x\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})$.}\end{cases}

The diffeomorphism
\Xi_{\alpha}
maps
\partial(\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2}))\times[0,\infty)
onto
\partial B_{2}\times[0,\infty)
and
\operatorname{ext}_{1}((\psi_{\alpha}f)\circ\varphi_{\alpha})
equals zero in a neighborhood of

\partial B_{2}\times[0,\infty)
so the expression in the first line of (
2.37
) equals zero for
x
near
\partial(\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2}))
and so the second line of (
2.21
) defines a smooth extension beyond

\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})
.

Evidently,
\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f
is continuous on
\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]
, agrees with
f
on
\mathcal{M}\times\{0\}
, vanishes in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{M}\times\{1\}
and is
smooth in
\mathcal{M}\times(0,1)
.

Note that
(\psi_{\alpha}f)\circ\varphi_{\alpha}\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(B_{1})
has compact support in
B_{1}
and

(2.38)

[(\psi_{\alpha}f)\circ\varphi_{\alpha}]_{\gamma}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}.

Indeed, for
u,v\in\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{1})
we have

\begin{split}|\psi_{\alpha}(u)f(u)-\psi_{\alpha}(v)f(v)|&\leq\|f\|_{\infty}|\psi_{\alpha}(u)-\psi_{\alpha}(v)|+\|\psi_{\alpha}\|_{\infty}|f(u)-f(v)|\\
&\lesssim\ (\|f\|_{\infty}+[f]_{\gamma})d(u,v)^{\gamma}\end{split}

and (
2.38
) follows, because
d(\varphi_{\alpha}(x),\varphi_{\alpha}(y))
is comparable to
|x-y|

for
x,y\in B_{1}
.
Hence
\operatorname{ext}_{1}((\psi_{\alpha}f)\circ\varphi_{\alpha})
has support inside
B_{2}\times[0,1)
and Lemma
2.52
in concert with (
2.38
) yields

(2.39)

[\operatorname{ext}_{1}((\psi_{\alpha}f)\circ\varphi_{\alpha})]_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+})}+[\operatorname{ext}_{1}((\psi_{\alpha}f)\circ\varphi_{\alpha})]_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+})}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}.

Since
\Xi_{\alpha}
is a diffeomorphism with bounded derivatives, it follows that

[\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f]_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M}\times[0,1])}+[\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f]_{W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M}\times[0,1])}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}.

We still need to estimate
\|\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f\|_{\infty}
and
\|\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f\|_{p}
.
For
0\leq t\leq 1
we have

\begin{split}|(\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f)(x,t)|&\leq|(\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f)(x,t)-(\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f)(x,0)|+|f(x)|\\
&\leq[\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f]_{\gamma}+\|f\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}.\end{split}

Therefore,
\|\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f\|_{p}\lesssim\|\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}
.
This estimate along with (
2.39
) completes the proof.
∎

Using similar arguments we can also construct an extension operator for forms.

Corollary 2.56
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an oriented, compact,
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary and let
1<p<\infty
and
0\leq k\leq n
. Then there is a bounded linear extension operator

\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}:\Omega^{k}W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\mathcal{M})\to\Omega^{k}W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M}\times[0,1])

such that the extension
\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}\kappa
of any
\kappa\in W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}
, is
C^{\infty}
smooth in
\mathcal{M}\times(0,1)
, and equal zero in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{M}\times\{1\}
.
Moreover, if

1\geq\gamma>1-\frac{1}{p}
and
0\leq k\leq n
, then

\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}:\Omega^{k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})\to\Omega^{k}(W^{1,p}\cap C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma})((\mathcal{M}\times[0,1])).

Proof.

Let
\{\varphi_{\alpha}\}
,
\{\psi_{\alpha}\}
and
\operatorname{ext}_{\alpha}

be as in the proof of Proposition
2.53
(see Remark
2.54
). Then

\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}=(x_{1}^{\alpha},\ldots,x_{n}^{\alpha})
is a coordinate system in
\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})\subset\mathcal{M}
. This coordinate system extends to the coordinate system

\Xi_{\alpha}(x,t)=(\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}(x),t)=(x_{1}^{\alpha},\ldots,x_{n}^{\alpha},t)\qquad\text{on}\quad\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})\times[0,\infty).

In particular,
dx_{I}^{\alpha}
can be regarded as a differential form on
\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})\subset\mathcal{M}
as well as a form on
\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})\times[0,\infty)\subset\mathcal{M}\times[0,\infty)
.

If
\kappa
is a
k
-form on
\mathcal{M}
, then

\kappa=\sum_{\alpha}\psi_{\alpha}\kappa
. Since
\psi_{\alpha}\kappa
has compact support in
\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{1})
, it has a unique representation as

\psi_{\alpha}\kappa=\sum_{|I|=k}\kappa_{I}^{\alpha}\,dx_{I}^{\alpha},

where the coefficients
\kappa_{I}^{\alpha}
are functions with the same regularity as
\kappa
, compactly supported in
\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{1})
. Now we define the extension as

\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}\kappa=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{|I|=k}\operatorname{ext}_{\alpha}(\kappa_{I}^{\alpha})\,dx_{I}^{\alpha},

where
dx_{I}^{\alpha}
are regarded as differential forms on
\mathcal{M}\times[0,\infty)
.
Since
\operatorname{ext}_{\alpha}(\kappa_{I}^{\alpha})(x,t)
vanishes if
x\not\in\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})
,

\operatorname{ext}_{\alpha}(\kappa_{I}^{\alpha})\,dx_{I}^{\alpha}
is a globally defined form on
\mathcal{M}\times[0,\infty)
, that is equal zero if
x\not\in\varphi_{\alpha}(B_{2})
.
∎

3.
Distributional Jacobian

If functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
, belong to the fractional Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
0<s<1
,
1<p<\infty
, the pointwise Jacobian
df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}
is not well defined, but we may try to define the
distributional Jacobian
acting on
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as the limit

\langle\varphi,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi\,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{R^{n}}\varphi\,df_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}^{\varepsilon},

where
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni f_{i}^{\varepsilon}\to f_{i}
in
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
However, it is not obvious under what assumptions about
s
and
p
, the limit exists and is independent of the choice of a smooth approximation.

Although, we denoted the limit as the integral
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi\,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}
(assuming it exists and is independent of the choice of a smooth approximation), we need to understand that it is only a formal notation for the distribution acting on the test function
\varphi
, the distribution that is defined as a limit of the true integrals; notation

\langle\varphi,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}\rangle
is however, a more standard way to denote evaluation of a distribution on a test function.

In this section we will explore the above idea
in a more general context of differential forms. Our results will generalize some of the results of Brezis and Nguyen
[
5
, Theorem 3 and Corollary 1]
and of Züst
[
75
, Theorem 3.2]
,
[
74
]
. See also
[
16
]
, and
[
66
,
49
]
for corresponding results in the abstract harmonic analysis.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and closed
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold. Let

\lambda\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}(\mathcal{M})\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{i}\in\Omega^{\ell_{i}-1}W^{1-\frac{1}{p_{i}},p_{i}}\left(\mathcal{M}\right),\ i=1,2,\ldots,k,

where
\ell\geq 0
,
\ell_{i}\geq 1
,
1<q,p_{i}<\infty
,
\ell+\ell_{1}+\ldots+\ell_{k}=n
, and

(3.1)

\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\ldots+\frac{1}{p_{k}}=1.

Assume we have approximations

\Omega^{\ell}W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\ni\lambda^{\varepsilon}\to\lambda\ \text{in $W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}$}\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega^{\ell_{i}-1}\operatorname{Lip}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\ni\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\to\gamma_{i}\ \text{in $W^{1-\frac{1}{p_{i}},p_{i}}$}

as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then the limit

(3.2)

\langle\lambda,d\gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge d\gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}

exists and it does not depend on a choice of approximations
\lambda^{\varepsilon}
,
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Existence of a smooth (and hence Lipschitz) approximation
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\to\gamma_{i}
follows from Remark
2.44
. Existence of an approximation
\lambda^{\varepsilon}\to\lambda
is obvious, because we can take
\lambda^{\varepsilon}=\lambda
. Note that in this case we have

\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge d\gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge d\gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}.

Proof.

Let
0\leq m\leq n
be an integer and let

\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}:\Omega^{m}W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\to\Omega^{m}W^{1,p}\big(\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]\big)

be the extension operator from Corollary
2.56
. If
m=0
, then this is an extension operator on functions.
Recall that
\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}\kappa
is
C^{\infty}
on
\mathcal{M}\times(0,1)
, it vanishes in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{M}\times\{1\}
and it is smooth up to
\mathcal{M}\times\{0\}
, if
\kappa\in C^{\infty}
. Moreover,
\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}\kappa
is defined by the same formula for all
p
, but of course, it depends on
m
.

Let
\lambda^{\varepsilon,\delta}\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
,
\lambda^{\varepsilon,\delta}\to\lambda^{\varepsilon}
in
W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}
as
\delta\to 0
.
Since
\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal{M})\subset W^{1,p_{i}}(\mathcal{M})
, we can find a smooth approximation

\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\in\Omega^{\ell_{i}-1}(\mathcal{M})
,
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\to\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
in
W^{1,p_{i}}
. Since
W^{1,p_{i}}(\mathcal{M})\subset W^{1-\frac{1}{p_{i}},p_{i}}(\mathcal{M})
(see e.g.
[
31
, Proposition 28]
), we also have that
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\to\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
in
W^{1-\frac{1}{p_{i}},p_{i}}(\mathcal{M})
.

Let
\Lambda=\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda\in W^{1,q}
,
\Gamma_{i}=\operatorname{ext}_{\mathcal{M}}\gamma_{i}\in W^{1,p_{i}}

and let
\Lambda^{\varepsilon}
,
\Lambda^{\varepsilon,\delta}
,
\Gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
and
\Gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}
be the extensions of
\lambda^{\varepsilon},\lambda^{\varepsilon,\delta}
,
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
and
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}
respectively. Since the extension operator is continuous, we have

\Lambda^{\varepsilon,\delta}\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}\Lambda^{\varepsilon},\quad\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\Lambda\ \text{in $W^{1,q}$}\quad\text{and}\quad\Gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\Gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon},\quad\Gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\Gamma_{i}\ \text{in $W^{1,p_{i}}$}.

Observe that

\begin{split}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda^{\varepsilon,\delta}\wedge d\gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon,\delta}&=\int_{\partial(\mathcal{M}\times[0,1])}\Lambda^{\varepsilon,\delta}\wedge d\Gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\\
&=\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}d\Lambda^{\varepsilon,\delta}\wedge d\Gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon,\delta}.\end{split}

The first equality follows from the fact that the extension of smooth forms is smooth up to the boundary and that it
vanishes on
\mathcal{M}\times\{1\}
, which is a part of the boundary of
\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]
.
The second equality is the Stokes theorem.

Letting
\delta\to 0
we obtain

(3.3)

\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}=\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}d\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\Gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}.

Indeed,

\lambda^{\varepsilon,\delta}\to\lambda^{\varepsilon}\text{ in }L^{q},\quad d\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\to d\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\text{ in }L^{p_{i}},\quad d\Lambda^{\varepsilon,\delta}\to d\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\text{ in }L^{q},\quad d\Gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\to d\Gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\text{ in }L^{p_{i}},

so (
3.3
) is a consequence of (
3.1
) and Hölder’s inequality, just like in (
2.30
).
Now the same argument shows that

(3.4)

\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}=\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}d\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\Gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}d\Lambda\wedge d\Gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\Gamma_{k}.

We proved existence of the limit (
3.2
). It is independent of the approximation, because the right hand side of (
3.4
) does not depend on the approximation.
∎

In the case in which
p_{i}=p
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,k
, we have
p>k
and
q=\frac{p}{p-k}
. In that case we get

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and closed
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold. Let

\lambda,\tilde{\lambda}\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\in\Omega^{\ell_{i}-1}W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}\left(\mathcal{M}\right),\ i=1,2,\ldots,k

where
\ell\geq 0
,
\ell_{i}\geq 1
,
\ell+\ell_{1}+\ldots+\ell_{k}=n
,

1\leq k<p<\infty
,
q=\frac{p}{p-k}
. Then
the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
are satisfied with
p_{i}=p
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\left|\,\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge d\gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}-\int_{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{\lambda}\wedge d\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{\gamma}_{k}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}}\|\gamma\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}+\|\tilde{\lambda}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}}\|\gamma-\tilde{\gamma}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}\right)\left(\|\gamma\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}+\|\tilde{\gamma}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}\right)^{k-1},

where
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})
and
\tilde{\gamma}=(\tilde{\gamma}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\gamma}_{k})
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda^{\varepsilon},\tilde{\lambda}^{\varepsilon},\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\varepsilon}
be smooth approximations and let

\Lambda^{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon},\Gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Gamma}_{i}^{\varepsilon}
be their extensions to
\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]
as in the proof of Theorem
3.1
. Also let
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}=(\Gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon},\ldots,\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon})
and

\tilde{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}=(\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}^{\varepsilon},\ldots,\tilde{\Gamma}_{k}^{\varepsilon})
. We have

\displaystyle d\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\Gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle-d\tilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{\Gamma}_{k}^{\varepsilon}=d(\Lambda^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon})\wedge d\Gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k}d\tilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{\Gamma}_{j-1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge d(\Gamma_{j}^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\Gamma}_{j}^{\varepsilon})\wedge d{\Gamma}_{j+1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d{\Gamma}_{k}^{\varepsilon}.

Therefore,

(3.5)

\begin{split}&|d\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\Gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}-d\tilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{\Gamma}_{k}^{\varepsilon}|\\
&\lesssim|D(\Lambda^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon})|\,|D\Gamma^{\varepsilon}|^{k}+|D\tilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon}|\,|D(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon})|(|D\Gamma^{\varepsilon}|+|D\tilde{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}|)^{k-1}.\end{split}

Integration, Stokes’ theorem, and Hölder’s inequality yield

\displaystyle\left|\,\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}-\tilde{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\tilde{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}_{k}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\,\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}d\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\Gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}-d\tilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{\Gamma}_{k}^{\varepsilon}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\,\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}|D(\Lambda^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon})|^{q}\right)^{1/q}\left(\,\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}|D\Gamma^{\varepsilon}|^{p}\right)^{k/p}

\displaystyle+\left(\,\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}|D\tilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon}|^{q}\right)^{1/q}\left(\,\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}|D(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon})|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\left(\,\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}|D\Gamma^{\varepsilon}|^{p}+|D\tilde{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}|^{p}\right)^{(k-1)/p}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\lambda^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}}\|\gamma^{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}^{k}+\|\tilde{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}}\|\gamma^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}\left(\|\gamma^{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}+\|\tilde{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}\right)^{k-1}

and the result follows upon passing to the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and closed
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold. Let

\lambda\in\Omega^{k}W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}\left(\mathcal{M}\right),

where
k,\ell\geq 0
,
k+\ell=n-1
,
1<p,q<\infty
and
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
. Then

\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\lambda\wedge\gamma=(-1)^{k+1}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge d\gamma.

Proof.

Under the given assumptions the generalized integrals, defined as limits of integrals of smooth approximations, exist. Since

0=\int_{\mathcal{M}}d(\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge\gamma^{\varepsilon})=\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge\gamma^{\varepsilon}+(-1)^{k}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon},

the result follows upon passing to the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

Several results stated below are straightforward consequences of Theorems
3.1
and
3.3
. However, we state them explicitly to provide ‘ready to use’ tools.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and closed
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold. Let

f\in W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}(\mathcal{M}),\ g_{i}\in W^{1-\frac{1}{p_{i}},p_{i}}(\mathcal{M}),\quad 1<q,p_{i}<\infty,\ i=1,2,\ldots,n,

where

(3.6)

\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\ldots+\frac{1}{p_{n}}=1.

If

W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}(\mathcal{M})\ni f^{\varepsilon}\to f
in
W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}

and

\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal{M})\ni g_{i}^{\varepsilon}\to g_{i}
in
W^{1-\frac{1}{p_{i}},p_{i}}

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, then the limit

\langle f,dg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}fdg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{\varepsilon}\,dg_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}^{\varepsilon}

exists and it does not depend on the choice of approximations
f^{\varepsilon}
,
g_{i}^{\varepsilon}
.

Remark 3.6
.

In this particular instance of Theorem
3.1
,
f
and
g_{i}
are functions, so
dg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}
is a generalized distributional Jacobian of a mapping
g=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}):\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
acting on a function
f
.

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and closed
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold. Let

f,\tilde{f}\in W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}(\mathcal{M}),\ g,\tilde{g}\in W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\mathcal{M})^{n},\quad n<p<\infty,\ q=\frac{p}{p-n}.

Then assumptions of Corollary
3.5
are satisfied with
p_{i}=p
and the distributional Jacobains

dg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}
and
d\tilde{g}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{g}_{n}
exist. Moreover

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{M}}fdg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}-\int_{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{f}\tilde{g}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{g}_{n}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}}\|g\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}}\|g-\tilde{g}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}\right)\left(\|g\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}+\|\tilde{g}\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}}\right)^{n-1}.

Since
C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}\subset W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}
for
1-\frac{1}{p}<\alpha\leq 1
(by Corollary
2.47
), we can replace the fractional Sobolev spaces in
the above results by Hölder spaces. However, since smooth mappings are not dense in the Hölder space, we have to relax the approximation condition. This gives the following result.

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and compact
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with or without boundary. Let

\lambda\in\Omega^{\ell}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{i}\in\Omega^{\ell_{i}-1}C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta_{i}}\left(\mathcal{M}\right),\quad i=1,2,\ldots,k,

where

\ell+\ell_{1}+\ldots+\ell_{k}=n,\ \ell\geq 0,\ \ell_{i}\geq 1,\qquad\alpha+\beta_{1}+\ldots+\beta_{k}>k,\ \alpha,\beta_{i}\in(0,1].

Assume that
0<\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
and
0<\beta_{i}^{\prime}\leq\beta_{i}
are such that

\alpha^{\prime}+\beta_{1}^{\prime}+\ldots+\beta_{k}^{\prime}>k
and that
we have approximations

\Omega^{\ell}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\ni\lambda^{\varepsilon}\to\lambda\ \text{in $C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}$}\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega^{\ell_{i}-1}\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal{M})\ni\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\to\gamma_{i}\ \text{in $C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta_{i}^{\prime}}$}

as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then the limit

(3.7)

\langle\lambda,d\gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge d\gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}

exists and it does not depend on a choice of approximations
\lambda^{\varepsilon}
,
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
.

Remark 3.9
.

If families
(\lambda^{\varepsilon})\subset C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}
and
(\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon})\subset\operatorname{Lip}
,
i=1,2,\ldots,n
are bounded in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}
and
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta_{i}}
respectively, and

\lambda^{\varepsilon}\rightrightarrows\lambda,\quad\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\rightrightarrows\gamma_{i},\ i=1,2,\ldots,n,\quad\text{uniformly as $\varepsilon\to 0$,}

then according to Corollary
2.21
the assumptions of Theorem
3.8
are satisfied and hence (
3.7
) holds true. Existence of such a family
(\lambda^{\varepsilon})
is obvious (take
\lambda^{\varepsilon}=\lambda
) and the existence of families
(\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon})
is guaranteed by Corollary
2.34
and Remark
2.36
.

Remark 3.10
.

In Theorem
3.1
we assumed that
\mathcal{M}
has no boundary, but in Theorem
3.8
we allow
\mathcal{M}
to have boundary. This is because the assumptions in Theorem
3.8
are stronger: if forms are Hölder continuous on
\mathcal{M}
, then their restrictions to the boundary are Hölder continuous too and we can apply the extension operator to the restrictions of the forms to the boundary.

Proof.

Under the given assumptions we can find
1<q,p_{i}<\infty
such that

\alpha^{\prime}>1-\frac{1}{q},\quad\beta_{i}^{\prime}>1-\frac{1}{p_{i}},\quad\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\ldots+\frac{1}{p_{k}}=1.

Assume first that
\partial\mathcal{M}=\varnothing
.
Since by Corollary
2.47
,

C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}\subset W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}
,
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta_{i}^{\prime}}\subset W^{1-\frac{1}{p_{i}},p_{i}}
, the result follows from Theorem
3.1
.

Assume now that
\partial\mathcal{M}\neq\varnothing
. The proof in this case is elementary yet delicate.

Using the collar neighborhood theorem, we can
construct a smooth manifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
without boundary, by gluing two copies of
\mathcal{M}
along the boundary.
A neighborhood of the boundary of the ‘twin manifold’
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}
, is diffeomorphic to
\partial\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]
, where
\partial\mathcal{M}\times\{0\}
corresponds to
\partial\mathcal{M}
and
\partial\mathcal{M}\times\{1\}
is in the interior of the twin manifold. By writing
\partial\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]
in the proof below, we will always refer to this neighborhood.

If
(x,U)
,
x(U)=\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}
, is a good coordinate system on
\mathcal{M}
near a boundary point in
\partial\mathcal{M}
, then the coefficients of the forms
\lambda
,
\lambda^{\varepsilon}
,
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
expressed in this coordinate system are Hölder and Lipschitz continuous on

\mathbb{B}^{n-1}=\{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}:\,x_{n}=0\}.

They are Hölder continuous with exponents
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta_{i}^{\prime}
and coefficients of
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
are Lipschitz continuous. This includes coefficients of components of the forms that involve
dx_{n}
.

An obvious modification of the proof of Corollary
2.56
that takes into account extension of coefficients involving
dx_{n}
, gives us an extension of the forms
\lambda
,
\lambda^{\varepsilon}
and
\gamma_{i}
,
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
to the forms (denoted by the same symbols) such that

\lambda,\lambda^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega^{\ell}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}),\qquad\gamma_{i},\in\Omega^{\ell_{i}-1}C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta_{i}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}),\quad\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega^{\ell_{i}-1}\operatorname{Lip}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}),

and

\lambda,\lambda^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,q}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}),\qquad\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega^{\ell_{i}-1}W^{1,p_{i}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}).

While the proof of Corollary
2.56
extends the forms to
\partial\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]
only, the forms vanish near
\partial\mathcal{M}\times\{1\}
, so we assume that the forms
\lambda
,
\lambda^{\varepsilon}
,
\gamma_{i}
,
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}

are equal zero beyond
\partial\mathcal{M}\times\{1\}
in the twin manifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}
.

Note that if
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
is
C^{\infty}
on
\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, then the extension of
\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
is Lipschitz continuous only. While it is
C^{\infty}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\partial\mathcal{M}
, smoothness at the points of
\partial\mathcal{M}
is not guaranteed – extending smooth functions from a half-space to the whole space is not that easy (see e.g.
[
65
]
).

Since the extension operator is bounded, it follows that

(3.8)

\lambda^{\varepsilon}\to\lambda\text{ in }W^{1,q}\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\to\gamma_{i}\text{ in }W^{1,p_{i}}\quad\text{ on }\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}.

Since the extension operator is bounded in the Hölder norms
C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}
and
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta_{i}^{\prime}}
, convergence in the Hölder norms on
\mathcal{M}
implies that

\lambda^{\varepsilon}\to\lambda\text{ in }C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\to\gamma_{i}\text{ in }C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta_{i}^{\prime}}\quad\text{ on }\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

and hence

\lambda^{\varepsilon}\to\lambda\text{ in }W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Lip}\ni\gamma_{i}^{\varepsilon}\to\gamma_{i}\text{ in }W^{1-\frac{1}{p_{i}},p_{i}}\quad\text{ on }\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

Therefore, Theorem
3.1
yields that the limit

(3.9)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}

exists and is independent of the choice of the original approximations on
\mathcal{M}
.
Note that the integral

\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge d\gamma_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\gamma_{k}

is defined in the classical sense, because the forms belong to suitable Sobolev spaces.
Thus, to prove existence of the limit (
3.7
) and its independence of approximations, it suffices to show that

(3.10)

\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge d\gamma_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\gamma_{k}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}}\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k},

because then, we will have that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}\lambda^{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}-\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge d\gamma_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\gamma_{k}.

The proof of (
3.10
) uses (
3.8
) and is in fact,
very similar to that of (
2.30
). We leave details to the reader; they may find some additional details in the last part of the proof of Corollary
3.11
, where we estimated differences of integrals over
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}
.
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and compact
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with or without boundary. Let

\lambda,\tilde{\lambda}\in\Omega^{\ell}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\in\Omega^{\ell_{i}-1}C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}\left(\mathcal{M}\right),

where

\ell+\ell_{1}+\ldots+\ell_{k}=n,\ \ell\geq 0,\ \ell_{i}\geq 1,\qquad\alpha+k\beta>k,\ \alpha,\beta\in(0,1].

Then the assumptions of Theorem
3.8
are satisfied with
\beta_{i}=\beta
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\left|\,\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge d\gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}-\int_{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{\lambda}\wedge d\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{\gamma}_{k}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}\|\gamma\|_{C^{0,\beta}}+\|\tilde{\lambda}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}\|\gamma-\tilde{\gamma}\|_{C^{0,\beta}}\right)\left(\|\gamma\|_{C^{0,\beta}}+\|\tilde{\gamma}\|_{C^{0,\beta}}\right)^{k-1},

where
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})
and
\tilde{\gamma}=(\tilde{\gamma}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\gamma}_{k})
.

Proof.

We will use notation from the proof of Theorem
3.8
. Assume that

\alpha\geq\alpha^{\prime}>1-\frac{1}{q},\qquad\beta\geq\beta^{\prime}>1-\frac{1}{p},\qquad\frac{1}{q}+\frac{k}{p}=1,

and assume that the generalized integrals are defined through approximations in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}
and
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta^{\prime}}
. Therefore, we can assume that the forms
\gamma
and
\tilde{\gamma}
are Lipschitz continuous.

The triangle inequality

\Bigg|\int_{\mathcal{M}}\heartsuit-\int_{\mathcal{M}}\diamondsuit\,\Bigg|\leq\Bigg|\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}\heartsuit-\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}\diamondsuit\,\Bigg|+\Bigg|\int_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}\setminus\mathcal{M}}\heartsuit-\int_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}\setminus\mathcal{M}}\diamondsuit\,\Bigg|

allows us to estimate differences of the integrals over
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}
separately.

Since the extension operator constructed in the proof of Theorem
3.8
is bounded in the Hölder norms,

C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}\subset W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q},\ C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta^{\prime}}\subset W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\cdot\|_{C^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}}\lesssim\|\cdot\|_{C^{0,\alpha}},\ \|\cdot\|_{C^{0,\beta^{\prime}}}\lesssim\|\cdot\|_{C^{0,\beta}},

we have that

\|\lambda\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{q},q}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}})}\lesssim\|\lambda\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathcal{M})},\qquad\|\gamma\|_{W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}})}\lesssim\|\gamma\|_{C^{0,\beta}(\mathcal{M})}

and similar estimates for
\tilde{\lambda}
and
\tilde{\gamma}
. Now the estimate for the difference of integrals over
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
follows from Theorem
3.3
.

To estimate the difference of integrals over
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}
we use the inequality similar to (
3.5
)

\begin{split}&|\lambda\wedge d\gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}-\tilde{\lambda}\wedge d\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{\gamma}_{k}|\\
&\leq|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}|\,|d\gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}|+|\tilde{\lambda}|\,|d\gamma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\gamma_{k}-d\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{\gamma}_{k}|\\
&\lesssim|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}|\,|D\gamma|^{k}+|\tilde{\lambda}|\,|D(\gamma-\tilde{\gamma})|(|D\gamma|+|D\tilde{\gamma}|)^{k-1}.\end{split}

Next, we integrate this inequality over
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}
and estimate the right hand side with the help of Hölder’s inequality

\begin{split}\Bigg|\int_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}\setminus\mathcal{M}}\heartsuit-\int_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}\setminus\mathcal{M}}\diamondsuit\,\Bigg|&\lesssim\|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}\|_{q}\|D\gamma\|_{p}^{k}+\|\tilde{\lambda}\|_{q}\|D(\gamma-\tilde{\gamma})\|_{p}(\|D\gamma\|_{p}+\|D\tilde{\gamma}\|_{p})^{k-1}\\
&\leq(\|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}\|_{q}\|D\gamma\|_{p}+\|\tilde{\lambda}\|_{q}\|D(\gamma-\tilde{\gamma})\|_{p})(\|D\gamma\|_{p}+\|D\tilde{\gamma}\|_{p})^{k-1}.\end{split}

Here the norms on the right hand side are computed over
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}
.
Now the desired estimate in the Hölder norm on
\mathcal{M}
follows from the fact that the extensions
\lambda
,
\tilde{\lambda}
and
\gamma
,
\tilde{\gamma}
from the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}
are bounded operators from
C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}
and
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta^{\prime}}
to
W^{1,q}
and
W^{1,p}
respectively. It remains only to observe that

\|\cdot\|_{C^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}(\partial\mathcal{M})}\leq\|\cdot\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathcal{M})}
and similarly for
\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta
.
∎

Let us now state a special case of the result that will be useful in the applications.

Corollary 3.12
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with Lipschitz boundary.
Let

f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}(\Omega)
,

g,\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}(\Omega)^{n}
,
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1]
,

\alpha+n\beta>n
.

If
\alpha^{\prime}\in(0,\alpha]
,
\beta^{\prime}\in(0,\beta]
,
\alpha^{\prime}+n\beta^{\prime}>n
and

(3.11)

C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}(\Omega)\ni f^{\varepsilon}\to f
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}

and

\operatorname{Lip}({\Omega})^{n}\ni g^{\varepsilon}\to g
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta_{i}^{\prime}}

as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

then the limit

(3.12)

\langle f,dg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}\rangle=\int_{\Omega}fdg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}f^{\varepsilon}\,dg_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}^{\varepsilon}

exists and it does not depend on the choice of approximations
f^{\varepsilon}
,
g^{\varepsilon}
.

Moreover, if
f,\tilde{f}\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}(\Omega)
,

g,\tilde{g}\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}(\Omega)^{n}
,
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1]
,

\alpha+n\beta>n
, then

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega}fdg_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dg_{n}-\int_{\Omega}\tilde{f}\tilde{g}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{g}_{n}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}\|g\|_{C^{0,\beta}}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}\|g-\tilde{g}\|_{C^{0,\beta}}\right)\left(\|g\|_{C^{0,\beta}}+\|\tilde{g}\|_{C^{0,\beta}}\right)^{n-1}.

Proof.

While in Theorem
3.8
and Corollary
3.11
we assume that the boundary of
\mathcal{M}
is smooth, the proof can easily be adapted to the case of bounded domains with Lipschitz boundary. A crucial step in the proof was an extension of forms from
\mathcal{M}
to
\partial\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
with the help of Corollary
2.56
.

Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a ball such that
\Omega\Subset B
. Since
\Omega
at its boundary points is locally bi-Lipschitz equivalent to
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
, and both
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}
and
W^{1,p}
spaces are invariant under a bi-Lipschitz change of variables,
an obvious modification of the proof of Proposition
2.53
allows us to extend the
functions
f
,
f^{\varepsilon}
and
g
,
g^{\varepsilon}
from
\Omega
(in fact, from
\partial\Omega)

to compactly supported functions in
B
and
B
can be embedded into an oriented and closed manifold. The remainder of the proof follows arguments used in the proofs of Theorem
3.8
and Corollary
3.11
.
∎

3.1.
The one dimensional case

The next result is a straightforward consequence of Corollary
3.12
. However, since it will play a very important role in the study of the Heisenberg group, we prefer to give an elementary and direct proof.

Recall that the existence of a smooth approximation
f^{\varepsilon}
and
g^{\varepsilon}
in the statement of Proposition
3.13
is guaranteed by Proposition
2.32
. Indeed, we can always assume that
0<\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha
and
0<\beta^{\prime}<\beta
.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}([a,b])
,
g\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}([a,b])
,
0<\alpha,\beta\leq 1
,
\alpha+\beta>1
.
Let
0<\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
,
0<\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta
be such that
\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}>1
.
If

\operatorname{Lip}([a,b])\ni f^{\varepsilon}\to f
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}([a,b])

and

\operatorname{Lip}([a,b])\ni g^{\varepsilon}\to g
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta^{\prime}}([a,b])
,

then the limit

(3.13)

\int_{a}^{b}f\,dg:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{a}^{b}f^{\varepsilon}\,dg^{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{a}^{b}f^{\varepsilon}(x)(g^{\varepsilon})^{\prime}(x)\,dx

exists and it does not depend on the choice of the approximation. Moreover, if

\tilde{f}\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}([a,b])
,
\tilde{g}\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta}([a,b])
, then

(3.14)

\left|\int_{a}^{b}f\,dg-\int_{a}^{b}\tilde{f}\,d\tilde{g}\right|\lesssim\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}[g]_{\beta}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}[g-\tilde{g}]_{\beta}.

Remark 3.14
.

This result is essentially due to Young
[
73
]
who defined the integral
\int_{a}^{b}f\,dg
of Hölder continuous functions (under assumptions of Proposition
3.13
) as the Stieltjes integral. See also
[
74
,
75
]
for extension of Young’s approach to higher dimensional integrals. However, our approach is different.

We will precede the proof by presenting some auxiliary results.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1]
,
\alpha+\beta>1
. If
f\in C^{0,\alpha}([a,b])
,
g\in\operatorname{Lip}([a,b])
,
f(a)=f(b)
and
g(a)=g(b)
, then

\Big|\int_{a}^{b}f(x)g^{\prime}(x)\,dx\Big|\leq C(\alpha,\beta)|b-a|^{\alpha+\beta}\,[f]_{\alpha}\,[g]_{\beta}.

Proof.

Our proof will be based on elementary estimates of Fourier series.
By a linear change of variables we may assume that
[a,b]=[0,1]
.
Since
f(0)=f(1)
and
g(0)=g(1)
, we can extend
f
and
g
to
1
-periodic functions on
\mathbb{R}
. Note that

(3.15)

[f]_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim[f]_{C^{0,\alpha}([0,1])},

but we do not necessarily have equality, because in the periodic extension of
f
we have to glue
f
near
0
with
f
near
1
. Similarly
[g]_{C^{0,\beta}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim[g]_{C^{0,\beta}([0,1])}
.
In what follows
[\,\cdot\,]_{\alpha}
and
[\,\cdot\,]_{\beta}
will stand for
[\,\cdot\,]_{C^{0,\alpha}([0,1])}
and
[\,\cdot\,]_{C^{0,\beta}([0,1])}
respectively.

Recall that the Fourier coefficients of
f
are defined by

\hat{f}(n)=\int_{0}^{1}f(x)e^{-2\pi inx}\,dx
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For
\tau>0
, let
f_{\tau}(x):=f(x+\tau)
, so

\hat{f}_{\tau}(n)=\int_{0}^{1}f(x+\tau)e^{-2\pi inx}\,dx=\int_{0}^{1}f(x)e^{-2\pi in(x-\tau)}\,dx=e^{2\pi in\tau}\hat{f}(n).

Therefore,

(f-f_{\tau})^{\wedge}(n)=\hat{f}(n)(1-e^{2\pi in\tau})
,
and Parseval’s identity yields

(3.16)

\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}|\hat{f}(n)|^{2}|1-e^{2\pi in\tau}|^{2}=\|f-f_{\tau}\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,1])}\lesssim[f]^{2}_{\alpha}\,\tau^{2\alpha}.

Note that we have here inequality
\lesssim
instead of
\leq
, because of (
3.15
).

Our aim is to use (
3.16
) to obtain a good upper bound for
|\hat{f}(n)|^{2}
. To this end we want to select
\tau
so that
|1-e^{2\pi in\tau}|\geq 1
. Such
\tau
has to depend on
n
and we can get an estimate only for
n
being in a certain range like
2^{k}\leq|n|<2^{k+1}
.

Fix an integer
k\geq 0
and let
\tau=2^{-(k+2)}
. If
2^{k}\leq n<2^{k+1}
, then

\pi/2\leq 2\pi n\tau<\pi
.
If
2^{k}\leq-n<2^{k+1}
, then
-\pi<2\pi n\tau\leq-\pi/2
. Therefore,

\operatorname{Re}e^{2\pi in\tau}\leq 0,\quad\text{and hence}\quad|1-e^{2\pi in\tau}|>1,\quad\text{when $2^{k}\leq|n|<2^{k+1}$}.

This and (
3.16
) yield

(3.17)

\sum_{2^{k}\leq|n|<2^{k+1}}|\hat{f}(n)|^{2}\leq\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}|\hat{f}(n)|^{2}|1-e^{2\pi in\tau}|^{2}\lesssim[f]^{2}_{\alpha}\,\tau^{2\alpha}\approx[f]^{2}_{\alpha}\,2^{-2k\alpha}.

Similarly,

(3.18)

\sum_{2^{k}\leq|n|<2^{k+1}}|\hat{g}(n)|^{2}\lesssim[g]^{2}_{\beta}\,2^{-2k\beta}.

Recall that
\hat{g^{\prime}}(n)=2\pi in\hat{g}(n)
. Therefore, Parseval’s identity yields

\begin{split}\Bigg|\int_{0}^{1}f(x)g^{\prime}(x)\,dx\Bigg|&\lesssim\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}|n|\,|\hat{f}(n)|\,|\hat{g}(n)|\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{k}\sum_{2^{k}\leq|n|<2^{k+1}}|\hat{f}(n)|\,|\hat{g}(n)|\\
&\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{k}[f]_{\alpha}\,2^{-k\alpha}[g]_{\beta}\,2^{-k\beta}\approx[f]_{\alpha}\,[g]_{\beta}.\end{split}

The last inequality is a consequence of (
3.17
), (
3.18
) and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Finally, convergence of the last sum follows from the assumption that
\alpha+\beta>1
.
∎

Corollary 3.16
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1]
,
\alpha+\beta>1
. If
f\in C^{0,\alpha}([a,b])
and
g\in\operatorname{Lip}([a,b])
, then

(3.19)

\Bigg|\int_{a}^{b}f(x)g^{\prime}(x)\,dx\Bigg|\leq C(\alpha,\beta,b-a)\|f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}[g]_{\beta}.

If in addition
f(p)=0
for some
p\in[a,b]
, then

(3.20)

\Bigg|\int_{a}^{b}f(x)g^{\prime}(x)\,dx\Bigg|\leq C(\alpha,\beta)|b-a|^{\alpha+\beta}[f]_{\alpha}\,[g]_{\beta}.

Remark 3.17
.

Note that the dependence on
b-a
in (
3.19
) is not as nice as in (
3.20
). It is due to lack of nice scaling of
\|f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}
with respect to linear transformations.

Proof.

As before, we may assume that
[a,b]=[0,1]
. The values of the constants in the inequalities (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) will follow from a simple linear change of variables.
First, observe that it suffices to prove a seemingly weaker inequality

(3.21)

\Bigg|\int_{0}^{1}f(x)g^{\prime}(x)\,dx\Bigg|\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}\|g\|_{C^{0,\beta}}.

Indeed, if
h\in C^{0,\gamma}([0,1])
and
h(p)=0
for some
p\in[0,1]
, then

\|h\|_{\infty}\leq[h]_{\gamma}\qquad\text{so}\qquad\|h\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\lesssim[h]_{\gamma}.

Thus, (
3.21
) applied to
g(x)-g(0)
yields (
3.19
) and (
3.19
) implies (
3.20
), when
f(p)=0
.

Therefore, if remains to prove (
3.21
).
Let
m:=f(1)-f(0)
and
M=g(1)-g(0)
, so
|m|\leq 2\|f\|_{\infty}
and
|M|\leq 2\|g\|_{\infty}
. Let
\phi(x):=f(x)-mx
and
\psi(x)=g(x)-Mx
. Then
\phi(0)=\phi(1)
,
\psi(0)=\psi(1)
and we may apply Lemma
3.15
to the functions
\phi
and
\psi
. Note that

[\phi]_{\alpha}\leq[f]_{\alpha}+2\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}.

Similarly,
[\psi]_{\beta}\leq 2\|g\|_{C^{0,\beta}}
. We have

\begin{split}&\Bigg|\int_{0}^{1}f(x)g^{\prime}(x)\,dx\Bigg|=\Bigg|\int_{0}^{1}(\phi(x)+mx)(\psi(x)+Mx)^{\prime}\,dx\Bigg|\\
&\leq\Bigg|\int_{0}^{1}\phi(x)\psi^{\prime}(x)\,dx\Bigg|+\Bigg|M\int_{0}^{1}\phi(x)\,dx\Bigg|+\Bigg|m\int_{0}^{1}x\psi^{\prime}(x)\,dx\Bigg|+\Bigg|mM\int_{0}^{1}x\,dx\Bigg|\\
&=A+B+C+D.\end{split}

Since the functions
\phi
and
\psi
satisfy assumptions of Lemma
3.15
, we have

A\lesssim[\phi]_{\alpha}\,[\psi]_{\beta}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}\|g\|_{C^{0,\beta}}.

Clearly,
B+D\lesssim\|f\|_{\infty}\|g\|_{\infty}
. To estimate
C
we use simple integration by parts

C=\Bigg|m\int_{0}^{1}x\psi^{\prime}(x)\,dx\Bigg|=|m|\,\Bigg|\psi(1)-\int_{0}^{1}\psi(x)\,dx\Bigg|\lesssim\|f\|_{\infty}\|g\|_{\infty}.

Adding the estimates for
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
yields the result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.13
.

If
f^{\varepsilon},\tilde{f}^{\varepsilon},g^{\varepsilon},\tilde{g}^{\varepsilon}\in\operatorname{Lip}([a,b])
, then Corollary
3.16
and the traingle inequality

\Bigg|\int_{a}^{b}f^{\varepsilon}(g^{\varepsilon})^{\prime}\,dx-\int_{a}^{b}\tilde{f}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{g}^{\varepsilon})^{\prime}\,dx\Bigg|\leq\Bigg|\int_{a}^{b}(f^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{f}^{\varepsilon})(g^{\varepsilon})^{\prime}\,dx\Bigg|+\Bigg|\int_{a}^{b}\tilde{f}^{\varepsilon}(g^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{g}^{\varepsilon})^{\prime}\,dx\Bigg|

imply that

(3.22)

\Bigg|\int_{a}^{b}f^{\varepsilon}\,dg^{\varepsilon}-\int_{a}^{b}\tilde{f}^{\varepsilon}\,d\tilde{g}^{\varepsilon}\Bigg|\lesssim\|f^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{f}^{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}}[g^{\varepsilon}]_{\beta^{\prime}}+\|\tilde{f}^{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{0,\alpha^{\prime}}}[g^{\varepsilon}-\tilde{g}^{\varepsilon}]_{\beta^{\prime}}.

This inequality implies the existence of limit (
3.13
). Passing to the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
, we see that
(
3.22
) is also true for
f
,
\tilde{f}
,
g
and
\tilde{g}
. Then (
3.14
) is a consequence of
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
and
\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta
.
∎

4.
Pullbacks of differential forms

In Section
3
we discussed generalized Jacobians of Hölder and fractional Sobolev mappings. If
f
is a smooth mappings, then

df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}=f^{*}(dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{n})
, so the Jacobian is a special case of a pullback of a differential form.
In this section we will generalize results of Section
3
to the case of pullbacks of more general forms. While most of the results discussed here can be generalized to mappings in

W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}
, and even to mappings in more general Triebel-Lizorkin or Besov spaces
[
66
]
, for the sake of simplicity we will assume that the mappings are Hölder continuous.
Section
7.4
contains related material.

To simplify notation, we will often write in this section
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}
in place of

\|\cdot\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}
. This should not lead to a confusion with the
L^{p}
-norm, because for the Hölder spaces we will always use Greek letters to denote the exponent of Hölder continuity. Thus, for example, in (
4.1
),
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
is the supremum norm, while other norms are Hölder.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and compact
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with or without boundary. Let
f\in\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{m})
.
Any form
\kappa\in\Omega^{k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
1\leq k\leq n
, can be written as

\kappa=\sum_{|I|=k}\kappa_{I}\,dy_{I},\quad\text{where $\kappa_{I}\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{m})$, so}\quad f^{*}\kappa=\sum_{|I|=k}(\kappa_{I}\circ f)\,df_{I}.

Since in the discussion below, each term can be treated separately, we can assume that

\kappa=\psi\,dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{k},\ \psi\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{m}),\quad\text{and hence}\quad f^{*}\kappa=(\psi\circ f)\,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{k}.

Therefore, for
\tau\in\Omega^{n-k}C^{0}(\mathcal{M})
, we have

\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau=(-1)^{k(n-k)}\int_{\mathcal{M}}(\psi\circ f)\tau\wedge df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{k}.

Assume now that

f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma},\ \frac{k}{k+\alpha}<\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Lip}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{R}^{m})\ni f^{\varepsilon}\to f\ \text{in $C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}$.}

It follows from Proposition
2.19
that for
0<\beta<\alpha
,

(4.1)

\|\psi\circ f-\psi\circ f^{\varepsilon}\|_{\beta\gamma^{\prime}}\lesssim[\psi]_{\alpha}(\|f\|_{\gamma^{\prime}}+\|f^{\varepsilon}\|_{\gamma^{\prime}})^{\beta}\|f-f^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{\alpha-\beta}.

If
\tau\in\Omega^{n-k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
, then
\|\tau\|_{\beta\gamma^{\prime}}\lesssim\|\tau\|_{\alpha\gamma}
and Lemma
2.17
yield

(4.2)

\|(\psi\circ f)\tau-(\psi\circ f^{\varepsilon})\tau\|_{\beta\gamma^{\prime}}\lesssim[\psi]_{\alpha}\|\tau\|_{\alpha\gamma}(\|f\|_{\gamma^{\prime}}+\|f^{\varepsilon}\|_{\gamma^{\prime}})^{\beta}\|f-f^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{\alpha-\beta},

so
(\psi\circ f^{\varepsilon})\tau\to(\psi\circ f)\tau
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\beta\gamma^{\prime}}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

(Inequality (
4.1
) and hence (
4.2
) holds true with
\gamma^{\prime}
replaced by
\gamma
. The proof remains the same.)

If
\xi\in(\frac{k}{k+\alpha},\gamma^{\prime})
, then we can find
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
, such that
\alpha\xi=\beta\gamma^{\prime}
and hence

(\psi\circ f^{\varepsilon})\tau\to(\psi\circ f)\tau\text{ in }C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha\xi}\quad\text{for }\frac{k}{k+\alpha}<\xi<\gamma^{\prime}.

We will apply now Theorem
3.8
(cf. Corollary
3.11
) to

\lambda:=(\psi\circ f)\tau,\ {\lambda^{\varepsilon}}:=(\psi\circ f^{\varepsilon})\tau\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha\xi}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{i},f_{i}^{\varepsilon}\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}

(
f_{i}
plays a role of
\gamma_{i}
).
Since
\alpha\xi+k\gamma^{\prime}>k

(because
\xi,\gamma^{\prime}>\frac{k}{k+\alpha}
), Theorem
3.8
yields the existence of the following limit
and its independence of the choice of a Lipschitz approximation
f^{\varepsilon}
:

\int_{\mathcal{M}}(\psi\circ f)\,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{k}\wedge\tau:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}(\psi\circ f^{\varepsilon})\,df_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{k}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\tau.

We proved the following result:

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and compact
n
-dimensional Riemnnian manifold with or without boundary. Let

f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{m}),\quad\text{where}\quad 1\leq k\leq n,\ k\in\mathbb{N},\ \alpha\in(0,1],\ \frac{k}{k+\alpha}<\gamma\leq 1.

If

\operatorname{Lip}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{R}^{m})\ni f^{\varepsilon}\to f\quad\text{in $C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}$ for some $\frac{k}{k+\alpha}<\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma$,}

then for any

\kappa\in\Omega^{k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\quad\text{and any}\quad\tau\in\Omega^{n-k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha\gamma}\left(\mathcal{M}\right),

the limit

\langle f^{*}\kappa,\tau\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}(f^{\varepsilon})^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau

exists and does not depend of the choice of
\gamma^{\prime}\in\left(\frac{k}{k+\alpha},\gamma\right]
and a Lipschitz approximation
f^{\varepsilon}
.

Remark 4.2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.1
, it follows that if
\kappa=\sum_{|I|=k}\kappa_{I}\,dy_{I}
, then the distributional pullback satisfies

f^{*}\kappa=\sum_{|I|=k}(\kappa_{I}\circ f)\,df_{I}

in a sense that

\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sum_{|I|=k}\int_{\mathcal{M}}(\kappa_{I}\circ f)\,df^{\varepsilon}_{I}\wedge\tau:=\sum_{|I|=k}\int_{\mathcal{M}}(\kappa_{I}\circ f)\,df_{I}\wedge\tau.

To see this, it suffices to slightly modify the proof of Theorem
4.1
and apply Theorem
3.8
to

\lambda^{\varepsilon}=\lambda=(\psi\circ f)\tau
.

The next result is a counterpart of Corollary
3.11
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and compact
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with or without boundary. If

f,\tilde{f}\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{m}),\quad 1\leq k\leq n,\quad\alpha\in(0,1],\quad\frac{k}{k+\alpha}<\gamma\leq 1

and

\kappa\in\Omega^{k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),\qquad\tau\in\Omega^{n-k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha\gamma}\left(\mathcal{M}\right),

then

(4.3)

\Bigg|\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau\Bigg|\lesssim[\kappa]_{\alpha}\|\tau\|_{C^{0,\alpha\gamma}}\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{k+\alpha}.

Moreover,
for any
\beta\in\big(\frac{k(1-\gamma)}{\gamma},\alpha\big)

(4.4)

\Bigg|\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau-\int_{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{f}^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau\Bigg|\lesssim[\kappa]_{\alpha}\|\tau\|_{C^{0,\alpha\gamma}}(\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}})^{k+\beta}\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{\alpha-\beta}.

Remark 4.4
.

It follows from
\gamma>\frac{k}{k+\alpha}
that
\alpha>\frac{k(1-\gamma)}{\gamma}
, so the interval
\big(\frac{k(1-\gamma)}{\gamma},\alpha\big)
is not empty.

Proof.

We will prove (
4.4
) only as (
4.3
) follows directly from (
4.4
).
As before, we may assume that
\kappa=\psi dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{k}
,
\psi\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
.

Since inequality (
4.2
) is true with
\gamma^{\prime}
replaced by
\gamma
, we have

(4.5)

\|(\psi\circ f)\tau-(\psi\circ\tilde{f})\tau\|_{\beta\gamma}\lesssim[\psi]_{\alpha}\|\tau\|_{\alpha\gamma}(\|f\|_{\gamma}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{\gamma})^{\beta}\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{\gamma}^{\alpha-\beta}.

We applied here a rough estimate
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\leq\|\cdot\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}
to the last factor.
In particular, replacing
f
by
0
yields

(4.6)

\|(\psi\circ\tilde{f})\tau\|_{\beta\gamma}\lesssim[\psi]_{\alpha}\|\tau\|_{\alpha\gamma}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\gamma}^{\alpha}.

Let
\beta\in\big(\frac{k(1-\gamma)}{\gamma},\alpha\big)
. It follows that
\delta+k\gamma>k
, where
\delta=\beta\gamma
.

With the obvious change of notation, we can write a special case of Corollary
3.11
as follows:

If
\lambda,\tilde{\lambda}\in\Omega^{n-k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\delta}(\mathcal{M})
and
f_{i},\tilde{f}_{i}\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
,
i=1,2,\ldots,k
, where
\delta+k\gamma>k
, then

(4.7)

\begin{split}&\Bigg|\int_{\mathcal{M}}\lambda\wedge df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{k}-\int_{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{\lambda}\wedge d\tilde{f}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{f}_{k}\Bigg|\\
&\lesssim(\|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}\|_{\delta}\|f\|_{\gamma}+\|\tilde{\lambda}\|_{\delta}\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{\gamma})(\|f\|_{\gamma}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{\gamma})^{k-1}.\end{split}

Applying this inequality along with (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) to

\lambda:=(\psi\circ f)\tau\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\lambda}:=(\psi\circ\tilde{f})\tau

leads after simple calculations, to

\begin{split}&\Bigg|\int_{\mathcal{M}}(\psi\circ f)\tau\wedge df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{k}-\int_{\mathcal{M}}(\psi\circ\tilde{f})\tau\wedge d\tilde{f}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\tilde{f}_{k}\Bigg|\\
&\lesssim\|\tau\|_{\alpha\gamma}[\psi]_{\alpha}(\|f\|_{\gamma}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{\gamma})^{k+\beta}\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{\gamma}^{\alpha-\beta}.\end{split}

The proof is complete.
∎

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented
n
-dimensional manifold with or without boundary. Let

f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{m})
, where
\gamma\in\big(\frac{k}{k+1},1\big]
,
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. If

C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{m})\ni f^{\varepsilon}\to f\quad\text{pointwise on a dense subset of $\mathcal{M}$ as }\varepsilon\to 0,\text{ and}

\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\|f^{\varepsilon}\|_{C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(K)}<\infty\quad\text{for every compact set }K\subset\mathcal{M},

then for any
\kappa\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
and any
\tau\in\Omega_{c}^{n-k}C^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
, the limit

\langle f^{*}\kappa,\tau\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}(f^{\varepsilon})^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau

exists and satisfies

\bigg|\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau\bigg|\lesssim C(\kappa,U_{\tau},f(U_{\tau}))\|\tau\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\|f\|^{k+1}_{C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(U_{\tau})},

where
U_{\tau}\Subset\mathcal{M}
is any neighborhood of
\operatorname{supp}\tau
.

Remark 4.6
.

In the case of manifolds with boundary we assume that
\tau\in\Omega_{c}^{n-k}C^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
has support disjoint from the boundary, so the boundary does not play any role.

Proof.

Let
U_{\tau}^{\prime}\subset U_{\tau}
be a compact submanifold of
\mathcal{M}
(possibly with boundary) that contains
\operatorname{supp}\tau
in its interior. Then, the result follows from Theorems
4.1
and
4.3
with
\alpha=1
, applied to
U_{\tau}^{\prime}
. While
[\kappa]_{1}
need not be finite,
[\kappa]_{1}
is finite in a neighborhood of
f(U_{\tau})
. We also apply Corollary
2.22
to
f^{\varepsilon}
.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with smooth boundary and let
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\gamma\in\big(\frac{n}{n+1},1\big]
. If

\operatorname{Lip}({\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni f^{\varepsilon}\to f\quad\text{in $C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}$ for some $\frac{n}{n+1}<\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma$,}

then for any
v\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and any
g\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\Omega)
the limit

\int_{\Omega}(v\circ f)(x)J_{f}(x)g(x)\,dx:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}(v\circ f^{\varepsilon})(x)J_{f^{\varepsilon}}(x)g(x)\,dx=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}(v\circ f)J_{f^{\varepsilon}}(x)g(x)\,dx

exists and it does not depend on the choice of
\gamma^{\prime}\in\left(\frac{n}{n+1},\gamma\right]
and a Lipschitz approximation
f^{\varepsilon}
.

Remark 4.8
.

Later we will prove that the result is true with
g=1
and
v\in L^{\infty}
, under the weaker assumption that
\gamma\in\big(\frac{n-1}{n},1\big]
, see Theorem
5.19
.

Proof.

We will apply Theorem
4.1
and Remark
4.2
with

\mathcal{M}=\Omega
,
m=n=k
,
\alpha=1
,
\kappa=v\,dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{n}
and
\tau=g
(
\tau
as a zero form is a function).
It suffices to observe that

(f^{\varepsilon})^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau=(v\circ f^{\varepsilon})J_{f^{\varepsilon}}g(x)\,dx_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{n}.

Since
v
as an arbitrary smooth function,
\kappa
does not necessarily belong to
C^{0,\alpha}=\operatorname{Lip}
, but this is not a problem since the family
f^{\varepsilon}
takes values in a bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
where
v
is Lipschitz.
∎

5.
Green, Stokes and the change of variables formula

The aim of this section is to generalize the change of variables formula involving degree,
the Green theorem, and the Stokes theorem, to the case of Hölder continuous mappings.

Let us start with the classical version of the change of variables formula.

Throughout this section we assume that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded domain.

Definition 5.1

(
[
27
, Definition 1.2]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain. Assume that
f\in C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\partial\Omega)
be a regular value of
f
. We define the
local degree of
f
at
y
with respect to
\Omega
as

\operatorname{deg}(f,\Omega,y)=\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}\operatorname{sgn}J_{f}(x).

Moreover, if
y\not\in f(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
, we set
\operatorname{deg}(f,\Omega,y)=0
.

Lemma 5.2

(
[
27
, Proposition 1.8]
)
.

Let
V
be a connected component of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\partial\Omega)
and assume
y_{1},y_{2}\in V
are regular values of
f
. Then
\operatorname{deg}(f,\Omega,y_{1})=\operatorname{deg}(f,\Omega,y_{2})
.

Lemma
5.2
allows us to define the local degree at
all
points
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\Omega)
, by setting
\operatorname{deg}(f,\Omega,y)=\operatorname{deg}(f,\Omega,y_{1})
,
where
y_{1}
is any regular value of
f
lying in the same component of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\Omega)
as
y
.

The local degree is a
C^{\infty}
homotopy invariant:

Lemma 5.3

(
[
27
, Theorem 1.12]
)
.

If
H:\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
C^{\infty}
mapping such that
H(\cdot,0)=f(\cdot)
,
H(\cdot,1)=g(\cdot)

and
y\not\in H(\partial\Omega\times[0,1])
,
then
\operatorname{deg}(f,\Omega,y)=\operatorname{deg}(g,\Omega,y)
.

Proposition
5.3
allows us to extend the notion of local degree to continuous mappings:

Definition 5.4

(
[
27
, Definition 1.18]
)
.

If
f\in C(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
y\not\in f(\partial\Omega)
, we set
\operatorname{deg}(f,\Omega,y)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\operatorname{deg}(f_{\varepsilon},\Omega,y)
, where
f_{\varepsilon}\rightrightarrows f
is a smooth approximation of
f
that converges uniformly on
\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Given
y\not\in f(\partial\Omega)
,
it follows from Lemma
5.3
that there is
\varepsilon_{o}>0
such that for all
0<\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}<\varepsilon_{o}
,

\operatorname{deg}(f_{\varepsilon_{1}},\Omega,p)=\operatorname{deg}(f_{\varepsilon_{2}},\Omega,y)
so the limit exists. Moreover, the degree

\operatorname{deg}(f,\Omega,y)
defined in that way does not depend on the choice of the approximation.

The next lemma easily follows from Lemma
5.3
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain and let

f:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a continuous mapping. If
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\partial\Omega)
, and
F_{1},F_{2}:\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
are two continuous extensions of
f
, then

\operatorname{deg}(F_{1},\Omega,y)=\operatorname{deg}(F_{2},\Omega,y).

Since the degree is independent of the choice of the continuous extension, it depends only on the values of the mapping
f
on the boundary of the domain, and we define:

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain,
f:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, a continuous mapping, and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\partial\Omega)
. Then, we define the
winding number of
f
around
y
by

w(f,y)=\operatorname{deg}(F,\Omega,y),

where
F:\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is any continuous extension of
f
.

The classical change of variables formula reads as follows, see
[
27
, Theorem 5.27]
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with smooth boundary and let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. If
v\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then

(5.1)

\int_{\Omega}(v\circ f)J_{f}(x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}v(y)w(f,y)\,dy.

Remark 5.8
.

The function
w(f,y)
is not defined when
y\in f(\partial\Omega)
. However, the
n
-dimensional measure of the set
f(\partial\Omega)
equals zero, so
w(f,y)
is defined almost everywhere.

Remark 5.9
.

In fact, the change of variables is true for Lipschitz mappings
f
or even for Sobolev mappings satisfying some additional conditions. It is true also for much more general domains.
However, for our purposes the above statement is sufficient. For a proof and generalizations, see Chapter 5 in
[
27
]
.

Assume now that

v=\partial u/\partial y_{i}
for some
u\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial y_{i}}\circ f\right)J_{f}\,dx_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{n}

\displaystyle=f^{*}\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial y_{i}}\,dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=f^{*}\left((-1)^{i-1}d(u\,dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{dy_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i-1}d\left((u\circ f)df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{df_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n})\right).

The Stokes theorem (Lemma
2.1
) along with Lemma
5.7
yields

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with smooth boundary and let
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\in C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})

Then for
u\in W_{\rm loc}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
i=1,2,\ldots,n
we have

(5.2)

(-1)^{i-1}\int_{\partial\Omega}(u\circ f)\,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{df_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\partial u}{\partial y_{i}}(y)w(f,y)\,dy.

Remark 5.11
.

When
u=y_{i}
, this is a multidimensional version of Green’s theorem, see also Corollary
5.23
.

Any form
\kappa\in\Omega^{n-1}W_{\rm loc}^{1,\infty}\big(\mathbb{R}^{n}\big)
can be written as

\kappa=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i-1}u_{i}\,dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{dy_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{n}.

Let
U=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})
. Then (
5.2
) yields

(5.3)

\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{*}\kappa=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\operatorname{div}U(y)\,w(f,y)\,dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w(f,y)\,d\kappa(y).

This gives the following version of the Stokes theorem.

Corollary 5.12
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with smooth boundary and let
f\in C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then for any
\kappa\in\Omega^{n-1}W_{\rm loc}^{1,\infty}\big(\mathbb{R}^{n}\big)
, we have

(5.4)

\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{*}\kappa=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w(f,y)\,d\kappa(y).

Remark 5.13
.

Let
F:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a smooth extension of
f
. Then (
5.4
) takes the form of

\int_{\partial\Omega}F^{*}\kappa=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\operatorname{deg}(F,\Omega,y)\,d\kappa(y),

and it can be regarded as Stokes’ theorem for the singular parametric surface
F:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where the degree counts the self-intersections of the surface taking into account the orientation.

The purpose of this section is to generalize Lemma
5.7
, Corollary
5.10
and Corollary
5.12
to the case of Hölder continuous mappings. We will assume that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded domain with smooth boundary and
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
\gamma>\frac{n-1}{n}
. Under these assumptions, the
n
-dimensional measure of
f(\partial\Omega)
equals zero and hence
w(f,\cdot)
is defined almost everywhere.

Theorem
5.14
due to Olbermann
[
56
]
, deals with integrability properties of the function
w(f,\cdot)
. A simpler proof was discovered by De Lellis and Inauen
[
18
]
who also proved fractional Sobolev regularity of the function
w(f,\cdot)
. Our proof is similar to that in
[
18
]
. See also
[
77
, Theorem 1.3]
.

Theorem 5.14
.

If
\Omega
is a bounded domain with smooth boundary and
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
\gamma\in\big(\frac{n-1}{n},1\big]
, then

(a)

\|w(f,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{\frac{n}{p}}

for all
1<p<\frac{n\gamma}{n-1}
,
where the implied constant depends on
n
,
p
,
\gamma
, and
\Omega
only.

(b)

If
f_{k}\to f
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}
, then
w(f_{k},\cdot)\to w(f,\cdot)
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, for all
1\leq p<\frac{n\gamma}{n-1}
.

Remark 5.15
.

Since
w(f,\cdot)
equals zero outside a bounded set, it follows that
w(f,\cdot)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

In the proof we will need three lemmata. All of them are well known.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
\mu
be a finite measure on a space
X
. Suppose that
f_{k}\to f

\mu
-a.e., and
\sup_{k}\|f_{k}\|_{L^{q}(X)}<\infty
for some
1<q<\infty
. Then
f\in L^{q}(X)
and
f_{k}\to f
in
L^{p}(X)
for any
1\leq p<q
.

Proof.

Let
M=\sup_{k}\|f_{k}\|_{L^{q}}
. It follows from Fatou’s lemma that
f\in L^{q}
and
\|f\|_{L^{q}}\leq M
. It remains to prove that
f_{k}\to f
in
L^{p}
. Suppose to the contrary that there is
\varepsilon>0
and a subsequence such that
\|f_{k_{\ell}}-f\|_{L^{p}}>\varepsilon
. According to Egorov’s theorem, there is
E\subset X
, such that

f_{k_{\ell}}\rightrightarrows f\text{ uniformly on $E$}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu(X\setminus E)<\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{4M}\Big)^{\frac{pq}{q-p}}.

Minkowski’s and Hölder’s inequalities yield

\begin{split}\|f_{k_{\ell}}-f\|_{L^{p}}&=\|(f_{k_{\ell}}-f)\chi_{E}+(f_{k_{\ell}}-f)\chi_{X\setminus E}\|_{L^{p}}\\
&\leq\Bigg(\int_{E}|f_{k_{\ell}}-f|^{p}\,d\mu\Bigg)^{1/p}+\Bigg(\int_{X\setminus E}|f_{k_{\ell}}-f|^{q}\,d\mu\Bigg)^{1/q}\mu(X\setminus E)^{\frac{q-p}{pq}}\\
&\leq\Bigg(\int_{E}|f_{k_{\ell}}-f|^{p}\,d\mu\Bigg)^{1/p}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}<\varepsilon,\end{split}

for sufficiently large
\ell
, which leads to a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. If

(5.5)

U(x)=\frac{1}{n\omega_{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{x-z}{|x-z|^{n}}\phi(z)\,dz,

then
U\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies
\operatorname{div}U=\phi
. Moreover,

(5.6)

\|DU\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n,p)\|\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\quad\text{for all $1<p<\infty$.}

Proof.

Let
\Gamma
be the fundamental solution of the Laplace equation. Then
\Gamma*\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
solves the Poisson equation

\Delta(\Gamma*\phi)=\phi
and it follows from basic Calderón-Zygmund estimates (see
[
29
, Theorem 9.9]
) that

\|D^{2}(\Gamma*\phi)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n,p)\|\Delta(\Gamma*\phi)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\text{for }1<p<\infty.

Let
U:=D(\Gamma*\phi)=(D\Gamma)*\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Clearly
U
satisfies
\operatorname{div}U=\phi
.
Since

D\Gamma(x)=\frac{1}{n\omega_{n}}\frac{x}{|x|^{n}},

U
is defined by (
5.5
). We have
