\|DU\|_{L^{p}}=\|D^{2}(\Gamma*\phi)\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{L^{p}}
.
∎

The next result is a consequence of Morrey’s inequality, see
[
23
, Section 4.5.3]
.

Lemma 5.18
.

If
u\in W^{1,p}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
n<p<\infty
, satisfies
Du\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then

|u(y)-u(x)|\leq C(n,p)|x-y|^{1-\frac{n}{p}}\|Du\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\quad\text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Proof of Theorem
5.14
.

We will begin with the proof of (a).
Assume first that
f\in C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
If
\alpha\in(0,1]
is such that

(5.7)

\gamma>\frac{n-1}{n-1+\alpha},

then for any
\kappa\in\Omega^{n-1}C_{\rm b}^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, (
4.3
) applied to
\mathcal{M}=\partial\Omega
, yields the estimate

\Bigg|\,\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{*}\kappa\Bigg|\lesssim[\kappa]_{\alpha}\|f\|^{n-1+\alpha}_{C^{0,\gamma}}.

Let
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and let
U=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be the solution to
\operatorname{div}U=\phi
as in Lemma
5.17
. If

\kappa=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i-1}u_{i}\,dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{dy_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{n},

then (
5.3
) yields

\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{*}\kappa=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\operatorname{div}U(y)\,w(f,y)\,dy=\int_{R^{n}}\phi(y)w(f,y)\,dy,

and hence

(5.8)

\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi(y)w(f,y)\,dy\Bigg|\lesssim[U]_{\alpha}\|f\|^{n-1+\alpha}_{C^{0,\gamma}}.

Let
q
be the Hölder conjugate to
p
. Since
1<p<\frac{n}{n-1}
, it follows that
n<q<\infty
.
Let
\alpha=1-\frac{n}{q}
and note that (
5.7
) is satisfied. Lemmata
5.18
and
5.17
yield

[U]_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|DU\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

and hence (
5.8
) implies

\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi(y)w(f,y)\,dy\Bigg|\lesssim\|\phi\|_{L^{q}}\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{\frac{n}{p}}.

Taking supremum over all
\phi
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{q}}\leq 1
, proves the estimate

\|w(f,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{\frac{n}{p}}
, under the assumption that
f\in C^{\infty}
. That completes the proof of (a) for smooth mappings.
The general case will be obtained by approximation.

Thus, assume that
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
f_{k}\in C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a sequence
such that
f_{k}\rightrightarrows f
uniformly and
\sup_{k}\|f_{k}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}
, see
Corollary
2.34
. We have,

\|w(f_{k},\cdot)\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\|f_{k}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{\frac{n}{p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{\frac{n}{p}},

and it suffices to prove that
w(f_{k},\cdot)\to w(f,\cdot)
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Since
w(f_{k},y)\to w(f,y)
pointwise for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\partial\Omega)
, it follows that
w(f_{k},\cdot)\to w(f,\cdot)
almost everywhere.

Fix
\tilde{p}
, such that

p<\tilde{p}<\frac{n\gamma}{n-1}
.
We proved the inequality in (a) for smooth mappings. Applying it to
f_{k}
with
p
replaced by
\tilde{p}
, we have

\|w(f_{k},\cdot)\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}}\lesssim\|f_{k}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{\frac{n}{\tilde{p}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{\frac{n}{\tilde{p}}}.

Note that there is
R>0
such that
w(f_{k},y)=w(f,y)=0
for all
k
and all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R}
. Since
w(f_{k},\cdot)\to w(f,\cdot)
almost everywhere, and the sequence is bounded in
L^{\tilde{p}}
, it follows from Lemma
5.16
that
w(f_{k},\cdot)\to w(f,\cdot)
in
L^{p}(B_{R})
and hence in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. That completes the proof of (a).

The proof of part (b) is essentially contained in estimates used in the proof of part (a). Let
\tilde{p}
, and
R
be as above.

If
f_{k}\to f
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}
, then
w(f_{k},\cdot)\to w(f,\cdot)
almost everywhere. Since

\|w(f_{k},\cdot)\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}}\lesssim\sup_{k}\|f_{k}\|^{\frac{n}{\tilde{p}}}_{C^{0,{\gamma}}}<\infty,

and
\tilde{p}>p
, it follows from Lemma
5.16
that
w(f_{k},\cdot)\to w(f,\cdot)
in
L^{p}(B_{R})
and hence in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The proof is complete.
∎

Compare the next result with Corollary
4.7
.

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with smooth boundary and let
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\gamma\in\big(\frac{n-1}{n},1\big]
. If

C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\Omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni f^{\varepsilon}\to f\quad\text{in $C^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}$ for some $\frac{n-1}{n}<\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma$,}

then for any
v\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the limit

(5.9)

\int_{\Omega}(v\circ f)(x)J_{f}(x)\,dx:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}(v\circ f^{\varepsilon})(x)J_{f^{\varepsilon}}(x)\,dx

exists and it does not depend on the choice of
\gamma^{\prime}\in\left(\frac{n-1}{n},\gamma\right]
and a smooth approximation
f^{\varepsilon}
. Moreover,

(5.10)

\int_{\Omega}(v\circ f)(x)J_{f}(x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}v(y)w(f,y)\,dy.

Remark 5.20
.

The result is true for a larger class of functions
v
due to higher integrability of the winding number, but we consider the case
v\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}
only in order to avoid technical difficulties. This case is sufficient for most of the applications. For related results see also

[
16
, Proposition 6]
,
[
47
, Lemma 2.2]
,

[
51
, Proposition 7.1]
, and
[
52
, Lemma 3.1.]
,
[
77
, Theorem 1.3]
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
5.7
and Theorem
5.14
(b),

\int_{\Omega}(v\circ f^{\varepsilon})(x)J_{f^{\varepsilon}}(x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}v(y)w(f^{\varepsilon},y)\,dy\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}v(y)w(f,y)\,dy,\quad\text{as $\varepsilon\to 0$,}

because
w(f^{\varepsilon},\cdot)\to w(f,\cdot)
in
L^{1}
, the functions vanish outside a certain ball, and
v\in L^{\infty}
on that ball.
We proved that the limit (
5.9
) satisfies (
5.10
) and hence it does not depend on the choice of
\gamma^{\prime}
and approximation.
∎

Theorem 5.21
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with smooth boundary and let
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\gamma\in\big(\frac{n-1}{n},1\big]
.
If

C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni f^{\varepsilon}\to f\quad\text{in $C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}$ for some $\frac{n-1}{n}<\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma$,}

Then for any
\kappa\in\Omega^{n-1}W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}\big(\mathbb{R}^{n}\big)
, the limit

(5.11)

\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{*}\kappa:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\partial\Omega}(f^{\varepsilon})^{*}\kappa

exists and it does not depend on the choice of
\gamma^{\prime}\in\left(\frac{n-1}{n},\gamma\right]
and a smooth approximation
f^{\varepsilon}
. Moreover,

(5.12)

\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{*}\kappa=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w(f,y)\,d\kappa(y).

Remark 5.22
.

Comments in Remark
5.20
apply here as well.

Proof.

According to Corollary
5.12
, (
5.3
), and Theorem
5.14
(b),

\int_{\partial\Omega}(f^{\varepsilon})^{*}\kappa=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\operatorname{div}U(y)w(f^{\varepsilon},y)\,dy\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\to 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\operatorname{div}U(y)w(f,y)\,dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w(f,y)\,d\kappa(y),

because
w(f^{\varepsilon},\cdot)\to w(f,\cdot)
in
L^{1}
, the functions vanish outside a certain ball and
\operatorname{div}U\in L^{\infty}
on that ball.
We proved that the limit (
5.11
) satisfies (
5.12
) and hence it does not depend on the choice of
\gamma^{\prime}
and approximation.
∎

Applying Theorem
5.21
to

\kappa=(-1)^{i-1}y_{i}\,dy_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{dy_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{n}
yields

u=x_{i}
and we immediately obtain (cf.
[
47
, Lemma 2.3]
):

Corollary 5.23
.

If
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded domain with smooth boundary and
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
\gamma>\frac{n-1}{n}
, then

(5.13)

(-1)^{i-1}\int_{\partial\Omega}f_{i}\,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{df_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w(f,y)\,dy\quad\text{for $i=1,2,\ldots,n$.}

In particular we obtain the following generalization of Green’s formula

(5.14)

\frac{1}{n}\int_{\partial\Omega}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i-1}f_{i}\,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{df_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w(f,y)\,dy.

Remark 5.24
.

As in Theorem
5.21
, the generalized integral on the left hand side of (
5.13
) is defined as the limit of integrals of smooth approximations

\int_{\partial\Omega}f_{i}\,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{df_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\partial\Omega}f_{i}^{\varepsilon}\,df_{1}^{\varepsilon}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{df_{i}^{\varepsilon}}\wedge\ldots\wedge df^{\varepsilon}_{n},

where
C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni f^{\varepsilon}\to f
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}
for some
\gamma^{\prime}\in(\frac{n-1}{n},\gamma]
.

Remark 5.25
.

Note that the right hand side of (
5.14
) can be interpreted as the oriented volume enclosed by
f:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In particular, if
\partial\Omega
is connected and
f
is an embedding, the right hand side of (
5.14
) equals
\pm 1
times the volume enclosed by the surface
f(\partial\Omega)
.

The next result follows from Theorem
5.19
applied to
v=1
and from Corollary
5.23
.

Corollary 5.26
.

If
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded domain with smooth boundary and
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
\gamma>\frac{n-1}{n}
, then

\int_{\Omega}df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\int_{\partial\Omega}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i-1}f_{i}\,df_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{df_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{n}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w(f,y)\,dy.

The special case when
n=2
and
\Omega=\mathbb{B}^{2}
, so
\partial\Omega=\mathbb{S}^{1}
, was originally proved in
[
47
, Lemma 2.3]
.
We will write
\gamma
instead of
f
to be consistent with notation used in Section
6
.

Corollary 5.27
.

If
\gamma=(\gamma^{x},\gamma^{y}):\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for some
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
, then

(5.15)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\gamma^{x}d\gamma^{y}-\gamma^{y}d\gamma^{x}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\gamma,z)\,dz.

We also have

Corollary 5.28
.

If
f=(f^{x},f^{y}):\mathbb{B}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for some
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
, then

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{2}}df^{x}\wedge df^{y}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(f,z)\,dz.

Both corollaries will be used in Section
6
.

6.
Heisenberg groups

6.1.
Preliminaries

In this section we collect basic definitions and facts from the theory of the Heisenberg groups.

Definition 6.1
.

The
Heisenberg group
is a Lie group

\mathbb{H}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
equipped with the group law

\displaystyle(z,t)*(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left(z+z^{\prime},t+t^{\prime}+2\,{\rm Im}\,\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}\overline{z_{j}^{\prime}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(x+x^{\prime},y+y^{\prime},t+t^{\prime}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(y_{j}x_{j}^{\prime}-x_{j}y_{j}^{\prime})\right).

Here and in what follows we will denote coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
by

(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},t)
.
Also,
t
will be called the
height
of the point
(z,t)
.

Note also that if we identify elements
z
and
z^{\prime}
with vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, then the
product in the Heisenberg group can be written in terms of the standard symplectic form, see (
2.5
)

(z,t)*(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})=(z+z^{\prime},t+t^{\prime}-2\upomega(z,z^{\prime})).

A basis of left invariant vector fields is given
at any point
(x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{n},t)\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, by

X_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}+2y_{j}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\quad Y_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}-2x_{j}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\quad T=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}

for
j=1,2,\dots,n
.
These left invariant vector fields determine the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}^{n}
.
It is not hard to see that

[X_{j},Y_{j}]=-4T
for
j=1\dots,n

and all other commutators vanish.

Definition 6.2
.

The
Horizontal distribution
in the Heisenberg group
is a subbundle of the tangent bundle defined by

H_{p}\mathbb{H}^{n}=\text{span}\left\{X_{1}(p),Y_{1}(p),\dots,X_{n}(p),Y_{n}(p)\right\}\quad\text{for all }p\in\mathbb{H}^{n}.

This is a smooth distribution of

2n
-dimensional subspaces in the

(2n+1)
-dimensional tangent space
T_{p}\mathbb{H}^{n}=T_{p}\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
.

The next lemma is straightforward. It shows that the horizontal distribution in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
is nothing else but a standard contact structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

The horizontal distribution
H\mathbb{H}^{n}
is the kernel of a standard contact form

\upalpha=dt+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(x_{j}\,dy_{j}-y_{j}\,dx_{j}).

i.e.
H_{p}\mathbb{H}^{n}=\operatorname{ker}\upalpha(p)\subset T_{p}\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}

Definition 6.4
.

An absolutely continuous curve
\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}

is a
horizontal curve
if it is almost everywhere tangent to the horizontal distribution i.e.,

\gamma^{\prime}(s)\in H_{\gamma(s)}\mathbb{H}^{n}\quad\text{for almost every }s\in[a,b].

It easily follows from Lemma
6.3
that an absolutely continuous curve

\gamma(s)=(\gamma^{x_{1}}(s),\gamma^{y_{1}}(s),\dots,\gamma^{x_{n}}(s),\gamma^{y_{n}}(s),\gamma^{t}(s))

is horizontal if and only if

(6.1)

(\gamma^{t})^{\prime}(s)=2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\gamma^{y_{j}}(s)(\gamma^{x_{j}})^{\prime}(s)-\gamma^{x_{j}}(s)(\gamma^{y_{j}})^{\prime}(s))\quad\text{ for almost every }s\in[a,b].

Let
\gamma_{j}=(\gamma^{x_{j}},\gamma^{y_{j}})
be the projection of
\gamma
onto the
x_{j}y_{j}
-plane. Integrating (
6.1
) yields the following formula for the change of height
\gamma^{t}
along the curve
\gamma
:

(6.2)

\begin{split}\gamma^{t}(b)-\gamma^{t}(a)&=2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{a}^{b}\gamma^{y_{j}}(s)(\gamma^{x_{j}}(s))^{\prime}-\gamma^{x_{j}}(s)(\gamma^{y_{j}}(s))^{\prime}\,ds\\
&=-4\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\gamma_{j}}x_{j}\,dy_{j}-y_{j}\,dx_{j}\Bigg).\end{split}

This formula has a nice geometric interpretation.

If
\gamma
is a closed horizontal curve,
\gamma(a)=\gamma(b)
, then (
6.2
) along with Green’s formula that is true for absolutely continuous curves (see, (
5.15
)), give

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\gamma_{j},y)\,dy=0.

That means, the sum of the oriented areas enclosed by the curves
\gamma_{j}
equals zero.

Definition 6.5
.

With an absolutely continuous curve
\eta:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, that is not necessarily closed, we associate a closed curve
\bar{\eta}:[a-1,b+1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by

\bar{\eta}(s)=\begin{cases}(s-a+1)\eta(a)&\text{if $a-1\leq s\leq a$,}\\
\eta(s)&\text{if $a\leq s\leq b$,}\\
(b+1-s)\eta(b)&\text{if $b\leq s\leq b+1$.}\end{cases}

That is, we first follow along a straight line from the origin
0
to
\eta(a)
, then, we follow the curve
\eta
and then, we follow a straight line from
\eta(b)
back to the origin
0
.

It is easy to see that

(6.3)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\eta}x\,dy-y\,dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\bar{\eta}}x\,dy-y\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\bar{\eta},y)\,dy

equals the oriented area enclosed by the closed curve
\bar{\eta}
. This generalizes Green’s formula to the case of non-closed curves.

Now, if
\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is a horizontal curve that is not necessarily closed, then (
6.2
) and (
6.3
) yield a formula for the change of height

(6.4)

\gamma^{t}(b)-\gamma^{t}(a)=-4\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\bar{\gamma}_{j},y)\,dy.

While the above results are straightforward consequences of the definition of a horizontal curve, we will see later that most of these results generalize to the case of
\alpha
-Hölder continuous curves
\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
, provided
\alpha>1/2
.

Every Lie group can be equipped with a left invariant Riemannian metric. We equip the Heisenberg
group with the left invariant metric such that the vectors

X_{1}(p),Y_{1}(p),\ldots,X_{n}(p),Y_{n}(p),T(p)
are orthonormal at every point
p\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
.
We denote this left invariant metric on
\mathbb{H}^{n}
by
g
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Every two points in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
can be connected by a horizontal curve.

Indeed, it is not difficult to construct directly a piecewise smooth horizontal curve connecting any two points, or one can use an explicit construction of a geodesic. For a construction of a geodesic see e.g.
[
39
]
and references therein.

Definition 6.7
.

Let
\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
be a horizontal curve and write

\gamma^{\prime}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}(t)X_{j}(\gamma(t))+b_{j}(t)Y_{j}(\gamma(t))\quad\text{ a.e. }t\in[a,b].

The
horizontal length
of
\gamma

is defined as the length of
\gamma
with respect to the metric
g

\ell_{H}(\gamma):=\int_{a}^{b}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|_{H}\,dt

where

|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|_{H}=\sqrt{\langle\gamma^{\prime}(t),\gamma^{\prime}(t)\rangle_{g}}=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}(a_{j}(t))^{2}+(b_{j}(t))^{2}}\,.

Definition 6.8
.

The
Carnot-Carathéodory metric
in
\mathbb{H}^{n}

is defined for all
p,q\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
by

d_{cc}(p,q)=\inf_{\gamma}\{\ell_{H}(\gamma)\},

where the infimum is taken over all horizontal curves
connecting
p
and
q
.

It follows Lemma
6.6
that
d_{cc}(p,q)<\infty
for all
p,q\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
and hence
d_{cc}
is a metric in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

The Carnot-Carathéodory metric is very far from being Euclidean. In some directions it is Euclidean, but in some other directions it is the square root of the Euclidean metric. In general, we have the following well known comparison of the metrics:

Lemma 6.9
.

If
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is compact,
then there is a constant
C=C(K)\geq 1

such that

C^{-1}|p-q|\leq d_{cc}(p,q)\leq C|p-q|^{1/2}\quad\text{for all $p,q\in K$.}

The lower bound directly follows from the definition of the
d_{cc}
metric, while the upper bound can be concluded from Lemma
6.11
and (
6.5
).

While the metric
d_{cc}
is difficult to evaluate, there is a bi-Lipschitz equivalent metric that can be computed explicitly.

Definition 6.10
.

For any
(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, the
Korányi norm

\|\cdot\|_{K}
is

\|(z,t)\|_{K}:=(|z|^{4}+t^{2})^{1/4}

and the
Korányi metric

d_{K}
is

d_{K}(p,q):=\|q^{-1}*p\|_{K}.

While it is non-obvious, one can prove that

d_{K}
satisfies the triangle inequality and therefore it is a metric, see
[
46
, p. 320]
.
Moreover, it is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to the Carnot-Carathéodory metric,

[
11
, Section 2.1]
:

Lemma 6.11
.

d_{K}
is a metric. Moreover, the metrics

d_{cc}
and
d_{K}
are bi-Lipschitz equivalent.
That is, there is a constant
C\geq 1
such that

C^{-1}d_{K}(p,q)\leq d_{cc}(p,q)\leq Cd_{K}(p,q)\quad\text{for all $p,q\in\mathbb{H}^{n}$.}

In what follows, we will regard
\mathbb{H}^{n}
as a metric space with the Korányi metric
d_{K}
.

One may easily check that for
p=(z,t)
and
q=(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})
we have

(6.5)

\begin{split}d_{K}(p,q)=\|q^{-1}*p\|_{K}&=\left(|z-z^{\prime}|^{4}+\Bigg|t-t^{\prime}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(x_{j}^{\prime}y_{j}-x_{j}y_{j}^{\prime})\Bigg|^{2}\,\right)^{1/4}\\
&\approx|z-z^{\prime}|+\Bigg|t-t^{\prime}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(x_{j}^{\prime}y_{j}-x_{j}y_{j}^{\prime})\Bigg|^{1/2}\,.\end{split}

It will also be convenient to use notation

(6.6)

d_{K}(p,q)=(|\pi(p)-\pi(q)|^{4}+|\varphi(p,q)|^{2})^{1/4},

where
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,
\pi(z,t)=z
is the orthogonal projection and

(6.7)

\varphi(p,q)=t-t^{\prime}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(x_{j}^{\prime}y_{j}-x_{j}y_{j}^{\prime}).

The Heisenberg group is equipped with dilations
\delta_{r}:\mathbb{H}^{n}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
,
r\geq 0
, defined by

(6.8)

\delta_{r}(x,t)=(rx,r^{2}t).

For
r>0
, the dilationas are group authomorphisms, but this fact will play no role here.
The following obvious observation is very useful

(6.9)

d_{K}(\delta_{r}p,\delta_{r}q)=rd_{K}(p,q)\quad\text{for all }p,q\in\mathbb{H}^{n}\text{ and }r\geq 0.

Note that
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, means that

(6.10)

[f]_{\gamma}=\sup\left\{\frac{d_{K}(f(x),f(y))}{|x-y|^{\gamma}}:\,x,y\in\Omega,\ x\neq y\right\}<\infty.

Recall that
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
stands for a subspace of
C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
that consists of mappings that are bounded as mappings into
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
.

If
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, is Lipschitz continuous, then by Lemma
6.9
,
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is locally Lipschitz continuous and hence differentiable a.e. It turns out that at every point
p
of differentiability of
f
(and hence almost everywhere), the derivative of
f
maps the tangent space
T_{p}\mathbb{R}^{m}
to the horizontal space
H_{f(p)}\mathbb{H}^{n}
, which is equivalent to the fact that
f^{*}\upalpha(p)=0
.
This is a consequence of the following more general result, see
[
2
, Proposition 8.1]
.

Proposition 6.12
.

Suppose that a mapping

f=(f^{x_{1}},f^{y_{1}},\ldots,f^{x_{n}},f^{y_{n}},f^{t}):\Omega\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
, defined on an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}

is of class
C^{0,\frac{1}{2}+}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
. If the components

f^{x_{j}},f^{y_{j}}
are differentiable at
p_{0}\in\Omega
for
j=1,2,\ldots,n
, then
the last component
f^{t}
is also differentiable at
p_{0}
and

Df^{t}(p_{0})=2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(f^{y_{j}}(p_{0})Df^{x_{j}}(p_{0})-f^{x_{j}}(p_{0})Df^{y_{j}}(p_{0})\right)\,,

i.e. the image of the derivative
Df(p_{0})
lies in the horizontal space
H_{f(p_{0})}\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

Remark 6.13
.

Recall that the class
C^{0,\frac{1}{2}+}
was defined in Definition
2.16
.

Proof.

From the assumptions about
f
, there is a
modulus of continuity
\omega
such that
for all
p
in a neighborhood of
p_{0}
we have

d_{\rm{K}}(f(p),f(p_{0}))\leq|p-p_{0}|^{1/2}\omega(|p-p_{0}|)
.
Indeed, if
E
is a compact closure of a neighborhood of
p_{0}
and

\omega_{1}(t):=\sup\Bigg\{\frac{d_{K}(f(p),f(q))}{|p-q|^{1/2}}:\,p,q\in E,\ 0<|p-q|\leq t\Bigg\},

then existence of a modulus of continuity
\omega
follows from Lemma
2.26
.

Therefore, (
6.5
) yields

\begin{split}&\left|f^{t}(p)-f^{t}(p_{0})-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big(f^{y_{j}}(p_{0})(f^{x_{j}}(p)-f^{x_{j}}(p_{0}))-f^{x_{j}}(p_{0})(f^{y_{j}}(p)-f^{y_{j}}(p_{0}))\big)\right|\\
&=\left|f^{t}(p)-f^{t}(p_{0})+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big(f^{x_{j}}(p_{0})f^{y_{j}}(p)-f^{x_{j}}(p)f^{y_{j}}(p_{0})\big)\right|\\
&\leq d_{K}(f(p),f(p_{0}))^{2}\leq|p-p_{0}|\omega^{2}(|p-p_{0}|),\end{split}

and hence

\displaystyle\left|f^{t}(p)-f^{t}(p_{0})-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big(f^{y_{j}}(p_{0})Df^{x_{j}}(p_{0})-f^{x_{j}}(p_{0})Df^{y_{j}}(p_{0})\big)(p-p_{0})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|p-p_{0}|\omega^{2}(|p-p_{0}|)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\sum_{j=1}^{n}|f^{y_{j}}(p_{0})|\,|f^{x_{j}}(p)-f^{x_{j}}(p_{0})-Df^{x_{j}}(p_{0})(p-p_{0})|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\sum_{j=1}^{n}|f^{x_{j}}(p_{0})|\,|f^{y_{j}}(p)-f^{y_{j}}(p_{0})-Df^{y_{j}}(p_{0})(p-p_{0})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(|p-p_{0}|),

because the functions
f^{x_{j}}
and
f^{y_{j}}
are differentiable at
p_{0}
. The proof is complete.
∎

It follows from Proposition
6.12
and the Rademacher theorem, that at almost every point, the derivative of a Lipschitz map
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, maps the tangent space to a horizontal subspace, and hence
\operatorname{rank}Df\leq 2n
almost everywhere. However, a stronger result is true.

Proposition 6.14
.

If
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is Lipschitz continuous, then
\operatorname{rank}Df\leq n
a.e.

Proof.

Components of
f
are locally Lipschitz continuous and

df^{t}=2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(f^{y_{j}}df^{x_{j}}-f^{x_{j}}df^{y_{j}})
.
Taking the distributional exterior derivative yields

\sum_{j=1}^{n}df^{x_{j}}\wedge df^{y_{j}}=0
.
That means
F^{*}\upomega=0
, where
\upomega
is the standard symplectic form and

F=(f^{x_{1}},f^{y_{1}},\ldots,f^{x_{n}},f^{y_{n}}):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
Thus
DF
maps
T_{p}\mathbb{R}^{m}
into an isotropic subspace of
T_{f(p)}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
so
\operatorname{rank}DF\leq n
by Lemma
2.5
. Since
Df^{t}
is a linear combination of
Df^{x_{j}}
and
Df^{y_{j}}
,
j=1,2,\ldots,n
, it follows that
\operatorname{rank}Df\leq n
.
∎

7.
Hölder continuous mappings into the Heisenberg group

7.1.
A characterization of Hölder continuous mappings into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

A smooth mapping,
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, defined on an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, is horizontal as a mapping into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, if and only if
f^{*}\upalpha=0
. The next result, generalizes this characterization to the case of Hölder continuous mappings.

Recall that
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,
\pi(z,t)=z
is the projection onto the first
2n
components.

Note that if
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is
\gamma
-Hölder continuous, then by Lemma
6.9
, it is only (locally)
\gamma/2
-Hölder continuous as a mapping into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. In fact,
\gamma/2
-Hölder continuity is the best estimate we can get: the identity map
\operatorname{id}:\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is Lipschitz continuous (
\gamma=1
), but
\operatorname{id}:\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
is only locally
1/2
-Hölder continuous, see also
[
36
, Proposition 3.1]
.

The next result characterizes maps
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
that are
\gamma
-Hölder continuous as maps into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
be a continuous map defined on an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and let
1/2<\gamma\leq 1
. Then
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
if and only if both of the conditions are satisfied

(a)

\pi\circ f\in C_{\rm loc}^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2n})
,

(b)

f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\upalpha\rightrightarrows 0
uniformly as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
on compact subsets of
\Omega
, where
f_{\varepsilon}=f*\eta_{\varepsilon}
is a standard
\varepsilon
-mollification of
f
(Definition
2.31
).

Both of the implications in Theorem
7.1
will be proved as separate results. In fact these results will provide stronger estimates. The implication
\Rightarrow
in Theorem
7.1
follows from Lemmata
6.9
and
6.11
(this proves (a)) and from the following result
[
37
, Lemma 2.2]
:

Theorem 7.2
.

Suppose that

f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is open and

0<\gamma\leq 1
. If
B^{m}(x_{o},2r)\subset\Omega
, then

\|f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\upalpha\|_{L^{\infty}(B^{m}(x_{o},r))}\leq 4^{\gamma}\|\nabla\eta\|_{1}[f]_{\gamma,2\varepsilon,B^{m}(x_{o},2r)}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}\quad\text{for all $0<\varepsilon<r$,}

where
f_{\varepsilon}=f*\eta_{\varepsilon}
is a standard
\varepsilon
-mollification of
f
.

The implication
\Leftarrow
in Theorem
7.1
follows from the next result.
Note that by Proposition
2.32
, condition (a) in Theorem
7.1
implies that

\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\,[\pi\circ f_{\varepsilon}]_{C^{0,\gamma}(K;\mathbb{R}^{2n})}<\infty\quad\text{if $K\subset\Omega$ is compact.}

Theorem 7.3
.

Suppose that
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is a continuous mapping defined on an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Let
1/2<\gamma\leq 1
.
Suppose that
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
is a sequence of smooth mappings such that for every compact set
K\subset\Omega
:

(a)

f_{i}\rightrightarrows f
and
f_{i}^{*}\upalpha\rightrightarrows 0
uniformly on
K
,

(b)

\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\,[\pi\circ f_{i}]_{C^{0,\gamma}(K;\mathbb{R}^{2n})}<\infty.

Then
f\in C_{\rm loc}^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
.

Proof of Theorem
7.2
.

Recall that
\varphi(p,q)
was defined in (
6.7
).
Let
p\in B^{m}(x_{o},r)
and let
\varepsilon\in(0,r)
.
In what follows we will identify
(f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\upalpha)(p)
with the vector (of equal length):

\begin{split}&(f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\upalpha)(p)=\nabla f^{t}_{\varepsilon}(p)+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(f_{\varepsilon}^{x_{j}}(p)\nabla f_{\varepsilon}^{y_{j}}(p)-f_{\varepsilon}^{y_{j}}(p)\nabla f_{\varepsilon}^{x_{j}}(p))=\\
&\varepsilon^{-m-1}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}}\left(\big(f^{t}(p-z)-f_{\varepsilon}^{t}(p)+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(f_{\varepsilon}^{x_{j}}(p)f^{y_{j}}(p-z)-f_{\varepsilon}^{y_{j}}(p)f^{x_{j}}(p-z)\big)\right)\nabla\eta\left(\frac{z}{\varepsilon}\right)\,dz\\
&=\varepsilon^{-m-1}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}}\varphi(f(p-z),f_{\varepsilon}(p))\nabla\eta\left(\frac{z}{\varepsilon}\right)\,dz\\
&=\varepsilon^{-m-1}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}}\varphi(f(p-z),f(p-w))\eta_{\varepsilon}(w)\nabla\eta\left(\frac{z}{\varepsilon}\right)\,dw\,dz.\end{split}

In the second equality we used the fact that

\int_{B_{\varepsilon}}f^{t}_{\varepsilon}(p)\nabla\eta\left(\frac{z}{\varepsilon}\right)\,dz=0
.
Easy verification of the last equality is left to the reader.
Note that (
6.6
) yields

|\varphi(f(p-z),f(p-w))|\leq d_{K}(f(p-z),f(p-w))^{2}\leq[f]_{\gamma,2\varepsilon,B^{m}(x_{o},2r)}^{2}(2\varepsilon)^{2\gamma}.

Therefore,

|(f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha)(p)|\leq[f]_{\gamma,2\varepsilon,B^{m}(x_{o},2r)}^{2}(2\varepsilon)^{2\gamma}\varepsilon^{-1}\varepsilon^{-m}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}}\left|\nabla\eta\left(\frac{z}{\varepsilon}\right)\right|\,dz=4^{\gamma}\|\nabla\eta\|_{1}[f]^{2}_{\gamma,2\varepsilon,B^{m}(x_{o},2r)}\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.3
.

Given a closed ball
\bar{B}\Subset\Omega
, it suffices to prove that there is
C>0

such that for any
p,q\in\bar{B}
,
p\neq q
, there is

i_{pq}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(7.1)

d_{K}(f_{i}(q),f_{i}(p))\leq C|q-p|^{\gamma}\quad\text{for all $i\geq i_{pq}$.}

Note that in estimate (
7.1
),
i_{pq}
is allowed to depend on the choice of
p
and
q
. However, the constant
C
is independent of
p,q\in\bar{B}
, but it depends on
\bar{B}
.

Once we prove (
7.1
), the
\gamma
-Hölder continuity of
f:\bar{B}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
will follow upon passing to the limit as
i\to\infty
.

Recall that (see (
6.6
))

(7.2)

d_{K}(f_{i}(q),f_{i}(p))\approx|\pi(f_{i}(q))-\pi(f_{i}(p))|+|\varphi(f_{i}(q),f_{i}(p))|^{1/2}.

Clearly,

(7.3)

|\pi(f_{i}(q))-\pi(f_{i}(p))|\leq C|q-p|^{\gamma}\quad\text{for all $p,q\in\bar{B}$ and all $i$},

by the assumption (b).
It remains to estimate
|\varphi(f_{i}(q),f_{i}(p))|
. With the notation

f_{i}=(f_{i}^{x_{1}},f_{i}^{y_{1}},\ldots,f_{i}^{x_{n}},f_{i}^{y_{n}},f_{i}^{t})\quad\text{and}\quad p_{s}=p+s(q-p),

we have

\begin{split}&\varphi(f_{i}(q),f_{i}(p))=f_{i}^{t}(q)-f_{i}^{t}(p)+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p)f_{i}^{y_{j}}(q)-f_{i}^{x_{j}}(q)f_{i}^{y_{j}}(p)\right)\\
&=f_{i}^{t}(q)-f_{i}^{t}(p)+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p)(f_{i}^{y_{j}}(q)-f_{i}^{y_{j}}(p))-f_{i}^{y_{j}}(p)(f_{i}^{x_{j}}(q)-f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p))\right)\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}df_{i}^{t}(p_{s})(q-p)\,ds+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{1}(f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p)df_{i}^{y_{j}}(p_{s})-f_{i}^{y_{j}}(p)df_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s}))(q-p)\,ds=:\heartsuit.\end{split}

Since

(f_{i}^{*}\upalpha)(p_{s})=df_{i}^{t}(p_{s})+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s})df_{i}^{y_{j}}(p_{s})-f_{i}^{y_{j}}(p_{s})df_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s})),

we have

\begin{split}\heartsuit&=\int_{0}^{1}(f_{i}^{*}\upalpha)(p_{s})(q-p)\,ds\\
&+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\big((f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p)-f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s}))df_{i}^{y_{j}}(p_{s})-(f^{y_{j}}_{i}(p)-f_{i}^{y_{j}}(p_{s}))df_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s})\big)(q-p)\,ds.\end{split}

Therefore,

(7.4)

\begin{split}|\varphi(f_{i}(q),f_{i}(p))|&\leq\sup_{\bar{B}}|f_{i}^{*}\upalpha|\,|q-p|+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Bigg|\int_{0}^{1}(f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p)-f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s}))\frac{d}{ds}f_{i}^{y_{j}}(p_{s})\,ds\Bigg|\\
&+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Bigg|\int_{0}^{1}(f_{i}^{y_{j}}(p)-f_{i}^{y_{j}}(p_{s}))\frac{d}{ds}f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s})\,ds\Bigg|.\end{split}

The integrals in the last two sums can be estimated with the help of inequality (
3.20
) in Corollary
3.16
.
To this end, we need to estimate the semi-norm
[\,\cdot\,]_{\gamma}
i.e., the constant in the
\gamma
-Hölder continuity of

(7.5)

[0,1]\ni s\mapsto f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s})\quad\text{and}\quad[0,1]\ni s\mapsto f_{i}^{y_{j}}(p_{s}).

Using (
7.3
), we obtain that

|f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s_{1}})-f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s_{2}})|\lesssim|p_{s_{1}}-p_{s_{2}}|^{\gamma}=|s_{1}-s_{2}|^{\gamma}|q-p|^{\gamma}\quad\text{so}\quad[f_{i}^{x_{j}}(p_{s})]_{\gamma}\lesssim|q-p|^{\gamma}.

Similar estimate holds for
f_{i}^{y_{j}}
. Since
\gamma>1/2
, (
3.20
) yields

(7.6)

|\varphi(f_{i}(q),f_{i}(p))|\leq\sup_{\bar{B}}|f_{i}^{*}\upalpha|\,|q-p|+C|q-p|^{2\gamma}.

Applying uniform convergence
f_{i}^{*}\upalpha\rightrightarrows 0
on
\bar{B}
,
given
p,q\in\bar{B}
,
p\neq q
, we can find
i_{pq}
such that

(7.7)

\sup_{\bar{B}}|f_{i}^{*}\upalpha|\leq|q-p|^{2\gamma-1}\quad\text{for $i\geq i_{pq}$.}

Note that
i_{pq}
depends on the choice of
p
and
q
.
In concert, (
7.2
), (
7.3
), (
7.6
) and (
7.7
) complete the proof of (
7.1
).
∎

The next result, Theorem
7.4
, characterizes Hölder continuous maps into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
as Hölder continuous maps into
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
for which the pullback of the contact form
f^{*}\upalpha=0
equals zero in the distributional sense.
We will precede the statement with some constructions needed in the proof.

Let
\kappa\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,m\}
be a smooth
k
-form. If
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is open and
f\in C_{\rm loc}^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
,
\frac{k}{k+1}<\gamma\leq 1
, then the pullback
f^{*}\kappa
is a well defined distribution (called also a
k
-current)

(7.8)

\langle f^{*}\kappa,\phi\rangle=\int_{\Omega}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\phi\quad\text{acting on }\phi\in\Omega_{c}^{m-k}(\Omega),

see Corollary
4.5
. In particular if
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma\leq 1
, then
f^{*}\upalpha
is a well defined distribution acting on
\Omega_{c}^{m-1}(\Omega)
.

If
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma\leq 1
, then

f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
and
f^{*}\upalpha=0
in the distributional sense (
7.8
), because of Theorem
7.1
(b).
The main objective of Theorem
7.4
is the converse implication that
f^{*}\upalpha=0
(which is seemingly weaker than the condition (b) in Theorem
7.1
) along with the Hölder continuity into
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, implies that
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
.

We also need to explain the notion of approximation of the distribution
f^{*}\upalpha
by mollification.
If
\tau\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
\tau(p)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tau_{j}(p)\,dp_{j}
, then we define

\tau_{\varepsilon}(p):=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\tau_{j}*\eta_{\varepsilon})(p)\,dp_{j}.

It is easy to check that

\begin{split}\tau_{\varepsilon}(p)&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(-1)^{j-1}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\eta_{\varepsilon}(p-z)\tau(z)\wedge dz_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{dz_{j}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dz_{m}\bigg)\,dp_{j}\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\eta_{\varepsilon}(p-z)\tau(z)\wedge(*dz_{j})\bigg)\,dp_{j}.\end{split}

We used here the Hodge star operator to abbreviate notation

(-1)^{j-1}dz_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{dz_{j}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dz_{m}=*dz_{j}.

Therefore, if
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma\leq 1
and
\kappa\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, we define

(7.9)

(f^{*}\kappa)_{\varepsilon}(p):=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\eta_{\varepsilon}(p-z)(f^{*}\kappa)(z)\wedge(*dz_{j})\bigg)\,dp_{j}.

Here, the integral in understood in the distributional sense and is defined through the approximations
f_{\delta}=f*\eta_{\delta}\to f
as
\delta\to 0
.
Clearly, (
7.9
) is well defined for

p\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=\{p\in\Omega:\,\operatorname{dist}(p,\partial\Omega)>\varepsilon\},

because this guarantees that

z\mapsto\eta_{\varepsilon}(p-z)(*dz_{j})=(-1)^{j-1}\eta_{\varepsilon}(p-z)dz_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{dz_{j}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dz_{m}\in\Omega_{c}^{m-1}(\Omega).

Note that in the case of the contact form we have

(f^{*}\upalpha)_{\varepsilon}(p)=df^{t}_{\varepsilon}(p)+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(f^{x_{i}}df^{y_{i}}-f^{y_{i}}df^{x_{i}}\big)_{\varepsilon}(p),

because
(df^{t})_{\varepsilon}=df^{t}_{\varepsilon}
. Indeed,

\begin{split}(df^{t})_{\varepsilon}(p)&=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\eta_{\varepsilon}(p-z)d(f^{t}*\eta_{\delta})(z)\wedge(*dz_{j})\bigg)\,dp_{j}.\\
&=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\partial_{j}\big(f^{t}*\eta_{\delta}\big)*\eta_{\varepsilon}(p)\,dp_{j}=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\partial_{j}\big(f^{t}*\eta_{\varepsilon}\big)*\eta_{\delta}(p)\,dp_{j}=df_{\varepsilon}^{t}(p).\end{split}

The proof of the following result employs arguments similar to those used in the proof of easier (affirmative) direction of the Onsager conjecture
[
15
]
.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma\leq 1
, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be open, and let
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
. Then
f\in C^{0,\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
if and only if
f^{*}\upalpha=0
in the distributional sense i.e.,

\langle f^{*}\upalpha,\phi\rangle=\int_{\Omega}f^{*}\upalpha\wedge\phi=0\quad\text{for all }\phi\in\Omega_{c}^{m-1}(\Omega).

Remark 7.5
.

Let us stress that for Hölder maps, if we replace
f^{*}\upalpha=0
in the distributional sense, by the condition
f^{*}\upalpha=0
in the pointwise a.e. sense, we get nothing. Indeed, for any
0<\gamma<1
, Salem
[
62
]
, constructed a surjective homeomorphism
g\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
that is differentiable a.e. and satisfies
g^{\prime}=0
a.e. Thus,

f(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},t)=(g(x_{1}),g(y_{1}),\ldots,g(x_{n}),g(y_{n}),g(t)),\quad f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1};\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})

is a surjective homeomorphism that is differentiable a.e. and satisfies
Df=0
a.e. Hence,
f^{*}\upalpha=0
a.e. On the other hand
f\not\in C^{0,\frac{1}{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1};\mathbb{H}^{n})
, because
g
is not locally Lipschitz continuous and
d_{K}\big((z,t),(z,t^{\prime})\big)=|t-t^{\prime}|^{1/2}
.

Another, more complicated construction
[
36
, Theorem 3.2]
, gives a continuous function
u:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
that is differentiable a.e., and such that the tangent spaces to the graph of
u
(the tangent spaces exist a.e.) are horizontal a.e. That is, the parametrization of the graph
f(x,y):=(x,y,u(x,y))
satisfies
f^{*}\upalpha=0
a.e. Moreover,
u
can have modulus of continuity arbitrarily close to the Lipschitz one.

See also
[
9
,
10
,
12
,
26
,
41
,
53
]
for rather difficult constructions of homeomorphisms with Sobolev regularity that have derivatives of low rank. Some of these examples were obtained using convex integration.

Proof of
Theorem

7.4
.

The implication
\Rightarrow
, as previously explained, follows straightforwardly from Theorem
7.1
(b). Therefore, it remains to prove the implications
\Leftarrow
.
In view of Theorem
7.1
it suffices to show that

f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\upalpha=(f*\eta_{\varepsilon})^{*}\upalpha\rightrightarrows 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly on every

\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega
.

Fix
\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega
and take
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(\Omega^{\prime},\partial\Omega)
. We can
find
\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega^{\prime\prime}\Subset\Omega
such that
\operatorname{dist}(\Omega^{\prime},\partial\Omega^{\prime\prime})>\varepsilon
.
We fix such an
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
.

Since
f^{*}\upalpha=0
, it follows that
(f^{*}\upalpha)_{\varepsilon}(p)=0
for all
p\in\Omega^{\prime}
. Therefore, for
p\in\Omega^{\prime}
we have

(7.10)

\begin{split}&(f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha)(p)=(f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha)(p)-(f^{*}\upalpha)_{\varepsilon}(p)\\
&=2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(f_{\varepsilon}^{x_{i}}(p)df_{\varepsilon}^{y_{i}}(p)-(f^{x_{i}}df^{y_{i}})_{\varepsilon}(p)\big)-2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(f_{\varepsilon}^{y_{i}}(p)df_{\varepsilon}^{x_{i}}(p)-(f^{y_{i}}df^{x_{i}})_{\varepsilon}(p)\big).\end{split}

Note that the terms involving
f^{t}
canceled out, because
(df^{t})_{\varepsilon}(p)=df_{\varepsilon}^{t}(p)
.
We will estimate the terms in the first sum only, because the estimates of the second sum are essentially the same. We have

\begin{split}&f_{\varepsilon}^{x_{i}}(p)df_{\varepsilon}^{y_{i}}(p)-(f^{x_{i}}df^{y_{i}})_{\varepsilon}(p)\\
&=\big((f_{\varepsilon}^{x_{i}}(p)-f^{x_{i}}(p))df_{\varepsilon}^{y_{i}}(p)\big)+\big((f^{x_{i}}(p)df_{\varepsilon}^{y_{i}}(p)-(f^{x_{i}}df^{y_{i}})_{\varepsilon}(p)\big)=A(p)+B(p).\end{split}

The following estimate of
A(p)
easily follows from the definition of convolution (see (
2.12
) and (
2.23
))

\begin{split}|A(p)|&=|f_{\varepsilon}^{x_{i}}(p)-f^{x_{i}}(p)|\,|df_{\varepsilon}^{y_{i}}(p)|\\
&\leq\varepsilon^{\gamma}[f^{x_{i}}]_{\gamma,B(p,\varepsilon)}\cdot\varepsilon^{\gamma-1}[f^{y_{i}}]_{\gamma,B(p,\varepsilon)}\|\nabla\eta\|_{1}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}[f]^{2}_{\gamma,B(p,\varepsilon)}.\end{split}

It remains to estimate
B(p)
. We have

\begin{split}&B(p)=f^{x_{i}}(p)df_{\varepsilon}^{y_{i}}(p)-(f^{x_{i}}df^{y_{i}})_{\varepsilon}(p)=f^{x_{i}}(p)(df^{y_{i}})_{\varepsilon}(p)-(f^{x_{i}}df^{y_{i}})_{\varepsilon}(p)\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\bigg(\int_{B(p,\varepsilon)}\eta_{\varepsilon}(p-z)\big(f^{x_{i}}(p)df^{y_{i}}(z)-f^{x_{i}}(z)df^{y_{i}}(z)\big)\wedge(*dz_{j})\bigg)\,dp_{j}.\end{split}

We could restrict this (generalized, distributional) integral to
B(p,\varepsilon)
, because

\eta_{\varepsilon}(p-\cdot)
is supported in
B(p,\varepsilon)
. For simplicity of notation assume that
p=0
. We will apply a change of variables to rescale the integral to
\mathbb{B}^{m}=B(0,1)
. Note that the (distributional) exterior derivative with respect to
z
satisfies

df^{y_{i}}(\varepsilon z)=\varepsilon^{-1}d(f^{y_{i}}(\varepsilon z)-f^{y_{i}}(0)).

Therefore, for
p=0
we have

|B(0)|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\varepsilon^{-1}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{m}}\underbrace{\big(f^{x_{i}}(0)-f^{x_{1}}(\varepsilon z)\big)\eta(-z)(*dz_{j})}_{\lambda}\wedge d\underbrace{\big(f^{y_{i}}(\varepsilon z)-f^{y_{i}}(0)\big)}_{\gamma_{1}}\bigg|

Now Corollary
3.11
with
k=1
,
\ell=m-1
,
\ell_{1}=1
and
\alpha=\beta=\gamma
yields

|B(0)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}\|\lambda\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\|\gamma_{1}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot\varepsilon^{\gamma}[f^{x_{i}}]_{\gamma,B_{\varepsilon}}\cdot\varepsilon^{\gamma}[f^{y_{i}}]_{\gamma,B_{\varepsilon}}\leq\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}[f]^{2}_{\gamma,B_{\varepsilon}}.

We used there the fact that if
g(0)=0
, then

\|g\|_{C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}\leq 2[g]_{\gamma,\mathbb{B}^{m}}
, and we used Lemma
2.17
to take care of
\eta(-z)
in the estimate of
\lambda
.

For any
p\in\Omega^{\prime}
the above estimate reads as

|B(p)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}[f]^{2}_{\gamma,B(p,\varepsilon)}
.
In concert, the estimates for
A(p)
and
B(p)
and similar estimates for the second sum in (
7.10
) yield

\big|(f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha)(p)\big|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}[f]^{2}_{\gamma,B(p,\varepsilon)}\leq\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}[f]^{2}_{\gamma,\Omega^{\prime\prime}}\quad\text{whenever }p\in\Omega^{\prime}.

Therefore
f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\rightrightarrows 0
uniformly on
\Omega^{\prime}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. The proof is complete.
∎

7.2.
Hölder continuous horizontal curves

If
\gamma
is an
\alpha
-Hölder continuous curve defined on
[0,1]
with values in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
or
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, then we can assume that
\gamma
is defined on
\mathbb{R}
since we can extend it to a curve that is constant in
(-\infty,0]
and in
[1,\infty)
. Clearly, the extended curve is also
\alpha
-Hölder continuous. The extension of
\gamma
to
\mathbb{R}
allows us to define the
\varepsilon
-mollification
\gamma_{\varepsilon}(t)
for all
t\in[0,1]
.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
be a curve and
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
. Then
\gamma\in C^{0,\alpha}([0,1];\mathbb{H}^{n})
if and only if

\pi\circ\gamma\in C^{0,\alpha}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, and

(7.11)

\gamma^{t}(b)-\gamma^{t}(a)=-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{a}^{b}\gamma^{x_{j}}d\gamma^{y_{j}}-\gamma^{y_{j}}d\gamma^{x_{j}}\quad\text{for all }0\leq a<b\leq 1,

where the integral in (
7.11
) is understood in the sense of (
3.13
). Moreover,

d_{K}(\gamma(b),\gamma(a))\lesssim_{n,\alpha}|b-a|^{\alpha}[\pi\circ\gamma]_{\alpha}\quad\text{for all }0\leq a<b\leq 1.

Remark 7.7
.

Identity (
7.11
) is an extension of (
6.2
) beyond the class of differentiable curves. The result completely fails if
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2}
since according to
Lemma

6.9
, any smooth curve
\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is
C^{0,1/2}
-Hölder continuous as a mapping into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

Remark 7.8
.

The implication from left to right was proved in
[
47
, Lemma 3.1]
.

Proof.

Assume that
\gamma\in C^{0,\alpha}([0,1];\mathbb{H}^{n})
. Clearly,
\pi\circ\gamma\in C^{0,\alpha}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.
Let
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
be an
\varepsilon
-mollification of
\gamma
. By
Theorem

7.1
(b),

\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha=d\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{t}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{x_{j}}d\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{y_{j}}-\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{y_{j}}d\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{x_{j}})\rightrightarrows 0\quad\text{uniformly on $[0,1]$.}

Integrating this form on
[a,b]
, letting
\varepsilon\to 0
and applying
Proposition

3.13
, yields (
7.11
).

Suppose now that
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
satisfies

\pi\circ\gamma\in C^{0,\alpha}
and (
7.11
). We will prove that
\gamma\in C^{0,\alpha}([0,1];\mathbb{H}^{n})
. In order to do so, we need to estimate (cf. (
6.6
))

d_{K}(\gamma(b),\gamma(a))\approx|\pi(\gamma(b))-\pi(\gamma(a))|+|\varphi(\gamma(b),\gamma(a))|^{1/2}.

Clearly,

(7.12)

|\pi(\gamma(b))-\pi(\gamma(a))|\leq|b-a|^{\alpha}[\pi\circ\gamma]_{\alpha},

so it remains to estimate

(7.13)

\varphi(\gamma(b),\gamma(a))=\gamma^{t}(b)-\gamma^{t}(a)+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\gamma^{x_{j}}(a)\gamma^{y_{j}}(b)-\gamma^{x_{j}}(b)\gamma^{y_{j}}(a)).

If we rewrite (
7.11
) as

0=\gamma^{t}(b)-\gamma^{t}(a)-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{a}^{b}\gamma^{y_{j}}d\gamma^{x_{j}}-\gamma^{x_{j}}d\gamma^{y_{j}},

and subtract it from (
7.13
), we have

(7.14)

\varphi(\gamma(b),\gamma(a))=2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{a}^{b}(\gamma^{y_{j}}-\gamma^{y_{j}}(a))d\gamma^{x_{j}}-(\gamma^{x_{j}}-\gamma^{x_{j}}(a))d\gamma^{y_{j}},

and (
3.20
) in
Corollary

3.16
yield

|\varphi(\gamma(b),\gamma(a))|\lesssim_{n,\alpha}|b-a|^{2\alpha}[\pi\circ\gamma]^{2}_{\alpha},

which together with (
7.12
) implies that

d_{K}(\gamma(b),\gamma(a))\lesssim_{n,\alpha}|b-a|^{\alpha}[\pi\circ\gamma]_{\alpha}.

We used here (
3.20
) which was proved for Lipschitz functions, but it applies to (
7.14
), because the integral in (
7.14
) is defined through smooth approximations.
∎

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
be an
\alpha
-Hölder continuous curve for some
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
. Then the change in height along
\gamma
equals the sum of the oriented areas enclosed by the closed curves
\bar{\gamma}_{j}
(see
Definition

6.5
):

\gamma^{t}(b)-\gamma^{t}(a)=-4\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\bar{\gamma}_{j},z)\,dz.

In particular, if
\gamma
is closed i.e.,
\gamma(a)=\gamma(b)
, the sum or areas enclosed by the closed curves
\gamma_{j}
equals zero

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\gamma_{j},z)\,dz=0.

Proof.

Theorem

7.6
and
Corollary

5.27
yield (cf. (
6.3
) and (
6.4
))

\begin{split}\gamma^{t}(b)-\gamma^{t}(a)&=-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{a}^{b}\gamma^{x_{j}}d\gamma^{y_{j}}-\gamma^{y_{j}}d\gamma^{x_{j}}\\
&=-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{a-1}^{b+1}\bar{\gamma}^{x_{j}}d\bar{\gamma}^{y_{j}}-\bar{\gamma}^{y_{j}}d\bar{\gamma}^{x_{j}}=-4\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\bar{\gamma}_{j},z)\,dz.\end{split}

∎

The next result shows that the
x,y
components of a Hölder map uniquely determine the map up to a vertical translation.

Corollary 7.10
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a domain and let
f,g\in C^{0,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
. If
\pi\circ f=\pi\circ g
i.e.,

f^{x_{j}}=g^{x_{j}}
and
f^{y_{j}}=g^{y_{j}}
for
j=1,2,\ldots,n
, then there is a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(7.15)

g^{t}(x)=f^{t}(x)+c\quad\text{for all }x\in\Omega.

Proof.

Fix
x_{o}\in\Omega
. Any
x\in\Omega
can be connected with
x_{o}
by a smooth curve
\eta:[0,1]\to\Omega
,
\eta(0)=x_{o}
,
\eta(1)=x
. The curves
\gamma_{f}:=f\circ\eta
and
\gamma_{g}:=g\circ\eta
are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous. Observe that
\gamma_{f}^{x_{j}}=\gamma_{g}^{x_{j}}
and
\gamma_{f}^{y_{j}}=\gamma_{g}^{y_{j}}
and we will simply write
\gamma^{x_{j}}
and
\gamma^{y_{j}}
. Now, (
7.11
) yields

f^{t}(x)-f^{t}(x_{o})=\gamma_{f}^{t}(1)-\gamma_{f}^{t}(0)=-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\gamma^{x_{j}}d\gamma^{y_{j}}-\gamma^{y_{j}}d\gamma^{x_{j}}=g^{t}(x)-g^{t}(x_{o}),

so (
7.15
) is satisfied with
c:=g^{t}(x_{o})-f^{t}(x_{o})
.
∎

7.3.
Lifting of Hölder continuous mappings

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be an open domain. A smooth map
F:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is called
Legendrian
or
horizontal
if
F^{*}\upalpha=0
i.e., if
F
maps vectors tangent to
\Omega
to horizontal vectors in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. We have seen in
Proposition

6.12
that every smooth
F
that belongs to
C^{0,\frac{1}{2}+}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
is Legendrian.

Let
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, be a smooth mapping. A natural question is to find conditions for the existence of a smooth function
\tau:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that the mapping

F=(f,\tau):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
is Legendrian. The mapping
F
is called a
Legendrian
or
horizontal lift
of
f
.
Note that if a Legendrian lift exists,
\tau
is uniquely determined up to an additive constant.
As we will see this problem has a very simple solution.

Let

\beta=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(x_{j}dy_{j}-y_{j}dx_{j})\quad\text{so}\quad\upalpha=dt+2\beta.

Then, the condition
F^{*}\upalpha=0
is equivalent to
d\tau+2f^{*}\beta=0
, so existence of
\tau
is equivalent to the condition that the from
f^{*}\beta
is exact. In particular
f^{*}\beta
must be closed, which is equivalent to

f^{*}\upomega=\frac{1}{2}f^{*}(d\beta)=\frac{1}{2}df^{*}\beta=0.

A smooth map
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfying
f^{*}\upomega=0
is called
Lagrangian
. Thus, a necessary condition for the existence of a Legendrian lift is that
f
is Lagrangian. However, if
\Omega
is simply connected, this condition is also sufficient. We proved

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
be a smooth map defined on a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Then, the following conditions are equivalent.

(a)

There is a horizontal lift
F=(f,\tau):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
of
f
,

(b)

The form
f^{*}\beta
is exact.

If in addition the domain
\Omega
is simply connected, we have one more equivalent condition:

(c)

f^{*}\upomega=0
.

This result is well known and our purpose is to generalize it to the case of Hölder continuous maps
f\in C^{0,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2n})
,
1/2<\alpha\leq 1
.

Recall that if
f
is smooth, then the
1
-form
f^{*}\beta
is exact if and only if

(7.16)

\int_{\eta}f^{*}\beta=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\eta^{*}(f^{*}\beta)=0\quad\text{for every piecewise smooth curve $\eta:\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\Omega$.}

Indeed, if
f^{*}\beta=-\frac{1}{2}d\tau
, then
\eta^{*}(f^{*}\beta)=-\frac{1}{2}d(\tau\circ\eta)
so the integral at (
7.16
) equals zero. On the other hand equality (
7.16
)
shows that if
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}:[0,1]\to\Omega
are piecewise smooth curves with equal endpoints

\eta_{1}(0)=\eta_{2}(0)
,
\eta_{1}(1)=\eta_{2}(1)
, then

\int_{\eta_{1}}f^{*}\beta=\int_{\eta_{2}}f^{*}\beta,\quad\text{and we can define}\quad\tau(x):=-2\int_{\eta_{x}}f^{*}\beta,

where
\eta_{x}
is any piecewise smooth curve connecting a fixed point
x_{o}\in\Omega
with
x\in\Omega
. Clearly,
f^{*}\beta=-\frac{1}{2}d\tau
.

Since we can identify
\eta:\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\Omega
with a curve
\eta:[0,1]\to\Omega
such that
\eta(0)=\eta(1)
, the reasoning presented above shows that
condition (b) in
Proposition

7.11
is equivalent to:

For every piecewise smooth closed curve
\eta:[0,1]\to\Omega
,
\eta(0)=\eta(1)
, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\gamma^{x_{j}}d\gamma^{y_{j}}-\gamma^{y_{j}}d\gamma^{x_{j}}=0,\quad\text{where }\gamma=f\circ\eta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

Note that the integrals in the above formula are well defined if
f\in C^{0,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2n})
,
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
, see
Proposition

3.13
.

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
f\in C^{0,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is an open domain and
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
. Then, the following conditions are equivalent

(a)

There is a function
\tau:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that

F=(f,\tau)\in C^{0,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
,

(b)

For every piecewise smooth closed curve

\eta:[0,1]\to\Omega
,
\eta(0)=\eta(1)
,

(7.17)

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\gamma^{x_{j}}d\gamma^{y_{j}}-\gamma^{y_{j}}d\gamma^{x_{j}}=0,\quad\text{where }\gamma=f\circ\eta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n},

and the integral is understood as in
Proposition

3.13
.

(c)

For every piecewise smooth closed curve

\eta:[0,1]\to\Omega
,
\eta(0)=\eta(1)
,

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\gamma_{j},z)\,dz=0,\quad\text{where }\gamma_{j}=(f^{x_{j}},f^{y_{j}})\circ\eta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Proof.

The result is an easy consequence of
Theorem

7.6
.
First observe that the equivalence of conditions (b) and (c) follows from
Corollary

5.27
and it remains to prove equivalence of (a) and (b).

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b). Since
f
has a horizontal lift
F=(f,\tau)
,
\Gamma=F\circ\eta
is a horizontal lift of
\gamma=f\circ\eta
. Since the curve
\Gamma
is closed, (
7.11
) implies (
7.17
).

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a).
Fix
x_{o}\in\Omega
. Any
x\in\Omega
can be connected by a piecewise smooth curve
\eta:[0,1]\to\Omega
,
\eta(0)=x_{o}
,
\eta(1)=x

and we define

\tau(x):=-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\gamma^{x_{j}}d\gamma^{y_{j}}-\gamma^{y_{j}}d\gamma^{x_{j}},\quad\text{where }\gamma=f\circ\eta:[0,1]\to\Omega.

Condition (
7.17
) guarantees that the integral that defines
\tau
does not depend on the choice of the curve
\eta
. Since
\eta|_{[0,t]}
connects
x_{o}
to
\eta(t)
, it follows that

\tau(\eta(t))=-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\gamma^{x_{j}}d\gamma^{y_{j}}-\gamma^{y_{j}}d\gamma^{x_{j}},

and hence for
0\leq a<b\leq 1
we have

\tau(\eta(b))-\tau(\eta(a))=-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{a}^{b}\gamma^{x_{j}}d\gamma^{y_{j}}-\gamma^{y_{j}}d\gamma^{x_{j}}.

Theorem

7.6
implies that the curve
(f,\tau)\circ\eta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
belongs to
C^{0,\alpha}([0,1];\mathbb{H}^{n})
. The Hölder continuity comes with estimates in terms of Hölder continuity of
f
.
This true for every piecewise smooth
\eta
. In particular it shows that
(f,\tau)
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous on any interval in
\Omega
and it follows that
(f,\tau)\in C^{0,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
. ∎

7.4.
Pullbacks of differential forms by Hölder mappings

In
Lemma

7.13
and
Corollary

7.14
,

f_{\varepsilon}=f*\eta_{\varepsilon}
stands for the
\varepsilon
-mollification of
f
as described in
Definition

2.31
.
Recall that the quantities
[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon,A}
and
[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon}
were defined in
Definition

2.16
.

Let us start with a simple estimate for a pullback for a
k
-form under an approximation of a Hölder continuous map.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is open,
B(x_{o},2r)\subset\Omega
, and
\gamma\in(0,1]
.
If
\kappa\in\Omega^{k}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa\|_{L^{\infty}(B^{m}(x_{o},r))}\lesssim\|\kappa\|_{\infty}[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon,B^{m}(x_{o},2r)}^{k}\varepsilon^{-k(1-\gamma)}\quad\text{for all $0<\varepsilon<r$,}

where the constant in the inequality depends on
m
,
d
,
k
, and
\eta
only.

Proof.

Let
\kappa=\sum_{|I|=k}\psi_{I}\,dy_{I}=\sum_{|I|=k}\psi_{I}\,dy_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{i_{k}}
. Then,

f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa=\sum_{|I|=k}(\psi_{I}\circ f_{\varepsilon})\,df_{\varepsilon}^{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{\varepsilon}^{i_{k}},\quad\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|\kappa\|_{\infty}\|Df_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{k}.

Recall that (see (
2.23
))

Df_{\varepsilon}(x)=\varepsilon^{-m-1}\int_{B^{m}(0,\varepsilon)}(f(x-z)-f(x))D\eta\left(\frac{z}{\varepsilon}\right)\,dz

so for
0<\varepsilon<r
we have

(7.18)

\|Df_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(B(x_{o},r))}\leq\varepsilon^{-(1-\gamma)}[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon,B^{m}(x_{o},2r)}\|D\eta\|_{1}.

and the lemma follows.
∎

If
f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
open, then by Lemmata
6.9
and
6.11

(7.19)

|f(p)-f(q)|\leq C(n,\|f\|_{\infty})d_{K}(f(p),f(q))\leq C(n,\|f\|_{\infty})[f]_{\gamma}\,|p-q|^{\gamma}.

Therefore,
Lemma

7.13
yields

Corollary 7.14
.

Let
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}^{n})
, where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is open,
B^{m}(x_{o},2r)\subset\Omega
, and
\gamma\in(0,1]
.
If
\kappa\in\Omega^{k}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
, then

\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa\|_{L^{\infty}(B^{m}(x_{o},r))}\lesssim\|\kappa\|_{\infty}[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon,B^{m}(x_{o},2r)}^{k}\varepsilon^{-k(1-\gamma)}\quad\text{for all $0<\varepsilon<r$,}

where the constant in the inequality depends on
k
,
m
,
n
,
\eta
and
\|f\|_{\infty}
only.

For
0<\gamma<1
, the estimate in
Corollary

7.14
blows up as
\varepsilon\to 0
, but the speed of the blow-up depends the exponent of Hölder continuity:
the blow-up is slower if
\gamma
is closer to
1
.
On the other hand, the next result shows that in the case of a pullback of the contact form, we get a much better estimate. In particular if
\gamma>1/2
, there is no blow-up and the estimate converges to zero (see also
Theorem

7.2
). Thus, is some generalized sense, Hölder continuous mappings with
\gamma>1/2

preserve horizontality. This fact will play a crucial role in the remaining part of the paper.

Corollary 7.15
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and compact
m
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with or without boundary. If
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
, then there is a smooth approximation

C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})\ni f_{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}f\quad\text{in}\quad C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})\ \text{for all $0<\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma$}

such that

\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa\|_{\infty}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-k(1-\gamma)}[f]^{k}_{\gamma,\varepsilon}\quad\text{for all}\ \kappa\in\Omega^{k}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\right),\ 1\leq k\leq 2n+1,

and

\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\|_{\infty}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}[f]^{2}_{\gamma,\varepsilon},\quad\text{when $\upalpha$ is the standard contact form.}

Here, we use notation
[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon}=[f]_{\gamma,\varepsilon,\mathcal{M}}
.

Remark 7.16
.

We do not need to assume that
\kappa
is bounded (cf.
Corollary

7.14
), because
f
is bounded and
\kappa
is bounded in a neighborhood of the image of
f
. Note also that the implied constants in the estimates in
Corollary

7.15
depend on
\|f\|_{\infty}

Remark 7.17
.

The construction of
f_{\varepsilon}
is similar to the construction of an approximation in
Corollary

2.34
.

Proof.

Assume first that
\partial\mathcal{M}=\varnothing
.
Using the Whitney embedding theorem we may assume that
\mathcal{M}
is smoothly embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{2m+1}
. The new Riemannian metric on
\mathcal{M}
inherited from the embedding into
\mathbb{R}^{2m+1}
will be comparable to the original one due to compactness on
\mathcal{M}
.

According to the tubular neighborhood theorem, there is an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2m+1}
containing
\mathcal{M}
such that the nearest point projection
\pi:\Omega\to\mathcal{M}
is uniquely defined, smooth and Lipschitz. Then

\tilde{f}=f\circ\pi\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega,\mathbb{H}^{n}).

Let
\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega
be an open set that contains
\mathcal{M}
and let

\varepsilon_{o}=\operatorname{dist}(\Omega^{\prime},\partial\Omega)
. Let
\tilde{f}_{\varepsilon}
be the standard approximation by mollification. It is well defined on
\Omega^{\prime}
(and hence on
\mathcal{M}
) for
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{o}
.
Since
\kappa
is bounded in a neighborhood of
\tilde{f}(\Omega)=f(\mathcal{M})
,

Corollary

7.14
yields

\|\tilde{f}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})}\leq\|\tilde{f}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-k(1-\gamma)}[\tilde{f}]^{k}_{\gamma,\varepsilon,\Omega}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-k(1-\gamma)}[f]^{k}_{\gamma,\varepsilon}\quad\text{for }0<\varepsilon<\frac{\varepsilon_{o}}{2}\,.

Similarly,
Theorem

7.2
yields

\|\tilde{f}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}[f]^{2}_{\gamma,\varepsilon},

Taking
f_{\varepsilon}:=\tilde{f}_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathcal{M}}
for
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{o}
, yields the result since according to
Proposition

2.32
, for all
0<\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
we have
\tilde{f}_{\varepsilon}\to\tilde{f}
in
C^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}(\Omega^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

If
\partial\mathcal{M}\neq\varnothing
, we glue two copies of
\mathcal{M}
along the boundary to obtain a closed manifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
, extend
f
to a mapping in
C^{0,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\mathbb{H}^{n})
(by reflection) and we
apply the above argument to
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
.
∎

As an application of
Corollary

7.15
we will prove the following important result.

Theorem 7.18
.

Let
m>n
be positive integers. Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and compact
m
-dimensional manifold with or without boundary, and let

f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{H}^{n})
, where
\gamma\in(\frac{k}{k+1},1]
,
n<k\leq m
.
Then, for any
\kappa\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
we have

(7.20)

\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau=0\quad\text{for all}\ \tau\in\Omega^{m-k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}\left(\mathcal{M}\right).

Remark 7.19
.

The ‘integral’ in (
7.20
) is understood in the distributional sense
as a limit of smooth approximations, see (
7.21
).
In the case in which
f
,
\kappa
and
\tau
are smooth, equality (
7.20
) implies that
f^{*}\kappa=0
for all smooth
k
forms
\kappa
(cf.
[
38
, Lemma 4.3]
) which, in turn, is equivalent to
\operatorname{rank}Df<k
. Thus, in some sense
Theorem

7.18
says that under the given assumptions, a generalized rank of the derivative of
f
is less than
k
even though it does not make any sense to talk about the derivative of
f
.

Proof.

According to
Theorem

4.1
(with
\alpha=1
), the limit

(7.21)

\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\kappa\wedge\tau

exists and does not depend on a choice of an approximation

C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})\ni f_{\varepsilon}\to f
if
f_{\varepsilon}\to f
in
C^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}
,
\gamma^{\prime}\in(\frac{k}{k+1},\gamma]
. In what follows we take
f_{\varepsilon}
to be the approximation of
f
from
Corollary

7.15
.

Assume first that
\tau\in\Omega^{m-k}(\mathcal{M})
. According to
Corollary

2.15
, we have a decomposition

\kappa=\upalpha\wedge\beta+d\upalpha\wedge\delta\quad\text{for some}\quad\beta\in\Omega^{k-1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\right),\ \delta\in\Omega^{k-2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\right).

A change of the order in the wedge product with respect to that in
Corollary

2.15
is not essential; it will make our estimates easier to write. Also we use
\delta
instead of
\gamma
because
\gamma
is reserved for the exponent of the Hölder continuity.

Stokes’ theorem yields (there is no boundary term if
\partial\mathcal{M}=\varnothing
)

\int_{\mathcal{M}}df_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\delta\wedge\tau=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\wedge d(f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\delta\wedge\tau)+\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\delta\wedge\tau.

Therefore,

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathcal{M}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\beta\wedge\tau+df_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\delta\wedge\tau\\
&=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\wedge\big(f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\beta+d\delta)\wedge\tau+(-1)^{k}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\delta\wedge d\tau\big)+\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\delta\wedge\tau.\end{split}

According to
Corollary

7.15

\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\|_{\infty}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1},\quad\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\beta+d\delta)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-(k-1)(1-\gamma)},\quad\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\delta\|_{\infty}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-(k-2)(1-\gamma)}.

Hence,

\left|\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\kappa\wedge\tau\right|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}\big(\varepsilon^{-(k-1)(1-\gamma)}+\varepsilon^{-(k-2)(1-\gamma)}\big)=\varepsilon^{(k+1)\gamma-k}\big(1+\varepsilon^{1-\gamma}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\to 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}0,

because
\gamma>\frac{k}{k+1}
.

In the general case when
\tau\in\Omega^{m-k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\mathcal{M})
, we approximate
\tau
smoothly in
C^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}
,
\gamma^{\prime}\in(\frac{k}{k+1},\gamma)
,
C^{\infty}\ni\tau_{\varepsilon}\to\tau
, and we have

0=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau_{\varepsilon}\to\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau,

where the convergence follows from the fact that distributional integral against
f^{*}\kappa
defines a bounded functional on
\Omega^{m-k}C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M})
, see
(
4.3
) (with
\alpha=1
and
\gamma
replaced by
\gamma^{\prime}
).
∎

Corollary 7.20
.

There is no topological embedding
f:\mathbb{S}^{2n}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
such that

f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{2n},\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\gamma>\frac{2n}{2n+1}
.

Remark 7.21
.

In the next section we will prove a much deeper non-embedding theorem,
Theorem

8.2
.

Proof.

Suppose to the contrary that such an embedding exists.
Since
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{2n},\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
by
Lemma

6.9
, it follows from
Corollary

5.23
that

(7.22)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}}f_{1}\,df_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{2n+1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}}w(f,y)\,dy\neq 0,

where the right hand side is non-zero since it equals
\pm
the volume of the region bounded by
f(\mathbb{S}^{2n})
(the sign depends on the orientation of
f
). On the other hand
Theorem

7.18
with

\kappa=y_{1}dy_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dy_{2n+1}\in\Omega^{2n}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau=1

yields

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}}f_{1}\,df_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge df_{2n+1}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}}f^{*}\kappa\wedge\tau=0

which is a contradiction.
∎

In the next section we will need the following

Corollary 7.22
.

Let
m>n
be positive integers. Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented and compact
m
-dimensional manifold with boundary, and let

f\in C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma}(\overline{\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{H}^{n})
, where
\gamma\in(\frac{m}{m+1},1]
.
Then, for any
\kappa\in\Omega^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
we have

\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}(d\kappa)=0.

Proof.

First, note that by
Theorem

4.1
with
\tau=1
, both of the integrals

\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa
and
\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}(d\kappa)
are well defined and independent of a smooth approximations in

C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}(\partial{\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
,
\frac{m-1}{m}<\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma

and

C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
,
\frac{m}{m+1}<\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma
respectively.

Let
f_{\varepsilon}
be a
C^{\infty}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
approximation of
f
in
C_{\rm b}^{0,\gamma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
,
\gamma^{\prime}\in(\frac{m}{m+1},\gamma]
. Since
\gamma^{\prime}>\frac{m}{m+1}>\frac{m-1}{m}
, we have that
f_{\varepsilon}|_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
is also a good approximation for the integral
\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa
.
Therefore, Stokes’ theorem and
Theorem

7.18
with
\tau=1
yields

\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\kappa\leftarrow\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(d\kappa)\rightarrow\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}(d\kappa)=0.

The proof is complete.
∎

8.
Non-embedding theorem of Gromov

Gromov
[
32
, 3.1.A]
proved the following non-embedding result:

Theorem 8.1
.

Every
\gamma
-Hölder continuous embedding
f:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
,
m\geq n+1
, satisfies
\gamma\leq\frac{n+1}{n+2}
.

The original proof is quite challenging as it relies on the
h
-principle and microflexibility. For a more detailed explanation of this proof, as well as an alternative approach using the Rumin complex
[
61
]
, see
[
58
]
. Additionally, there is another proof by Züst
[
76
]
that shows the non-existence of an embedding
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H}^{1})
for
\gamma>2/3
. This proof is based on a factorization through a topological tree. However, this argument cannot be extended to the general case of
Theorem

8.1
.
We should also mention here the work of Balogh, Kozhevnikov, and Pansu
[
3
]
described in the introduction.
The aim of this section is to present an elementary proof of a more general result,
Theorem

8.2
, originally proved in
[
37
]
.

Let us compare
Theorem

8.1
with
Corollary

7.20
. First of all, the bound for the exponent obtained in
Corollary

7.20
is larger than
\frac{n+1}{n+2}
. Both bounds are equal only when
n=1
. However, even in the case
n=1
,
Corollary

7.20
proves non-existence of an embedding
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{2},\mathbb{H}^{1})
,
\gamma>2/3
, and it is crucial for the proof that
\mathbb{S}^{2}
is a closed surface since the argument is based on the fact that the image of
\mathbb{S}^{2}

bounds a region in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of positive volume.

Theorem 8.2
.

Suppose that
m\geq n+1
,

\frac{1}{2}\leq\gamma\leq\frac{n+1}{n+2}\quad\text{and}\quad\theta=\frac{n+1}{n}-\frac{2\gamma}{n}.

Then, there does not exist a map

f\in C^{0,\gamma+}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{m};\mathbb{H}^{n})\cap C^{0,\theta}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{m};\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})

or a map

f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{m};\mathbb{H}^{n})\cap C^{0,\theta+}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{m};\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}),

such that
f|_{\mathbb{S}^{m-1}}
is a topological embedding.

Remark 8.3
.

If
\gamma\in(\frac{n+1}{n+2},1]
and
\theta=\frac{n+1}{n}-\frac{2\gamma}{n}
, then
Theorem

8.2
is still true, because it follows from the case
\gamma=\frac{n+1}{n+2}
(see
Corollary

8.4
). Therefore, including the range
\gamma\in(\frac{n+1}{n+2},1]
in
Theorem

8.2
would not provide any new information.

Corollary 8.4
.

If
m\geq n+1
, then there does not exist a mapping

(8.1)

f\in C^{0,\frac{n+1}{n+2}}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{m};\mathbb{H}^{n})\cap C^{0,\frac{n+1}{n+2}+}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{m};\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}).

such that
f|_{\mathbb{S}^{m-1}}
is a topological embedding. In particular, there is no topological embedding of
\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{m}
into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
of the class (
8.1
)

Proof.

If
\gamma=\frac{n+1}{n+2}
, then
\theta=\frac{n+1}{n+2}
.
∎

Remark 8.5
.

Since
C^{0,\gamma}\subset C^{0,\frac{n+1}{n+2}+}
for
\gamma>\frac{n+1}{n+2}
,
Theorem

8.1
follows from
Corollary

8.4
.

If
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
, then
\theta=1
and hence we have the following result which was proved in

[
2
, Theorem 1.11]
:

Corollary 8.6
.

Suppose that
m\geq n+1
. Then there does not exist an embedding

f\in C^{0,\frac{1}{2}+}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{m};\mathbb{H}^{n})\cap C^{0,1}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{m};\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}).

Remark 8.7
.

In some sense, we can regard
Theorem

8.2
as an interpolation of
Corollary

8.4
and
Corollary

8.6
.

In order to prove
Theorem

8.2
we need to construct a certain differential form
\kappa
. This is done in
Section

8.1
.
As a straightforward application of
Corollary

7.22
to this form
\kappa
, in
Section

8.2
, we present a very short proof of Gromov’s
Theorem

8.1
, a special case of
Theorem

8.2
. The proof of
Theorem

8.2
is based on the same idea, but it is more complicated and it is presented in
Section

8.3
.

8.1.
Alexander duality

The content of this section is based on
[
37
]
. We included it for the sake of completeness.
The main result of this section is
Theorem

8.10
. While neither the statement nor the proof requires understanding of topology, the argument in the proof is based on ideas related to the Mayer–Vietoris sequence and the Alexander duality – see e.g.
[
54
, Chapter 5]
and
[
68
, Corollary 1.29]
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
k<n
and let
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a continuous map defined on a
k
-dimensional oriented and closed manifold
\mathcal{M}
. If
\omega\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies
d\omega=0
in a neighborhood of the compact set
f(\mathcal{M})
, then there is
\delta>0
such that if

g_{0},g_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfy
\|f-g_{i}\|_{\infty}<\delta
,
i=0,1
, then

\int_{\mathcal{M}}g_{0}^{*}\omega=\int_{\mathcal{M}}g_{1}^{*}\omega.

Remark 8.9
.

Therefore, if
f_{\varepsilon}\rightrightarrows f
is a smooth approximation of
f
, then
there is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for all
0<\tau<\varepsilon_{0}

(8.2)

\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}\omega:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{M}}f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\omega=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f_{\tau}^{*}\omega.

The limit exists and it does not depend on the choice of the approximation, allowing one to define the integral of the pullback of a form
\omega
for any continuous map, provided
d\omega
vanishes in a neighborhood of
f(\mathcal{M})
.

Proof.

Let

U_{\delta}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\,\operatorname{dist}(y,f(\mathcal{M}))<\delta\}.

Assume
\delta>0
is so small that
d\omega=0
in
U_{\delta}
. Let

H:\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad H(x,t)=(1-t)g_{0}(x)+tg_{1}(x)

be a smooth homotopy between
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
. Since

|H(x,t)-f(x)|\leq(1-t)|g_{0}(x)-f(x)|+t|g_{1}(x)-f(x)|<\delta,

it follows that the image of the homotopy
H
is contained in the open set
U_{\delta}
, where
d\omega=0
and the result follows from the Stokes theorem

\int_{\mathcal{M}}g_{1}^{*}\omega-\int_{\mathcal{M}}g_{0}^{*}\omega=\int_{\partial(\mathcal{M}\times[0,1])}H^{*}\omega=\int_{\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]}H^{*}(d\omega)=0.

∎

Theorem 8.10
.

Let
k<n
and let
f:\mathbb{S}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a topological embedding (i.e.
f
is continuous and one-to-one). Then, there is
\omega\in\Omega^{k}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

d\omega\in\Omega_{c}^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{k}))
and

(8.3)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{k}}f^{*}\omega=1,

where the integral is defined as the limit (
8.2
).

Remark 8.11
.

While we will not need this remark in what follows, it is important to understand that
Theorem

8.10
has a natural interpretation in the language of de Rham cohomology. According to
[
68
, Corollary 1.29]
and the Poincaré duality
[
40
, Theorem 3.35]
, we have that

(8.4)

H_{c}^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{k}))=\mathbb{R}.

If
\tau\in\Omega_{c}^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{k}))
,
d\tau=0
, then
\tau=d\eta
for some
\eta\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and the mapping

H_{c}^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{k}))\ni[\tau]\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{S}^{k}}f^{*}\eta\in\mathbb{R}

is well defined and gives the isomorphism (
8.4
). Therefore, if
\omega
is as in
Theorem

8.10
, then the closed form
\tau:=d\omega\in\Omega_{c}^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{k}))
is a generator of
H_{c}^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{k}))
.

Proof.

We will prove the result using induction with respect to
k
.

For
k=0
, we have
\mathbb{S}^{0}=\{-1,+1\}
. Since
\Omega^{0}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
consists of compactly supported smooth functions
\omega\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it follows that for any approximation
f_{\varepsilon}
of
f
we have

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{0}}f^{*}\omega=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{0}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\omega=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\omega(f_{\varepsilon}(+1))-\omega(f_{\varepsilon}(-1))\right).

So it suffices to pick
\omega\equiv 1
around
f(1)
and
\omega\equiv 0
around
f(-1)
to conclude the claim.

We now suppose that the result is true for
k-1
and we will prove it for
k
.

Let
\mathbb{S}_{+}
,
\mathbb{S}_{-}
and
E=\mathbb{S}_{+}\cap\mathbb{S}_{-}
be the northern hemisphere, southern hemisphere and the equator of
\mathbb{S}^{k}
. Here we assume that the hemispheres are closed.
Let

\tilde{\mathbb{S}}_{+}=f(\mathbb{S}_{+}),\quad\tilde{\mathbb{S}}_{-}=f(\mathbb{S}_{-})\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{E}=f(E).

Since
E=\mathbb{S}^{k-1}
, by the induction hypothesis we can find

\eta\in\Omega^{k-1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
d\eta=0
is a neighborhood of
\tilde{E}
and

(8.5)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{k-1}}f^{*}\eta=\int_{E}f^{*}\eta=1.

Therefore, there is
\delta>0
such that
d\eta=0
on

\tilde{E}_{\delta}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\operatorname{dist}(y,\tilde{E})<\delta\}.

Let
\{\psi_{+},\psi_{-},\psi_{E}\}
be a smooth partition of unity subordinate to the open covering

\{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\tilde{\mathbb{S}}_{-},\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\tilde{\mathbb{S}}_{+},\tilde{E}_{\delta}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Note that
\psi_{E}d\eta=0
since
d\eta
vanishes on
\tilde{E}_{\delta}
. We have

d\eta=\psi_{+}d\eta+\psi_{-}d\eta+\psi_{E}d\eta=\psi_{+}d\eta+\psi_{-}d\eta:=\omega+\sigma

where

\omega=\psi_{+}d\eta\in\Omega^{k}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\sigma=\psi_{-}d\eta\in\Omega^{k}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

We claim that the form
\omega
proves the induction step. To this end, we need to show that
d\omega
vanishes in a neighborhood of
f(\mathbb{S}^{k})
and that (
8.3
) is satisfied.

First observe that
0=d^{2}\eta=d\omega+d\sigma
, so
d\omega=-d\sigma
.
Now,
\omega
, and hence
d\omega
vanish in a neighborhood of
\tilde{\mathbb{S}}_{-}
, because
\omega=\psi_{+}d\eta
, and
\psi_{+}
is supported in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\tilde{\mathbb{S}}_{-}
. For a similar reason
d\omega=-d\sigma
vanishes in a neighborhood of
\tilde{\mathbb{S}}_{+}
. Therefore we proved that
d\omega
vanishes in a neighborhood of

\tilde{\mathbb{S}}_{-}\cup\tilde{\mathbb{S}}_{+}=f(\mathbb{S}^{k})
.

Let now
f_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{k};\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\|f-f_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then, according to
Lemma

8.8
,
Remark

8.9
, and (
8.5
), there is
\varepsilon_{o}>0
such that for
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{o}

\int_{E}f^{*}\eta=\int_{E}f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\eta=1,\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{S}^{k}}f^{*}\omega=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{k}}f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\omega.

Note that
\omega=\psi_{+}d\eta
vanishes in a neighborhood of
\tilde{\mathbb{S}}_{-}=f(\mathbb{S}_{-})
and
\sigma=\psi_{-}d\eta
vanishes in a neighborhood of
\tilde{S}_{+}=f(\mathbb{S}_{+})
.
Therefore, if
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small, say
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}<\varepsilon_{o}
, then
\omega
and
\sigma
will vanish in neighborhoods of
f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{S}_{-})
and
f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{S}_{+})
respectively. In particular,

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{k}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\omega=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{+}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\omega\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{S}_{+}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\sigma=0.

Therefore for
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}
we have

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{k}}f^{*}\omega=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{k}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\omega=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{+}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\omega=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{+}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(d\eta-\sigma)=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{+}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(d\eta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\scriptsize{(Stokes)}}}}{{=}}\int_{E}f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\eta=1.

The proof is complete.
∎

8.2.
Direct proof of Theorem
8.1

Suppose to the contrary that there is a
\gamma
-Hölder continuous embedding
f:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
, where
n\geq m+1
and
\gamma>\frac{n+1}{n+2}
. In particular,
f
restricted to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is an embedding and we can assume that
m=n+1
.
Since
f:\mathbb{S}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
is an embedding, Theorem
8.10
yields a form
\omega\in\Omega^{n}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
such that

1=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f^{*}\omega=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}f^{*}(d\omega)=0,

where the last equality follows from Corollary
7.22
.
The contradiction completes the proof.

\Box

The proof of
Theorem

8.2
follows from the same idea, but since the assumptions about the Hölder continuity of
f
are of different type than the assumptions in
Theorem

7.18
and
Corollary

7.22
