, instead of applying these results directly, we have to adapt the proofs of these results.

8.3.
Proof of Theorem
8.2

In the proof, we denote by
[f]_{\gamma}
and
[f]_{\theta}
the Hölder seminorms with respect to the Korányi metric in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
and the Euclidean metric in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, respectively.

We can assume that
m=n+1
since
\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{m}
contains
\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{n+1}
. Suppose to the contrary that there is

f\in C^{0,\gamma+}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{n+1},\mathbb{H}^{n})\cap C^{0,\theta}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{n+1},\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})\ \ \text{or}\ \ f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{n+1},\mathbb{H}^{n})\cap C^{0,\theta+}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{n+1},\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}),

such that
f|_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}
is a topological embedding and
\gamma
and
\theta
satisfy assumptions of the theorem. We can further assume that
f
is defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, because extending
f
to
x\not\in\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{n+1}
by
f(x):=f(x/|x|)
preserves regularity of the mapping.

By Theorem
8.10
there is

\kappa\in\Omega_{c}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
such that

d\kappa\in\Omega^{n+1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{n}))
and

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f^{*}\kappa=1.

According to Corollary
2.15
we have a decomposition

d\kappa=\upalpha\wedge\beta+d\upalpha\wedge\delta,\quad\text{for some}\quad\beta\in\Omega^{n}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{n})\right),\ \delta\in\Omega^{n-1}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\setminus f(\mathbb{S}^{n})\right).

Let
f_{\varepsilon}
be the approximation of
f:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
by mollification.
Since
\delta=0
near
f(\mathbb{S}^{n})
, we have that
f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\delta=0
near
\partial\mathbb{B}^{n+1}
, provided
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small. Thus, the form
d(f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\upalpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\delta)
has compact support in
\mathbb{B}^{n+1}
, and Stokes’ theorem yields

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}df_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\delta=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\upalpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(d\delta).

Therefore, Theorem
7.2
and Lemma
7.13
yield

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f^{*}\kappa=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\kappa=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(d\kappa)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}\big(f^{*}_{\varepsilon}\alpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\beta+df_{\varepsilon}^{*}\alpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\delta\big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\alpha\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\beta+d\delta)\lesssim\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\alpha\|_{\infty}\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\beta+d\delta)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}[f]^{2}_{\gamma,2\varepsilon,B_{2}^{n+1}}\cdot\varepsilon^{-n(1-\theta)}[f]_{\theta,\varepsilon,B_{2}^{n+1}}^{n}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}[f]^{2}_{\gamma,2\varepsilon,B_{2}^{n+1}}[f]_{\theta,\varepsilon,B_{2}^{n+1}}^{n}=0,

because
f\in C^{0,\gamma+}
or
f\in C^{0,\theta+}
.

\Box

9.
Hodge decomposition

The theory of differential forms and the Hodge decomposition in Sobolev spaces was developed in
[
45
]
. We slightly adapt their results for our purposes. We also refer the interested reader to the monograph
[
17
]
.

Throughout this section we assume that
\mathcal{M}
is a compact and oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary.

The
Laplace-de Rham operator
is defined by

\Delta:\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})\to\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M}),\qquad\Delta\omega=(d\delta+\delta d)\omega.

We say that a form
\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
is
harmonic
if
\Delta\omega=0
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary. Then,
\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
,
0\leq\ell\leq k
, is harmonic if and only if
\omega
is closed
d\omega=0
and co-closed
\delta\omega=0
.

Proof.

The implication
\Leftarrow
is obvious. The other implication
\Rightarrow
easily follows from the integration by parts formula (
2.4
). Indeed,

0=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle\Delta\omega,\omega\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle d\omega,d\omega\rangle+\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle\delta\omega,\delta\omega\rangle=\|d\omega\|_{2}^{2}+\|\delta\omega\|_{2}^{2},

and hence
d\omega=0
and
\delta\omega=0
.
∎

For the proof of the next result, see
[
64
, Proposition 6.5]
.

Lemma 9.2

(
L^{p}
-Hodge decomposition)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary. Then, for any
p\in(1,\infty)
and any
\eta\in\Omega^{\ell}L^{p}(\mathcal{M})
,
0\leq\ell\leq k
, there exist

\omega_{1}\in\Omega^{\ell-1}W^{1,p}(\mathcal{M})\quad\text{and}\quad\omega_{2}\in\Omega^{\ell+1}(\mathcal{M}),

such that

\eta=d\omega_{1}+\delta\omega_{2}+h,

where
h\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
is a smooth harmonic form.

Remark 9.3
.

If
\ell=0
, then
\omega_{1}=0
, and similarly, if
\ell=k
, then
\omega_{2}=0
.

Remark 9.4
.

Later, we will sketch a proof of a special case of this result, see Corollary
9.13
.

Recall that de Rham cohomology is denoted by
H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
.
In particular,
H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
if for every closed smooth
\ell
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
,
d\omega=0
, there exists
\alpha\in\Omega^{\ell-1}(\mathcal{M})
such that
\omega=d\alpha
.
For example
H^{\ell}(\mathbb{S}^{n})=0
for all
\ell=1,\ldots,n-1
, and
H^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=0
for all
\ell\geq 1
.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary. Assume that
H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
for some
\ell=1,2,\ldots,k-1
. Then, for every
\omega\in\Omega^{k-\ell}(\mathcal{M})
satisfying
\delta\omega=0
, there is
\beta\in\Omega^{k-\ell+1}(\mathcal{M})
such that
\delta\beta=\omega
.

Proof.

Indeed,
\delta\omega=0
implies
d\ast\omega=0
, thus
\ast\omega=d\alpha
for some
\alpha\in\Omega^{\ell-1}(\mathcal{M})
, and then

\omega=(-1)^{\ell(k-\ell)}\ast\ast\omega=(-1)^{\ell(k-\ell)}\ast d\alpha=(-1)^{k(k-\ell+1)}\delta\ast\alpha.

Thus
\omega=\delta\beta
for
\beta=(-1)^{k(k-\ell+1)}\ast\alpha
.
∎

The next result is a straightforward consequence of the Poincaré duality
[
69
, Theorem 6.13]
.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary. Then for any
\ell=1,2,\ldots,k-1
we have that
\dim H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})=\dim H^{k-\ell}(\mathcal{M})
. In particular, if
H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
, then
H^{k-\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
.

For the proof of the next result, see
[
45
, Theorem 4.8]
.

Lemma 9.7

(Gaffney’s inequality)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary.
Then, for any
\ell=0,1,2,\ldots,k
we have

\|\omega\|_{1,2}\approx\|\omega\|_{2}+\|d\omega\|_{2}+\|\delta\omega\|_{2}\quad\text{for all }\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,2}(\mathcal{M}),

with constants dependent on
\mathcal{M}
only.

Here
\|\cdot\|_{1,2}
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
denote the Sobolev
W^{1,2}
and the
L^{2}
norms of the forms.

Remark 9.8
.

Usually Gaffney’s inequality is stated in the form of the inequality
\lesssim
, but since the opposite inequality is obvious, we have the norm equivalence
\approx
.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary.
Assume that
H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
for some
1\leq\ell\leq k-1
.
If
\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,2}(\mathcal{M})
satisfies
d\omega=0
and
\delta\omega=0
, then
\omega\equiv 0
.

Remark 9.10
.

In fact, a more general result of Hodge says that in every class of the cohomology group, there is a unique (up to a multiplicative constant) harmonic form, see
[
69
, Theorem 6.11]
.

Proof.

If
\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
satisfies
d\omega=0
and
\delta\omega=0
(i.e., if
\omega
is a smooth harmonic form), then
\omega=d\alpha=\delta\beta
for some
\alpha\in\Omega^{\ell-1}(\mathcal{M})
and
\beta\in\Omega^{\ell+1}(\mathcal{M})
. Indeed, existence of
\alpha
follows from
H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
. Since
H^{k-\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
by Lemma
9.6
, existence of
\beta
follows from Lemma
9.5
.
This is also true when
\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,2}(\mathcal{M})
satisfies
d\omega=0
and
\delta\omega=0
, but now the forms
\alpha
and
\beta
have Sobolev regularity, see
[
38
, Proposition 4.5.]
.
The integration by parts (
2.4
) yields

\|\omega\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle d\alpha,\delta\beta\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle d^{2}\alpha,\beta\rangle=0.

Consequently
\omega\equiv 0
.
∎

Lemma 9.11

(Gaffney-Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary.
If
H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
for some
1\leq\ell\leq k-1
, then
we have

\|\omega\|_{1,2}\approx\|d\omega\|_{2}+\|\delta\omega\|_{2}\quad\text{for all }\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,2}(\mathcal{M}),

with constants dependent on
\mathcal{M}
only.

Proof.

According to Lemma
9.7
, it suffices to prove the Poincaré type estimate

\|\omega\|_{2}\lesssim\|d\omega\|_{2}+\|\delta\omega\|_{2}.

The claim follows from a typical blowup-type argument used to prove the Poincaré inequality: Assume the claim is false, then there exists a sequence
\omega_{i}
with

\|\omega_{i}\|_{2}=1

and

(9.1)

\|d\omega_{i}\|_{2}+\|\delta\omega_{i}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{i}.

By Gaffney’s inequality, Lemma
9.7
,

\sup_{i}\|\omega_{i}\|_{1,2}<\infty.

From the Rellich-Kondrachov theorem (we can apply the Euclidean version on every coordinate patch of
\mathcal{M}
), we obtain a subsequence (still denoted by
\omega_{i}
) convergent in
L^{2}
to a limit
\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,2}(\mathcal{M})
,

(9.2)

\|\omega\|_{2}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\|\omega_{i}\|_{2}=1.

Moreover, using a partition of unity and arguing in coordinate-patches, we have weak convergence: for any
\eta\in\Omega^{k-\ell-1}(\mathcal{M})

\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\omega\wedge\eta=\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\omega_{i}\wedge\eta=0,

and for any
\tau\in\Omega^{k-\ell+1}(\mathcal{M})

\int_{\mathcal{M}}\delta\omega\wedge\tau=\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\delta\omega_{i}\wedge\tau=0.

The fact that the limits equal zero follows from (
9.1
).
Therefore,

d\omega=0
and
\delta\omega=0
. Hence, Lemma
9.9
yields
\omega\equiv 0
, which contradicts (
9.2
).
∎

Theorem 9.12
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary.
Assume also that
H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
for some
1\leq\ell\leq k-1
.
Then, for any
\ell
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
there exists a unique
\eta\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
such that

(9.3)

\Delta\eta=(d\delta+\delta d)\eta=\omega.

Moreover,

(9.4)

\|\eta\|_{1,2}\leq C(\mathcal{M})\|\omega\|_{2}.

Proof.

Consider the energy functional

\mathcal{E}(\eta):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle d\eta,d\eta\rangle+\langle\delta\eta,\delta\eta\rangle-2\langle\omega,\eta\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\|d\eta\|_{2}^{2}+\|\delta\eta\|_{2}^{2}\right)-\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle\omega,\eta\rangle.

We can minimize
\mathcal{E}
over
\eta\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,2}(\mathcal{M})
by the direct method of the calculus of variations. Indeed,
Lemma

9.11
, yields

\begin{split}\|\eta\|_{1,2}^{2}&\leq C(\mathcal{M})\frac{1}{2}\left(\|d\eta\|_{2}^{2}+\|\delta\eta\|_{2}^{2}\right)=C(\mathcal{M})\bigg(\mathcal{E}(\eta)+\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle\omega,\eta\rangle\bigg)\\
&\leq C(\mathcal{M})\left(\mathcal{E}(\eta)+\|\omega\|_{2}\,\|\eta\|_{2}\right).\end{split}

By Young’s inequality,

\|\eta\|_{1,2}^{2}\leq C(\mathcal{M})\mathcal{E}(\eta)+C^{\prime}(\mathcal{M})\|\omega\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{2}^{2}.

Absorbing
\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{2}^{2}
to the left-hand side we obtain

(9.5)

\|\eta\|_{1,2}^{2}\leq C(\mathcal{M})(\mathcal{E}(\eta)+\|\omega\|_{2}^{2}),

and hence
\mathcal{E}
is coercive.
Since
\mathcal{E}
is sequentially weakly lower semicontinuous on
W^{1,2}
, the direct method of calculus of variations gives a minimizer
\eta\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,2}(\mathcal{M})
of
\mathcal{E}
.

To derive the Euler-Lagrange equation for the minimizer
\eta
, observe that for any
\phi\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,2}(\mathcal{M})
, we have

\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\mathcal{E}(\eta+t\phi)=0
, and hence

(9.6)

\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle d\eta,d\phi\rangle+\langle\delta\eta,\delta\phi\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle\omega,\phi\rangle\quad\text{for all }\phi\in\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,2}(\mathcal{M}).

The integration by parts formula (
2.4
)
shows that (
9.6
) is the weak formulation of (
9.3
).
Observe now that
\Delta\eta=\omega
restricted to every coordinate patch is an elliptic equation
[
69
, Section 6.35]
, and by elliptic regularity theory (
[
69
, Theorem 6.30]
),
\eta\in C^{\infty}
.

For uniqueness, we need to show that if
\eta\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
is a harmonic form i.e.,
\Delta\eta=0
on
\mathcal{M}
, then
\eta\equiv 0
. This, however follows from Lemma
9.1
and Lemma
9.9
.

It remains to prove inequality (
9.4
). Since
\eta
is a minimizer over
\Omega^{\ell}W^{1,2}(\mathcal{M})
,
\mathcal{E}(\eta)\leq\mathcal{E}(0)=0
, and hence estimate (
9.5
) proves (
9.4
).
∎

As a consequence of Theorem
9.12
we obtain the following Hodge decomposition.

Corollary 9.13
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary.
Assume that
H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
for some
1\leq\ell\leq k-1
.
If
\omega\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
, then there are forms
\omega_{1}\in\Omega^{\ell-1}(\mathcal{M})
,
\omega_{2}\in\Omega^{\ell+1}(\mathcal{M})
such that

(9.7)

\omega=d\omega_{1}+\delta\omega_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta\omega_{1}=0,\ d\omega_{2}=0,

(9.8)

\|\omega_{1}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathcal{M}}\|d\omega_{1}\|_{2},\qquad\|\omega_{2}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathcal{M}}\|\delta\omega_{2}\|_{2},

(9.9)

\|\omega_{1}\|_{2}+\|\omega_{2}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathcal{M}}\|\omega\|_{2}.

Moreover, the forms
d\omega_{1}
and
\delta\omega_{2}
satisfying (
9.7
) are unique.

Remark 9.14
.

Uniqueness of the forms
d\omega_{1}
and
\delta\omega_{2}
means that if

\omega=d\tau_{1}+\delta\tau_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta\tau_{1}=0,\ d\tau_{2}=0,

then
d\tau_{1}=d\omega_{1}
and
\delta\tau_{2}=\delta\omega_{2}
. However, in general, the forms
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
need not be unique, because we can add to
\omega_{1}
and to
\omega_{2}
any harmonic form in
\Omega^{\ell-1}(\mathcal{M})
and
\Omega^{\ell+1}(\mathcal{M})
respectively.

Proof.

To prove uniqueness of a decomposition satisfying (
9.7
) we need to show that if

\omega=d\omega_{1}+\delta\omega_{2}=d\tau_{1}+\delta\tau_{2},\quad\delta\omega_{1}=\delta\tau_{1}=0,\quad d\omega_{2}=d\tau_{2}=0,

then
d\omega_{1}=d\tau_{1}
and
\delta\omega_{2}=\delta\tau_{2}
.
Since
d(\omega_{1}-\tau_{1})+\delta(\omega_{2}-\tau_{2})=\omega-\omega=0
, integration by parts (
2.4
) yields

0=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle d(\omega_{1}-\tau_{1}),d(\omega_{1}-\tau_{1})\rangle+\langle\delta(\omega_{2}-\tau_{2}),d(\omega_{1}-\tau_{1})\rangle=\|d(\omega_{1}-\tau_{1})\|_{2}^{2},

and hence
d\omega_{1}=d\tau_{1}
. Similar argument shows that
\|\delta(\omega_{2}-\tau_{2})\|_{2}^{2}=0
, so
\delta\omega_{2}=\delta\tau_{2}
.

Therefore, it remains to prove existence of the decomposition (
9.7
) satisfying (
9.8
) and (
9.9
).

Let
\eta\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
be a unique solution to
\Delta\eta=\omega
, see
Theorem

9.12
. Let
\lambda_{1}:=\delta\eta
and
\lambda_{2}:=d\eta
. Clearly,

(9.10)

d\lambda_{1}+\delta\lambda_{2}=\omega,\quad\delta\lambda_{1}=0,\quad d\lambda_{2}=0,\quad\|\lambda_{1}\|_{2}+\|\lambda_{2}\|_{2}\lesssim\|\eta\|_{1,2}\lesssim\|\omega\|_{2}.

That is, conditions (
9.7
) and (
9.9
) are satisfied, but (
9.8
) need not be true.

Let
\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}\in\Omega^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})
be unique solutions to

\Delta\eta^{\prime}=d\lambda_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta\eta^{\prime\prime}=\delta\lambda_{2}.

We claim that

(9.11)

d\delta\eta^{\prime}=d\lambda_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta d\eta^{\prime\prime}=\delta\lambda_{2}.

Indeed, since

d\delta\eta^{\prime}+\delta d\eta^{\prime}=\Delta\eta^{\prime}=d\lambda_{1},

we need to show that
\delta d\eta^{\prime}=0
and it easily follows from the integration by parts (
2.4
)

\|\delta d\eta^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle\delta d\eta^{\prime},\delta d\eta^{\prime}\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle\delta d\eta^{\prime},d\lambda_{1}-d\delta\eta^{\prime}\rangle=0,

so
\delta d\eta^{\prime}=0
. This proves the first equality in (
9.11
). The proof of the second equality is similar and left to the reader.
Let

\omega_{1}:=\delta\eta^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad\omega_{2}:=d\eta^{\prime\prime}.

Then (
9.11
) and (
9.10
) yield

d\omega_{1}+\delta\omega_{2}=d\lambda_{1}+\delta\lambda_{2}=\omega,\quad\delta\omega_{1}=0,\quad d\omega_{2}=0.

Moreover, (
9.4
) gives

\|\omega_{1}\|_{2}\lesssim\|\eta^{\prime}\|_{1,2}\lesssim\|d\lambda_{1}\|_{2}=\|d\omega_{1}\|_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\omega_{2}\|_{2}\lesssim\|\eta^{\prime\prime}\|_{1,2}\lesssim\|\delta\lambda_{2}\|_{2}=\|\delta\omega_{2}\|_{2}.

This proves (
9.8
). Finally,

\|\omega_{1}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\omega_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\|d\omega_{1}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\delta\omega_{2}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\omega\|_{2}^{2},

where the last equality follows from the fact that
\omega=d\omega_{1}+\delta\omega_{2}
is a decomposition of
\omega
into elements that are orthogonal in
\Omega^{\ell}L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
.
∎

As a consequence of
Corollary

9.13
we obtain the following Hodge decomposition result for pullbacks:

Theorem 9.15
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and oriented
k
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary and assume that

H^{\ell}(\mathcal{M})=0
for some
1\leq\ell\leq k-1
.

If
\kappa\in\Omega_{c}^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M};\mathbb{R}^{N})
, then there are forms

\omega_{f}\in\Omega^{\ell-1}(\mathcal{M})
and
\beta_{f}\in\Omega^{\ell+1}(\mathcal{M})
, such that

(9.12)

f^{\ast}(\kappa)=d\omega_{f}+\delta\beta_{f}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta\omega_{f}=0,\ d\beta_{f}=0,

and

(9.13)

\|\omega_{f}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathcal{M}}\|d\omega_{f}\|_{2},\qquad\|\beta_{f}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathcal{M}}\|\delta\beta_{f}\|_{2},

(9.14)

\|\omega_{f}\|_{2}+\|\beta_{f}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathcal{M}}\|f^{*}(\kappa)\|_{2}.

In the case when
N=2n+1
,
H^{n}(\mathcal{M})=0
, and
\kappa\in\Omega_{c}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
, we additionally have

(9.15)

\|\delta\beta_{f}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\kappa,\mathcal{M}}\|f^{\ast}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\ \left(1+\|Df\|_{\infty}^{n}\right),

where
\upalpha
is the standard contact form from Definition
2.12
.

Proof.

Let
\omega:=f^{*}(\kappa)
and let
\omega_{f}:=\omega_{1}
,
\beta_{f}:=\omega_{2}
be the forms from
Corollary

9.13
. Then (
9.12
), (
9.13
) and (
9.14
) follow immediately from
Corollary

9.13
.

It remains to prove (
9.15
). From the integration by parts (
2.4
) we have

\|\delta\beta_{f}\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle f^{*}(\kappa)-d\omega_{f},\delta\beta_{f}\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle f^{*}(d\kappa),\beta_{f}\rangle.

By assumption
\mathbb{R}^{N}=\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, and
d\kappa
is an
(n+1)
-form. From Corollary
2.15
we find

\tau\in\Omega_{c}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
and
\gamma\in\Omega_{c}^{n-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
such that

d\kappa=\tau\wedge\upalpha+d(\gamma\wedge\upalpha).

Note that

(9.16)

\|f^{*}(\tau)\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathcal{M}}\|f^{*}(\tau)\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{\kappa,\mathcal{M}}\|Df\|_{\infty}^{n},\quad\|f^{*}(\gamma)\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathcal{M}}\|f^{*}(\gamma)\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{\kappa,\mathcal{M}}\|Df\|_{\infty}^{n-1}.

The last constants depend on
\kappa
and
\mathcal{M}
only, because the forms
\tau
and
\gamma
are uniquely determined by
\kappa
and they are bounded.

Now, integration by parts and (
9.13
) yield

\begin{split}\|\delta\beta_{f}\|_{2}^{2}&=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\langle f^{*}(\tau)\wedge f^{*}(\upalpha),\beta_{f}\rangle+\langle f^{*}(\gamma)\wedge f^{*}(\upalpha),\delta\beta_{f}\rangle\\
&\leq\|f^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\big(\|f^{*}(\tau)\|_{2}\|\beta_{f}\|_{2}+\|f^{*}(\gamma)\|_{2}\|\delta\beta_{f}\|_{2}\big)\\
&\lesssim_{\mathcal{M}}\|f^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\big(\|f^{*}(\tau)\|_{2}+\|f^{*}(\gamma)\|_{2}\big)\|\delta\beta_{f}\|_{2}.\end{split}

This and (
9.16
) give

\|\delta\beta_{f}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\kappa,\mathcal{M}}\|f^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\big(\|Df\|_{\infty}^{n}+\|Df\|_{\infty}^{n-1}\big)\lesssim\|f^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\big(1+\|Df\|_{\infty}^{n}\big).

The proof is complete.
∎

10.
Nontriviality of Hölder homotopy groups

10.1.
Hölder homotopy groups

Hölder homotopy groups are defined as homotopy groups with the additional assumptions that all maps and homotopies involved are Hölder continuous.

More precisely, for a pointed metric space
(X,x_{0})
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
0<\gamma\leq 1
, we define the group
\pi_{k}^{\gamma}(X,x_{0})
as follows. Let
I^{k}=[0,1]^{k}
be the
k
-dimensional cube, then we set

\pi_{k}^{\gamma}(X,x_{0})=\left\{f\in C^{0,\gamma}(I^{k};X):\,\quad f\equiv x_{0}\quad\mbox{in a neighborhood of $\partial I_{k}$}\right\}/\sim

where
\sim
is the equivalence relation given by

f\sim g\quad\Longleftrightarrow\mbox{there is $H\in C^{0,\gamma}([0,1]\times I^{k};X)$ with}\begin{cases}H(0,\cdot)=f(\cdot)\\
H(1,\cdot)=g(\cdot)\\
H(t,\cdot)\equiv x_{0}\ \mbox{near $\partial I_{k}$.}\end{cases}

Although we will make no further mention of it, the group structure of
\pi_{k}^{\gamma}(X,x_{0})
is then defined as for the usual homotopy group
\pi_{k}(X,x_{0})
.

We say that a metric space
X
is
rectifiably connected
if any two points in
X
can be connected by a curve of finite length or equivalently, by a Lipschitz curve.
Equivalence follows from a well known fact that any curve of finite length admits an arc-length parametrization that is
1
-Lipschitz, see e.g.,
[
33
, Theorem 3.2]
.

If
X
is rectifiably connected, then for any
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, the groups
\pi_{k}^{\gamma}(X,x_{1})
and
\pi_{k}^{\gamma}(X,x_{2})
are isomorphic and we will simply wite
\pi_{k}^{\gamma}(X)
. Note that the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{n}
is rectifiably connected.

We will be mainly interested in determining whether
\pi_{k}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\neq 0
. To do so the following observation will be useful. The proof is standard and left to the reader.

Lemma 10.1
.

Assume that a metric space
X
is rectifiably connected and let
0<\gamma\leq 1
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\pi_{k}^{\gamma}(X)\neq 0
;

(2)

For any
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{k};X)
there is
H\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times[0,1];X)
and
x_{0}\in X
such that
H(\cdot,1)=f(\cdot)
and
H(\cdot,0)\equiv x_{0}
;

(3)

For any
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{k};X)
there is
F\in C^{0,\gamma}(\overline{\mathbb{B}}^{k+1};X)
such that
F(\theta)=f(\theta)
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{k}=\partial\overline{\mathbb{B}}^{k+1}
.

Let us start with a simple observation.

Proposition 10.2
.

If
k,n\geq 1
and
0<\gamma\leq\frac{1}{2}
, then
\pi_{k}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{H}^{n})=0
.

Proof.

Recall that
\delta_{t}:\mathbb{H}^{n}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
is the dilation defined in (
6.8
).

If
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{k};\mathbb{H}^{n})
, then

F\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times[0,1];\mathbb{H}^{n})
, where

F(\theta,t)=\delta_{t}f(\theta)\quad\text{for }(\theta,t)\in\mathbb{S}^{k}\times[0,1].

Indeed, (
6.5
) and (
6.9
) yield

\begin{split}&d_{K}(F(\theta,t),F(\xi,s))\leq d_{K}(\delta_{t}f(\theta),\delta_{t}f(\xi))+d_{K}(\delta_{t}f(\xi),\delta_{s}f(\xi))\\
&\lesssim td_{K}(f(\theta),f(\xi))+|t-s|\,\|f\|_{\infty}+|t^{2}-s^{2}|^{1/2}\|f\|_{\infty}^{1/2}\lesssim(|\theta-\xi|+|t-s|)^{\gamma}.\end{split}

Here
\|f\|_{\infty}
denotes the supremum norm of
f
with respect to the Euclidean norm in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
.
Since
F(\cdot,1)=f(\cdot)
and
F(\cdot,0)\equiv 0
, the result follows from Lemma
10.1
.
∎

When
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
the situation is more complicated. If
\gamma=1
, we have Lipschitz homotopy groups which we shall denote by
\pi_{k}^{\operatorname{Lip}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
. The following result is known.

Theorem 10.3
.

(1)

\pi^{\operatorname{Lip}}_{m}(\mathbb{H}^{n})=0
for all
1\leq m<n
;

(2)

\pi_{m}^{\operatorname{Lip}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})=0
for all
m\geq 2
;

(3)

\pi^{\operatorname{Lip}}_{n}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\neq 0
;

(4)

\pi^{\operatorname{Lip}}_{4n-1}(\mathbb{H}^{2n})\neq 0
;

(5)

\pi^{\operatorname{Lip}}_{n+1}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\neq 0
.

(6)

\pi^{\operatorname{Lip}}_{n+k(n-1)}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\neq 0
for
k=0,1,2,\ldots

(1) was proven in
[
70
, Theorem 1.1]
, see also
[
19
, Theorem 4.11]
. (2) is due to
[
71
, Theorem 5]
. (3) is due to
[
1
]
. For other proofs, see
[
19
, Theorem 4.11]
and
[
38
, Theorem 1.7]
. (4) was the main result of
[
38
]
. (5) is due to
[
34
]
. (6) is proven in the forthcoming paper
[
24
]
. Currently the authors of
[
24
]
are working on extending the result to the Hölder category. Manin
[
55
]
proved a similar result to (6) in the Lipschitz category, but with a different technique.

In the remaining part of
Section

10
, we will extend (3) and (4) to the Hölder case, see Theorems
10.5
and
10.7
respectively.

The proofs of (3) and (4) in the Lipschitz case were based on the fact that if
f\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{S}^{k};\mathbb{H}^{n})
then
\operatorname{rank}Df\leq n
almost everywhere and this was combined with the fact that intgrals of pullbacks of differential forms (degree or Hopf invariant) could detect non-trivial elements of the homotopy groups of spheres.
In
Theorem

7.18
we have found an alternative formulation for the notion of
\operatorname{rank}Df\leq n
, for Hölder continuous maps, and we combine this notion with the previous arguments to obtain our results.

To link the homotopy groups of spheres with the Hölder homotopy groups of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
we will need the followings result whose proof can be found in
[
1
, Section 4]
,
[
19
, Theorem 3.2]
,
[
22
, Example 3.1]
.

Lemma 10.4
.

For each
n\geq 1
, there is a smooth embedding
\phi:\mathbb{S}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, such that
\phi^{*}(\upalpha)=0
. As a consequence
\phi
is a bi-Lipschitz embedding of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

The next result is a straightforward consequence of Lemma
10.4
and Corollary
8.4
.

Theorem 10.5
.

For any
n\geq 1
we have
\pi^{\gamma}_{n}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\neq 0
for all
\gamma\in\big(\frac{n+1}{n+2},1\big]
.

Proof.

If
\phi:\mathbb{S}^{n}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
is a bi-Lipschitz embedding from Lemma
10.4
, then according to Corollary
8.4
,

f
cannot be extended to a map
\tilde{\phi}\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mkern 1.7mu\overline{\mkern-1.7mu\mathbb{B}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{n+1};\mathbb{H}^{n})
whenever

\gamma\in\big(\frac{n+1}{n+2},1\big]
. Hence Lemma
10.1
yields that
0\neq[\phi]\in\pi_{n}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
.
∎

Remark 10.6
.

A straightforward proof of Theorem
10.5
, that does not refer to Theorem
8.2
, follows also from the argument used in Section
8.2
.

The main result of
Section

10
is, however,
Theorem

10.7
. It is much more difficult than
Theorem

10.5
. The remaining part of the paper is solely devoted to its proof.

Theorem 10.7
.

For any
n\geq 1
,
we have
\pi^{\gamma}_{4n-1}(\mathbb{H}^{2n})\neq 0
for all
\gamma\in\big(\frac{4n+1}{4n+2},1\big]
.

In order to prove
Theorem

10.7
we need to extend the strategy from
[
38
]
and use the Hopf invariant combined with the new notion of the “rank of the derivative” for Hölder maps,
Theorem

7.18
. Classically, the Hopf invariant
\mathcal{H}(f)
is a homotopy invariant defined on maps
f:\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{2n}
, but we need to extend it to maps
f:\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}
.

10.2.
Generalized Hopf invariant

Let us start with recalling the definition of the classical Hopf invariant (cf.
[
4
]
).

Let
\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}}\in\Omega^{2n}(\mathbb{S}^{2n})
be the volume form on
\mathbb{S}^{2n}
. For any smooth map
f:\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{2n}
, we have
df^{*}(\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}})=f^{*}(d\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}})=0
. Since
H^{2n}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1})=0
, it follows that
f^{*}(\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}})=d\omega
for some
\omega\in\Omega^{2n-1}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1})
. Then, the
Hopf invariant
is defined by

\mathcal{H}(f):=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega\wedge d\omega=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega\wedge f^{*}(\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}}).

If
f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{S}^{2n})
are homotopic, then
\mathcal{H}(f)=\mathcal{H}(g)
.

Hopf
[
42
, Satz II, Satz II’]
proved the following important result which along with the homotopy invariance of
\mathcal{H}(\cdot)
implies that
\pi_{4n-1}(\mathbb{S}^{2n})\neq 0
.

Lemma 10.8
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a smooth map

g:\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{2n}
such that
\mathcal{H}(g)\neq 0
.

Our aim is to generalize the Hopf invariant to the case of mappings
f:\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}
. The construction presented below is a modification of that given in
[
38
]
.

Fix any form
\kappa\in\Omega_{c}^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
. Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
. Then
f^{*}(\kappa)\in\Omega^{2n}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1})
. Since
H^{2n}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1})=0
, Theorem
9.15
gives the Hodge decomposition

(10.1)

f^{*}(\kappa)=d\omega_{f}+\delta\beta_{f},\quad\omega_{f}\in\Omega^{2n-1}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}),\ \beta_{f}\in\Omega^{2n+1}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}),\quad\delta\omega_{f}=0,\ d\beta_{f}=0.

Then, we define the
generalized Hopf invariant
by

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f):=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f}\wedge d\omega_{f}.

Note that

(10.2)

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f}\wedge f^{*}(\kappa).

Indeed,
d\omega_{f}=f^{*}(\kappa)-\delta\beta_{f}
and the equality follows from integration by parts, because
\delta\omega_{f}=0
.

Observe also that
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)
does not depend on a particular choice of the Hodge decomposition in (
10.1
). That is, if

f^{*}(\kappa)=d\tau+\delta\gamma,\quad\delta\tau=0,\quad d\gamma=0,

then

(10.3)

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\tau\wedge d\tau=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\tau\wedge f^{*}(\kappa).

Indeed, the second equality follows from the same argument as in the proof of (
10.2
). As for the first equality, in
Corollary

9.13
we proved that
d\tau=d\omega_{f}
and hence integration by parts gives

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\tau\wedge d\tau=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f}\wedge d\omega_{f}-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\tau\wedge d\tau=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}(\omega_{f}-\tau)\wedge d\omega_{f}=0,

because
d(\omega_{f}-\tau)=0
.
However, in what follows, we will use the Hodge decomposition (
10.1
) from
Theorem

9.15
, because we will need to refer to estimates proved in
Theorem

9.15
.

It is important to be aware that
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(\cdot)
depends on the choice of the form
\kappa
.

We want to emphasize that
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)
is
not
an invariant of homotopies of
f:\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}
, unless
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(\cdot)\equiv 0
.
It was proved in
[
38
]
that
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(\cdot)
restricted to the class of Lipschitz maps

f\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
satisfying

\operatorname{rank}Df\leq 2n\quad\text{almost everywhere in $\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}$,}

is invariant under Lipschitz homotopies satisfying the same rank condition,

\operatorname{rank}DH\leq 2n
almost everywhere in
\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\times[0,1]
. Since the rank of the derivative of any Lipschitz mapping into
\mathbb{H}^{2n}
is less than or equal
2n
,
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(\cdot)
is an invariant of Lipschitz homotopies of Lipschitz maps
f\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
.
On the other hand, Hölder continuous mappings into
\mathbb{H}^{2n}
(and hence into
\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
need not be differentiable at any point, but
Theorem

7.18
(see also
Remark

7.19
) is a substitute for the condition about the rank of the derivative. Using this idea, we will prove in
Theorem

10.11
that
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(\cdot)

is an invariant of Hölder homotopies of Hölder maps into the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{2n}
.
For a precise statement, see
Theorem

10.11
and
Remark

10.10
.
This will be a far reaching and non-trivial generalization of the results from
[
38
]
.

While it can happen that
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(\cdot)\equiv 0
(for example if
\kappa=0
), in order to prove that
\pi_{4n-1}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{H}^{2n})\neq 0
(see
Theorem

10.7
), we will need to select a suitable form
\kappa
such that
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)\neq 0
for some smooth map
f:\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\to\mathbb{H}^{2n}=\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}
. This and
Theorem

10.11
will imply that
0\neq[f]\in\pi^{\gamma}_{4n-1}(\mathbb{H}^{2n})
. The form
\kappa
will be a compactly supported extension of the volume form of the embedded sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2n}
into
\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}
discussed in
Lemma

10.4
. For details, see
Section

10.3
.

Remark 10.9
.

Note that if
f
is smooth and
\operatorname{rank}Df\leq 2n
, then
d(f^{*}(\kappa))=f^{*}(d\kappa)=0
, because
d\kappa
is a
(2n+1)
-form. Hence
f^{*}(\kappa)=d\omega_{f}
i.e.,
\delta\beta_{f}=0
. For any smooth map
f
, the term
\delta\beta_{f}
measures how far
f^{*}(\kappa)
is from being exact. If
f:\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\to\mathbb{H}^{2n}
is in a suitable Hölder class, then “
\operatorname{rank}Df\leq 2n
” in the distributional sense of
Theorem

7.18
. Therefore, one should expect that if
f_{\varepsilon}
is an approximation of
f
by mollification, then suitable integrals involving
\delta\beta_{f_{\varepsilon}}
would converge to zero as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and the key estimate to achieve that is (
9.15
) combined with
Theorem

7.2
. This idea is used in the proof of the next result, see for example (
10.13
). It allows us to extend the proof of
[
38
, Proposition 5.8]
to the Hölder case. We hope that this explanation will help understand the proof.

Remark 10.10
.

Theorem

10.11
allows us to define
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)
when

\gamma\in\big(\frac{4n+1}{4n+2},1\big]
and

f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
is
C^{0,\gamma}
-homotopic to a map in
C^{\infty}\cap C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
.

If
\gamma\in\big(\frac{4n-1}{4n},1\big]
, then one can prove that

(10.4)

|\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)-\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(g)|\lesssim\big(1+\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{4n+1}+\|g\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}^{4n+1}\big)\|f-g\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}\text{ for }f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}).

This estimate allows one to define
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f_{\varepsilon})
for all
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
, and hence for all
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{4n+1})
. However, regarding the
C^{0,\gamma}
-homotopy invariance of maps in
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{4n+1})
, we could prove it only for
\gamma\in\big(\frac{4n+1}{4n+2},1\big]
, which is the same range as in
Theorem

10.11
.

One can prove (
10.4
) using hard harmonic analysis related to the paraproduct estimates in
[
66
,
49
]
. Alternatively, one can prove (
10.4
) using methods from
[
63
]
.

However, the proof of
Theorem

10.11
presented below is substantially easier, and arguably more elegant; although we do not prove (
10.4
),
Theorem

10.11
suffices to prove
Theorem

10.7
.

Theorem 10.11
.

Let
\gamma\in\big(\frac{4n+1}{4n+2},1\big]
and let
\kappa\in\Omega_{c}^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
. The generalized Hopf invariant
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(\cdot)

is a
C^{0,\gamma}
-homotopy invariant of mappings in the class
C^{\infty}\cap C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
.

More precisely, assume that
f,g\in C^{\infty}\cap C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
are
C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
-homotopic. That is, assume there exists
H\in C^{0,\gamma}([0,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
such that
H(0,\cdot)=f(\cdot)
and
H(1,\cdot)=g(\cdot)
. Then

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)=\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(g).

Remark 10.12
.

While
f,g\in C^{\infty}
, we require only Hölder continuity for
H
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}\to[0,1]
be a Lipschitz map such that
\varphi(t)=0
for all
t\approx 0
and
\varphi(t)=1
for all
t\approx 1
. Considering
H(\varphi(t),x)
instead of
H(t,x)
we can assume without lost of generality that for
\delta=\frac{1}{10}
we have
H\in C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
with

(10.5)

H(t,x)=f(x)\quad\mbox{for $|t|<2\delta$}\quad\text{and}\quad H(t,x)=g(x)\quad\text{for $|t-1|<2\delta$}

Denote by
f_{\varepsilon}
,
g_{\varepsilon}
, and
H_{\varepsilon}
, the mollifications of
f
,
g
, and
H
, as maps on
\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}
and
\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}
respectively. Such mollifications were used in
Section

7.4
. In particular we have

(10.6)

\|Df_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}+\|Dg_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}+\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon^{\gamma-1}C(f,g,H).

The proof follows from (
7.18
) combined with the arguments used in the proof of
Corollary

7.15
.

In view of (
10.5
), for small enough
\varepsilon>0
, we still have

(10.7)

H_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=f_{\varepsilon}(x)\quad\mbox{for $|t|<\delta$}\quad\text{and}\quad H_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=g_{\varepsilon}(x)\quad\text{for $|t-1|<\delta$}.

Since
\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, Künneth formula,
[
4
, p. 47]
, yields

H^{2n}(\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1})=0\quad\text{for all }n\geq 1.

Thus, we may apply Hodge decomposition,
Theorem

9.15
,

f_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)=d\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}+\delta\beta_{f_{\varepsilon}}\quad\mbox{in $\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}$},

g_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)=d\omega_{g_{\varepsilon}}+\delta\beta_{g_{\varepsilon}}\quad\mbox{in $\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}$}.

H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)=d\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}+\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\quad\mbox{in $\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}$}.

Note that since
f\in C^{\infty}
, we have

(10.8)

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa).

Indeed, according to
Corollary

9.13
, we have a Hodge decomposition

f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)-f^{*}(\kappa)=d\tau_{\varepsilon}+\delta\gamma_{\varepsilon},\quad\delta\tau_{\varepsilon}=0,\quad d\gamma_{\varepsilon}=0,

satisfying

(10.9)

\|\tau_{\varepsilon}\|_{2}\lesssim\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)-f^{*}(\kappa)\|_{2}.

Hence,

f^{*}_{\varepsilon}(\kappa)=d(\tau_{\varepsilon}+\omega_{f})+\delta(\gamma_{\varepsilon}+\beta_{f}).

Although, there is no reason to claim that
\tau_{\varepsilon}+\omega_{f}=\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}
or
\gamma_{\varepsilon}+\beta_{f}=\beta_{f_{\varepsilon}}
, (
10.3
) yields

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f_{\varepsilon})=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}(\tau_{\varepsilon}+\omega_{f})\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa).

Since

f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)\rightrightarrows f^{*}(\kappa)
uniformly, (
10.9
) yields that
\tau_{\varepsilon}+\omega_{f}\to\omega_{f}
in
L^{2}
, and hence
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f_{\varepsilon})\to\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)
.

Let

\imath_{t}:\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\to\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}.

Then
H_{\varepsilon}\circ\imath_{t}=f_{\varepsilon}
for
|t|<\delta
and
H_{\varepsilon}\circ\imath_{t}=g_{\varepsilon}
for
|t-1|<\delta
.
In particular

f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)=\imath_{t}^{*}H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)\text{ if }|t|<\delta\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)=\imath_{t}^{*}H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)\text{ if }|t-1|<\delta.

We claim that

(10.10)

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}\Bigg(\,\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath^{\ast}_{t}\left(\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\right)\Bigg)\,dt,

and similarly

(10.11)

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(g)&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[1-\delta,1+\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[1-\delta,1+\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[1-\delta,1+\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[1-\delta,1+\delta]}}\bigg(\,\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath^{\ast}_{t}\left(\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\right)\bigg)\,dt\\
&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}\bigg(\,\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath^{\ast}_{t+1}\left(\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\right)\bigg)\,dt.\end{split}

Recall that the barred integral stands for the integral average, see (
2.1
).
We will prove (
10.10
); the proof of (
10.11
) is the same.
Note that (
10.8
) trivially gives

(10.12)

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}\Bigg(\,\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\Bigg)\,dt,

because the integral over
\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}
does not depend on
t
.

In the calculations presented below one has to be careful: While
d\imath_{t}^{*}=\imath^{*}_{t}d
, the co-differential does not commute with pullback,
\delta\imath_{t}^{*}\neq\imath_{t}^{*}\delta
.

For every
t\in(-\delta,\delta)
we have

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\imath_{t}^{\ast}H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\imath_{t}^{\ast}d\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\imath_{t}^{\ast}\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\\
&\overset{\text{Stokes}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath_{t}^{\ast}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge d\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\imath_{t}^{\ast}\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath_{t}^{\ast}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge f_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath_{t}^{\ast}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\delta\beta_{f_{\varepsilon}}+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\imath_{t}^{\ast}\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath_{t}^{\ast}\left(\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\right)-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath_{t}^{\ast}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\delta\beta_{f_{\varepsilon}}+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\imath_{t}^{\ast}\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}.\end{split}

Therefore, in order to conclude (
10.10
) from (
10.12
), it suffices to show that

(10.13)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath_{t}^{\ast}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\delta\beta_{f_{\varepsilon}}\Bigg|\,dt=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\imath_{t}^{\ast}\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\Bigg|\,dt=0.

We have

\begin{split}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath_{t}^{\ast}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\delta\beta_{f_{\varepsilon}}\Bigg|\,dt&\leq\int_{[-\delta,\delta]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}|\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}|\,|\delta\beta_{f_{\varepsilon}}|\leq(2\delta)^{1/2}\|\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\|_{2}\|\delta\beta_{f_{\varepsilon}}\|_{2}\\
&\lesssim\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)\|_{2}(1+\|Df_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n})\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}=:\heartsuit.\end{split}

In the last inequality we used (
9.14
) and (
9.15
) respectively. The factor
(2\delta)^{1/2}
appears, because
\delta\beta_{f_{\varepsilon}}
is defined on
\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}
and does not depend on the variable
t\in[-\delta,\delta]
.

Clearly,

\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)\|_{2}\lesssim\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n}
, so (
10.6
) and
Theorem

7.2
give

\heartsuit\lesssim\varepsilon^{2n(\gamma-1)}\cdot\varepsilon^{2n(\gamma-1)}\cdot\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0,

because
4n(\gamma-1)+2\gamma-1>0
for
\gamma>\frac{4n+1}{4n+2}
.
The proof that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{f_{\varepsilon}}\wedge\imath_{t}^{\ast}\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\Bigg|\,dt=0

is similar and left to the reader. This completes the proof of (
10.13
) and hence that of (
10.10
). The proof of (
10.11
) is the same.

Since,

(10.14)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)-\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(g)\\
&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}\Bigg(\,\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath^{\ast}_{t}\left(\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\right)-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath^{\ast}_{t+1}\left(\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\right)\Bigg)\,,\end{split}

in order to prove that
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)=\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(g)
, it suffices to show that the above limit equals zero.
We have

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath^{\ast}_{t+1}\left(\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\right)-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\imath^{\ast}_{t}\left(\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\right)\\
\overset{\text{Stokes}}{=}&\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}d\left(\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\right)\\
=&\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}d\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)-\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(d\kappa)\\
=&\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa\wedge\kappa)-\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)-\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(d\kappa).\end{split}

Therefore, it suffices to show that each of the following integrals converges to zero:

(10.15)

\begin{split}&\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}\bigg(\,\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa\wedge\kappa)\Bigg)\,dt=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}\Bigg(\,\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\Bigg)\,dt\\
&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}\Bigg(\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(d\kappa)\Bigg)\,dt=0.\end{split}

As for the first integral, from Corollary
2.15
we find
\eta_{1}\in\Omega_{c}^{4n-1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
and
\eta_{2}\in\Omega_{c}^{4n-2}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
such that

\kappa\wedge\kappa=\eta_{1}\wedge\upalpha+d(\eta_{2}\wedge\upalpha).

The Stokes theorem yields

\begin{split}\Bigg|\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa\wedge\kappa)\Bigg|&\leq\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\eta_{1})|\,|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)|+\int_{\partial([t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1})}|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\eta_{2})|\,|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)|\\
&\lesssim\big(\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{4n-1}+\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{4n-2}\big)\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\\
&\lesssim\big(\varepsilon^{(4n-1)(\gamma-1)}+\varepsilon^{(4n-2)(\gamma-1)}\big)\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.\end{split}

Since the estimates
do not
depend on
t
, the first integral in (
10.15
) converges to zero.

Regarding the second integral in (
10.15
) we have

\begin{split}&\Bigg|\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(\kappa)\Bigg|\leq\|\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\|_{2}\ \|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)\|_{2}\\
&\lesssim\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}(1+\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n})\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}\cdot\varepsilon^{4n(\gamma-1)}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.\end{split}

Again, the estimates do not depend on
t
and hence the second integral in (
10.15
) converges to zero.

It remains to show that the third integral in (
10.15
) converges to zero. This time the estimates will depend on
t
.

From
Corollary

2.15
we find
\eta_{3}\in\Omega^{2n}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
and
\eta_{4}\in\Omega^{2n-1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
such that

d\kappa=\eta_{3}\wedge\upalpha+d(\eta_{4}\wedge\upalpha).

Stokes’ theorem yields

\begin{split}&\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}(d\kappa)=\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\eta_{3})\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\\
&+\int_{[t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}d\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\eta_{4})\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)-\int_{\partial([t,t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1})}\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\eta_{4})\wedge H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\\
&=I_{1,\varepsilon}(t)+I_{2,\varepsilon}(t)-I_{3,\varepsilon}(t),\end{split}

and it remains to show that

(10.16)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}I_{1,\varepsilon}(t)\,dt=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}I_{2,\varepsilon}(t)\,dt=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.5pt\kern-9.0pt\intop}\limits_{\kern-3.0pt[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}{\mathop{\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.6pt\kern-6.0pt\intop}\nolimits_{[-\delta,\delta]}}I_{3,\varepsilon}(t)\,dt=0.

Since (
9.14
) gives

(10.17)

\|\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\|_{1}\lesssim\|\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\|_{2}\lesssim\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\kappa)\|_{2}\lesssim\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n},

we have

|I_{1,\varepsilon}(t)|\lesssim\|\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\|_{1}\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\eta_{3})\|_{\infty}\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{4n}\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\varepsilon^{4n(\gamma-1)}\cdot\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}\to 0.

The estimates do not depend on
t
so the first integral in (
10.16
) converges to zero.

Note that

|I_{2,\varepsilon}(t)|\leq\|d\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\|_{1}\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\eta_{4})\|_{\infty}\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|d\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\|_{2}\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n-1}\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}.

Since

\|d\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\|_{2}=\|H^{*}_{\varepsilon}(\kappa)-\delta\beta_{H_{\varepsilon}}\|_{2}\lesssim\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n}+\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\big(1+\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n}\big),

we estimate

\begin{split}|I_{2,\varepsilon}(t)|&\lesssim\big(\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n}+\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}(1+\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n})\big)\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n-1}\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\\
&\lesssim\big(\varepsilon^{2n(\gamma-1)}+\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}\cdot\varepsilon^{2n(\gamma-1)}\big)\cdot\varepsilon^{(2n-1)(\gamma-1)}\cdot\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}\to 0.\end{split}

Again, the estimates do not depend on
t
so the second integral in (
10.16
) converges to zero.

While we could estimate
I_{1,\varepsilon}(t)
and
I_{2,\varepsilon}(t)
for each
t\in[-\delta,\delta]
, with estimates in terms of
\varepsilon
that are independent of
t
, this is not possible for
I_{3,\varepsilon}(t)
, because we do not have good estimates for
\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}
on individual slices
\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}
and
\{t+1\}\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}
. However, we can easily estimate the integral
\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}I_{3,\varepsilon}(t)\,dt
, where instead of estimating
\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}
on slices we can use the global estimate (
10.17
) on
\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}
. We have

\begin{split}\Bigg|\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}I_{3,\varepsilon}(t)\,dt\bigg|&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}}|\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}|\,|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\eta_{4})|\,|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)|\leq\|\omega_{H_{\varepsilon}}\|_{1}\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\eta_{4})\|_{\infty}\|H_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\\
&\lesssim\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n}\|DH_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}^{2n-1}\|H_{\varepsilon}(\upalpha)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2n(\gamma-1)}\cdot\varepsilon^{(2n-1)(\gamma-1)}\cdot\varepsilon^{2\gamma-1}\to 0.\end{split}

This proves convergence to zero of the last integral in (
10.16
) and completes the proof of

Theorem

10.11
.
∎

10.3.
Proof of
Theorem

10.7

We start with constructing
\kappa\in\Omega_{c}^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
such that
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)\neq 0
for some smooth map
f:\mathbb{S}^{4n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}
that is horizontal in the sense that
f^{*}(\upalpha)=0
; such a mapping necessarily belongs to
f\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{S}^{2n}\to\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}
be the smooth embedding from
Lemma

10.4
;
\phi
is a bi-Lipschitz embedding of
\mathbb{S}^{2n}
to
\mathbb{H}^{2n}
. Let
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1};\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})
be a smooth and compactly supported extension of
\phi^{-1}:\phi(\mathbb{S}^{2n})\to\mathbb{S}^{2n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
.

Recall that

\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}}=\sum_{j=1}^{2n+1}(-1)^{j-1}x_{j}\,dx_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{dx_{j}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{2n+1}\in\Omega^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1})

restricted to
\mathbb{S}^{2n}
is the volume form on
\mathbb{S}^{2n}
.
Now we define

(10.18)

\kappa:=\psi^{*}(\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}})\in\Omega_{c}^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}).

Lemma 10.13
.

If
\kappa\in\Omega_{c}^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
is defined by (
10.18
), then there is a smooth map
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
, such that
f\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
and

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)\neq 0
.

Proof.

Let
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{S}^{2n})
,
\mathcal{H}(g)\neq 0
, be as in
Lemma

10.8
, and let
\phi:\mathbb{S}^{2n}\to\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}
be the embedding from
Lemma

10.4
. Clearly,

f:=\phi\circ g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
satisfies
f\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{S}^{4n-1};\mathbb{H}^{2n})
. We claim that

\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)\neq 0
. Indeed, since
\psi\circ\phi=\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}}
, we have

f^{*}(\kappa)=g^{*}\phi^{*}(\kappa)=g^{*}\phi^{*}\psi^{*}(\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}})=g^{*}(\psi\circ\phi)^{*}(\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}})=g^{*}(\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2n}}),

and it easily follows that
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}(f)=\mathcal{H}(g)\neq 0
.
∎

If
f
is as in
Lemma

10.13
, then
Theorem

10.11
implies that
0\neq[f]\in\pi^{\gamma}_{4n-1}(\mathbb{H}^{2n})
. This completes the proof of
Theorem

10.7
.

\Box
